# The Woman They Unwrote

*The Register of Uses · Book Three*

**Review copy** · chapters 1–30 · **93,895 words** · 30 chapters · average 3,130

*Forty years of bread on a sill, set down in turn by seven households, for a woman the register does not have. The order that removed her says only this: It is entirely lawful.*

## One · The Book From Tindle

The window stuck at the top, as it had stuck every spring since I was a girl, and I put my shoulder to the frame and got it open a hand's width before the paint let go and it came down again. I left it. The room was warm enough, and the window had never been the point. I had only wanted to know whether the latch would hold if I pushed, the way I had wanted to know, at eighteen, whether my mother's door would open if I knocked. It had not. This one would not either. I turned from it and went back to the table, and the small ache in my shoulder was the only answer the window gave, which was the same answer the other door had given, and the sameness of the two answers was the kind of thing I had learned not to name out loud.

Edward had left the house before the light was fair. He did not say where he went on the days he did not say. I had stopped asking on the second month of our marriage. Not because I did not want to know. Because the asking made him close the notebook he carried and set it aside, and I had learned that some questions cost the person asked more than the answer was worth. He left the smell of cold earth on the step and the door standing open a moment after he was gone, the way he always did, as if he expected to be called back. No one called him. I closed the door and set the latch, and the latch held, which the window had not, and I noted the difference without assigning it a meaning.

The carrier's boy came to the back door at half past nine, with a parcel wrapped in oilcloth and tied with the red cord the Tindle parish office used, and a folded note from their new clerk, who had found an old rate-book that did not sit right and had sent it to the audit office in Collow, because the audit office was where Edward worked, and because, the clerk wrote, everyone in the parish said the woman in that house read things other people could not. The boy held the parcel away from his coat, as if the oilcloth might stain, and his eyes went past me into the kitchen where the loaf from the bakehouse sat on the board, and he was hungry and young enough to think the hunger showed only in his stomach and not in his face.

I took the parcel. The boy waited, hoping for a coin, and I gave him one, and he ran, and the red cord whipped once around his wrist before he caught it, and he was gone down the lane with the coin already in his mouth the way boys his age carry money they are afraid of losing.

The oilcloth smelled of the river. Under it the book was a quarto, the boards warped, the corners chewed by mice that had lived in it longer than the parish had kept it. The title page was gone. What remained was a column of names in a hand I did not know, entered year by year, the ink fading from black to brown to the pale grey that meant the pen had run dry and no one had cared to refill it. The mice had left their smell in the boards, a sour sweet rot. The paper had gone soft at the edges where they had nested. I held it by the spine and let it fall open in my hands, the way you let an old thing fall open, to see where it wanted to be read.

I set it on the table where the light came in sideways. Edward would want to see it when he came home. But the book had come to the house, and the house was mine to keep in order, and there is an order to a thing before you give it to a man who will read it for fault.

I opened it at the first leaf.

The rate-payers of Tindle, listed by dwelling, by hearth, by the number of their windows and the size of their garden. The usual lies. A man who owned two cows wrote one. A woman who owned nothing wrote her name with a steady hand that said she had been made to write it many times. I turned the leaves slowly. The book was honest in the way parish books are honest, which is to say it told the truth about who held the pen. The paper was thin. The ink had bled through in places, so that a name on the recto showed its ghost on the verso. The ghosts were as true as the names. A person who writes with a heavy hand is a person who carries weight, and the weight shows on both sides of the page.

At the back, someone had begun a second list. Not rates. A single column, fifteen lines, the names entered in a different hand, finer, the letters leaning. The writer had been in a hurry to be done, and ashamed to be seen hurrying. The first fourteen were names I did not know. The fifteenth had been scraped away.

Not crossed out. Scraped. The knife had gone under the ink and lifted it from the paper, and the paper had thinned to translucency along that line, so that where the name had been you could see the grain of the wood through the page. I had seen crossings-out before, in other books, black strikes through a name, the name still legible underneath, a person struck but not removed. This was different. This was a name taken out from under, so that the paper itself forgot it had ever carried the letters. The translucency ran the length of the line like a pale seam, and the wood of the table showed through it, and I thought of a wound that has closed over and left the skin thinner than it was, so that you can see the light through the place where the hurt went in.

I put my thumb to it.

The book was dead, of course. It had been a record before it had been a thing, and now it was only a thing, and I am permitted to read dead things. Three seconds. The knife, the hand that held it, the breath drawn in and held, they came up the way such things come, complete and finished, the way a room remembers its own use. A man at a high desk, winter light, the cross-bar of the window behind him throwing a shadow like a letter T across the page. The scrape of the blade under the name came first, a sound I felt in the pad of my thumb more than heard, a dry dragging against the grain, and then the breath held and the second hand came in, his own, writing something beside the bare place. The smell of the mice rose as the vision came, as if the book's own death-smell were part of the memory it held, and the cold of the page under my thumb was the cold of a winter room where a person does a thing he does not want to be seen doing. Then it was only paper again, and the smell of mice, and my thumb on the cold line where a woman's name had been lifted out.

I did not know her. I had not expected to. But the thinness of the paper where she had been told me something the rest of the book did not: that whoever took her out had not wanted the line to read empty. He had wanted the name gone and the fact of its having been there gone with it, as if a person could be unmade by patience. A crossing-out leaves the name under it, accusing. A scraping leaves nothing, which is a colder accusation, because it says the name was never meant to have been there at all.

I closed the book.

It was not my business. Tindle was not my parish. The name, if it had been a name, was not a name I had been asked to recover, and the rules I live by are plain on this: I do not ask who died, I do not ask about money, I do not take payment, and I do not ask for myself. The fifteenth line on a stranger's rate-book in a strange parish was none of those things and all of them at once, and I knew better than to touch it again.

I was twenty years old and had been given a second life because the first had not been used, and I had spent the year since learning the particular patience of a person who has been counted out once and then counted back in. There is a way a person walks after that, a slowness at the threshold, a habit of checking whether the door will open before she lifts her hand to it, and I had it, and I knew it for what it was. I did not waste the afternoon on a scraped line. I swept the hearth, I mended the latch on the pantry, I wrote two letters I had been putting off, and when the light went I lit the lamp and set the Tindle book on the shelf where Edward would find it when he came in.

***

The knock came again after the lamp was lit. It was the same boy, and he had the red cord wound around his fingers, and he held it out before he said anything, the way a boy holds out a fault.

"The cord is the parish's," he said. "The clerk counts it. He counts what goes out and he counts what comes back."

I took the cord. It was warm from his hand and damp where his palm had been.

"And the book?"

"He said to ask whether the book was the one. He said if it was not the one I was to bring it back and not lose the cord."

"The book was the one."

He nodded. He did not go. His eyes went past me to the board where the loaf sat, and this time he did not trouble to hide it.

I cut the loaf. The knife was blunt at the heel. I turned it and used the front of the blade, and the bread came away in a thick slice with the crust pulling.

"Slow," I said. "You will make yourself sick."

He slowed. He ate standing in the door with his back to the lane, in small bites, and the cord lay on the table between us, coiled the way he had dropped it. The coil was neat. A neat coil is wound on purpose and not by accident. The bread was gone. The board was bare.

"Did the clerk read the note before he sealed it?"

"He does not read to me."

"Did he write it at the desk or at the window?"

"At the desk. He wrote it out, and what he did not like went in the fire."

I put my thumb on the coil. A clerk who burns a letter and writes it again has decided what the letter must not say.

"Was anyone with him?"

"A woman came in and stood at the shelf and would not sit. The clerk stopped writing until she went out."

"What did she want?"

"She had a paper. He read it and gave it back."

He wiped his palms on his coat and took up the cord and wound it again around his fingers, over the little finger first and under the thumb, the same way each time.

"Tell the clerk the book was the one," I said. "Tell him I read it."

"He will ask what you read."

"Tell him I read the back."

He went down the lane with the cord on his hand. The dark came up the lane behind him. I stood in the door with the knife in my hand and the cut loaf open on the board, and the smell of the bread was the only warm thing left in the kitchen. The lane was empty.

***

I did not sleep at once. I sat at the window with my coat on, the same window that would not open, and I thought about the woman whose name had been lifted from the page as if she had never been entitled to be written. The coat was my mother's, the wool gone shiny at the elbows, and I wore it because the room held the evening cold and because wearing a dead woman's coat is a way of keeping her hands near mine when the questions come that she would have known how to ask.

I have been given a name twice. The first time it was read over me correctly, which the man who did it said was more than most. The second time it was written into a book that said I was owed a hearing no one had given me. I know what it is to be a line someone else controls. The first time, the line was my life, and a man decided I had not used it and took it back and gave it again, and the giving again was a kind of scraping too, a taking of the used part and a leaving of the unused part for me to find. The woman in the margin had been scraped down to the wood. I had been scraped down to the bone and given back the bone to build on. The shapes are not the same. The hand that scrapes learns one motion and uses it on whatever it means to erase.

That is not why I opened the book again. I opened it again because the shelf was across the room and the book was on it and I am the kind of person who, having put a thing away, will walk back across a cold floor to be sure it is still there.

The fifteenth line caught the lamp. The translucency ran the length of it, a pale seam in the page, and beside the bare place the second hand had written a line I had not read the first time, because the first time I had been watching the scrape and not the margin.

The margin said: *It is entirely lawful.*

I read those four words the way you read a door that has been left open in a house you thought was locked. They were not a threat. They were worse than a threat. They were an account settled, and the settling of it was the part that had been done to a person and called correct. Lawful is the word that admits no argument. A crossing-out can be contested; a name can be re-entered; a person struck can be unstruck by a later hand. But a scraping says the name was never rightly there, and the lawfulness of the scraping says the same thing twice, and the two together leave the woman outside the door with no handle to turn.

I closed the book a second time and put it back on the shelf, and this time I did not get up again.

The window would not open. The name was gone. The words beside it said the taking had been lawful, and lawful is the one word that admits no argument and leaves the smallest wound and the longest scar.

I sat with my coat on until the lamp guttered. Then I went to bed. I did not tell Edward about the book, because telling him would have been asking him to read it for fault, and I was not ready to give the woman in the margin over to fault.

I was only ready to wonder who she had been, which is the first step a person takes before she decides to ask, and the step I have learned, by marriage and by use, to take alone.

---

## Two · The Translucent Line

Edward found the Tindle book on the shelf when he came in for his dinner, and he weighed it in one hand the way he weighed most things, as if the object might be lighter or heavier than its looks and the difference was the only part worth his attention. The rain had followed him in on his coat, and he hung it by the door where the dripping made a small dark flower on the flags, and he set the book on the table and opened it at the back, where the second list began.

"You opened it," he said.

"I did."

He read the fourteen names above the bare place, and then his thumb came to rest on the fifteenth line the way mine had, on the thinned paper where the name had been lifted out. His thumb was broader than mine and the pad of it covered more of the seam, so that for a moment the translucency disappeared under his skin and came back when he lifted, the way a scar vanishes under pressure and returns when the pressure goes. His hand on a page is the only part of him that tells me what he is not saying, and I kept my eyes on it, the rest of him keeping still by training. There is a thing I have never asked him, which is whether the page ever says anything back. He is an auditor and he has put his thumb on ten thousand lines, and a thumb on a live line is not the same as a thumb on a dead one, and I have never once been able to tell which of the two he was doing in the moments he went still over a book. I decided early not to ask. A woman who reads the dead does not ask a man what the paper says to him, because if the answer is nothing then the question was a cruelty, and if the answer is something then she will not know what to do with it.

"You read it," he said. It was not a question.

"I read the book. The book is dead."

"That is not what I asked."

"I know what you asked." I had not touched the line a second time, not since the lamp had caught the margin. "The book is dead. What came up was the man at the desk and the knife. And the words beside it. You read the words."

"I read the words." He turned the book so the lamp fell across the margin. *It is entirely lawful.* "Four words. Written by the same hand that scraped the name, or a different one?"

"I could not tell. The scrape came first, then the hand writing. I did not stay for the comparison."

He closed the book. The cover was stained where the oilcloth had wept in the rain, and the stain spread a little under his palm, and he did not seem to mark it, or marked it and did not care, which is the same thing with Edward. "You could read it again."

"I could."

"Will you?"

I did not answer that with a yes or a no. I set my hand on the board, over the place where the fifteenth line lay under the leather, and the shape of the thinned paper came through the cover under my palm, the seam where a woman had been taken out and the page had not grown back. The cover was smooth and the seam was not, and my palm found it the way a blind person finds a word, by the change in the surface, and the change was the only part of her that remained in the room.

"I will," I said, "if you tell me what you mean to do with what comes up."

He looked at me the way he looked at a column that did not add, which is to say without blame and without surprise, only a steady attention to the part that was wrong. "I mean to write it down. Whatever comes up, I write it down. That is the whole of what I do with it."

That was true, and it was the reason I had married him, though I had not known that at the time and would not have admitted it if I had. He wrote things down and he did not argue with them afterward. A man who argues with his own record is a man who will argue with yours, and Edward had never once disputed a thing he had set in the notebook, not even when the thing was me.

I opened the book again. I put my thumb to the fifteenth line, on the bare place where the name had been, and I let the read come the way you let a door open when you have already seen the hinge rusted.

Three seconds.

The desk was higher than I had stood at, and the light came from a window with a cross-bar that threw a shadow like a letter T across the page. A man's hands, not old, ink under the nails, the knife going under the letters one by one and lifting them, and the lifting was a sound as much as a sight, a dry rasp against the grain that came to the ball of my thumb the way a far bell comes to the teeth. A pause, long enough that the air in the vision seemed to hold its breath. Then the second hand came in, his own, and wrote beside the bare place, and the words took shape in the air in front of me the way they had in the margin: *It is entirely lawful.* The smell of the mice was there too, faint, the book's own death-smell. Under it a colder smell: the smell of a room kept shut through a winter, of wax and damp stone, and of a person who does not mean to be found doing what he is doing.

And then, past the desk, a gate. A woman standing at it with a basket over her arm. She was not young. The basket held something wrapped in cloth, and her other hand rested on the gate as if she meant to open it or close it and had not decided. She looked up, not at me but at the house behind me, the way a person looks at a thing they have been told not to want. Her shawl had slipped at one shoulder and she had not righted it, and the hand on the gate was red with cold, and the cold came off her in the read the way cold comes off a stone you touch in winter, so that my own hand on the page went numb at the tip. Then the three seconds ended, and she was only ink, and the ink was the part where her name had been scraped away.

I took my thumb off the line.

The read had promised a woman and returned a wound. That is the shape of a cold read, and I had been given one where I had half-expected a person. She had not spoken. She had not been given the chance. The gate, the basket, the hand on the wood, those were hers, and the name was not, and the lawfulness of the taking was written next to the empty space where she had been entitled to be.

Edward had his notebook open. He wrote a line, and then another, and then he stopped and read back what he had written, and he nodded once, the way he nodded at a total that came out even. The nib scratched and then stopped, and the stopping was as deliberate as the writing, as if the pen were a person who had said its piece and would not be drawn further.

"The first," I said.

"The first what?"

"Entry in that book. You have written one for every question I have asked since we were married. That is the first from the Tindle book."

He looked at the page. "It is the twenty-fourth I have written since the wedding. It is the first about her."

"Then you are keeping two counts."

"One count," he said, "and a mark against the entries that came out of your hand. The mark is not a number. It is a way of finding them again."

"How many are marked?"

"One. Until tonight, none." He turned the page back a leaf so that I could see the column, and there was the mark, a small stroke in the margin, set against the line he had just written and against nothing else in the book.

"About the woman who is not there."

"About the line where she was." He closed the notebook and set it on the book, and the two objects together made a small weight on the table, the question and the record of the question, neither of them answered. "You will go to Tindle."

"I have not said I will."

"You will, or you will not sleep. You do not sleep when a line will not add. And this is a line that was made not to add."

He was right, and he knew he was right, and he did not press it, which was the part that made it impossible to argue. I had married a man who won arguments by writing them down and then leaving the page open where I could read that he had not finished his side.

The lamp made its small pool around the two books and the notebook, and Edward went to hang his coat, and the room went quiet in the way a room goes quiet when the one person who asks nothing has left it, so that the asking that had been in the air had nowhere to land. I sat with the cold seam under my thumb and did not open the book again. The read had given me all it would give. The rest would have to be found in Tindle, in the parish that had sent the book away rather than keep it, which is the truest thing a parish can say about a name it does not want.

A cold read is not the opposite of a warm one. It is a read that promises a person and returns a space where the person was taken out. I had half-expected, when the gate came up, to see the woman's face, to hear her name spoken in the vision the way names are spoken in the reading of the dead, who keep their names even when the living have struck them. She had kept nothing. The gate was hers, the basket was hers, the red hand on the wood was hers, but the name that would have made those things belong to a person had been scraped to the wood, and the scraping had been lawful, and the lawfulness was the part that left no handle to turn.

I thought of my own reading, the one that had brought me back. The man who read me had read a name that was still there, still written, still mine to be given, and the giving of it had been the whole of my second life. She had been read by a man who took the name out from under, so that there was nothing left to give. The first life had not been used, and the second had been the chance to use it. Hers had been unused and then unmade, so that there was not even a first life left to be given back.

That is a colder thing than death. Death leaves the name. A scraping leaves the wood.

Edward came back from the peg and stood at the other side of the table. He did not sit. He did not open the notebook again. The not-opening was its own kind of entry, the one he made by not making it, the way the clerk had set the pen down at the empty slot year after year and not written. He looked at the book, and then at my thumb on the cover where the seam was, and then at my face, and he found the read in me the way he finds a column that does not add, and he said nothing, because there was nothing to say that the book had not already said in four lawful words.

The thing that would follow me to Tindle, I knew then, was not the name and not the woman but the thin place in the page, the part of her the book could not grow back. A person scraped from a record leaves a thin place in the record, and the thin place is what the next reader finds, and the finding of it is the only meeting the scraped person is allowed to have, with a thumb, through leather, in the cold.

***

Edward went to the shelf for the small chisel and came back and knelt at the sill.

"The latch has gone slack," he said. "It goes slack under my hand."

"It has been slack since I came to the house."

He set the chisel to the plate and turned it. The plate came up off the wood. The paint came up with it. He laid the plate on the sill and turned it over and looked at the back of it.

"The screw has gone through the wood," he said. "There is nothing behind it to hold."

"Then it will not hold."

"Then it will not hold." He said it without weight, the way he said a total. He took the screw out with his thumb and forefinger and set it on the sill beside the chisel, and then he put his hand into the hole in the frame and pressed the wood on the far side of it, and his knuckles went white with the reaching. "There is a batten behind. The wood is soft. It gives under the thumb."

"Can you screw into it?"

"I could screw into it. It would hold, and then it would go slack again, and the slack would be worse for having been mended."

He sat back on his heels. He turned the plate in his hand. The rain came on again. It went at the glass behind him, and the glass went dark, and the room in the glass was this room, doubled and small and turned the wrong way round.

"Put it back," I said.

He put it back. He laid the plate over the hole and drove the screw with the heel of his hand. The screw turned. The screw did not bite. He took it out and set it in again. That time it bit a little. He left it in.

"That will hold until it does not," he said.

"That is most things."

"It is." He stood and wiped his palms. He set the chisel on the shelf and stood with his back to me, and then he said, without turning, "A lock that will not hold is worse than no lock. A lock that will not hold teaches a person to lean on it."

I put my thumb on the seam under the leather. "And a page that has been scraped?"

"That teaches nothing. That is only gone." He came back and sat and drew the notebook to him and did not open it. "A strike leaves a name to be read. A scrape leaves a space to be filled. A space can be filled by any hand. A name only by the one that knows it."

"Then the space is an invitation."

"It is a lock. The same as the latch. It looks like a way in. It is a way of keeping a thing shut."

He put his hand flat on the notebook and left it there, and we sat with the window at our backs, and the window stayed where it was.

***

I went to the window that would not open and put my shoulder to it again, though I knew it would not give, because the testing of it had become a way of measuring the day, and the day had not yet told me its size. The rain had stopped. The glass was cold against my sleeve, and the cold went up my arm the way the cold from the read had gone to the tip of my thumb. The two colds met at the shoulder and made a single ache. It was the shape of the woman at the gate, though I did not name it that then.

I have been given a second life because the first had not been used, and I know the particular weight of a thing withheld. She had been withheld a name. I had been withheld a use. The shapes are not the same, but the hand that withholds learns one motion and uses it twice. The man at the desk had withheld her name by scraping it; the world had withheld my use by taking my first life back and giving it again with the used part scraped away. I had found the use anyway, by asking, because the asking was the one thing no one could scrape out of me. She had not been given the chance to ask. The gate, the basket, the hand on the wood, those were the only things left of a person who had been told, by a lawful hand, that she was not entitled to want the house behind the gate.

I did not tell Edward that. I told him only that I would think, and I did. In the morning the book was where I had left it, and the notebook was beside it, and the woman at the gate was still nowhere. I had not decided whether to go to Tindle, only whether to open the book again. That was the smaller question, and the one I could answer.

I opened it. The fifteenth line caught the morning light the way it had caught the lamp, a pale seam in the page, and the margin still said *It is entirely lawful*, and the lawful taking of a woman's name was still the coldest thing in the room.

I put my thumb to the seam one more time, through the cover this time, so that I would not have to open the book to find her. The translucency was there under the leather, a thin place in a thin page, and my thumb knew it without seeing it, the way you know a healed wound by the change in the skin. That would be the thing that followed me to Tindle, I thought. Not the name. The thin place. The part of her that the book could not grow back.

I slept with the book on the shelf across the room and woke once with my thumb pressed to the empty air where the seam would be, as if in sleep my hand had gone looking for the thin place and found only the dark. The hand knew before the mind did. That is the way of a second life: the body keeps the account the mind would rather not tally, and tallies it in the small hours, with a thumb on air, the way a person counts coins they do not have by the weight of the ones they lost. By morning the seam was still there under the leather when I checked. The checking was its own kind of asking, the kind that requires no answer, because the only question is whether the wound is still where you left it. The wound was, and would be, until Tindle, where the man who made it might still be alive to be asked why he had chosen the knife over the crossing-out, and the lawfulness over the mercy.

---

## Three · The Name That Was Not Left

Edward kept the audit office in the room above the bakehouse, where the warmth rose through the floor and made the ink spread if you left the stopper off the bottle. I went up after breakfast, when he had gone to walk the bounds of the north field and would not be there to watch me take the Tindle register down from the shelf where he kept the books of parishes that had written to him for fault. The stair was narrow. The wall on one side was warm to the touch from the ovens below, and I put my hand to it as I climbed, the way you put a hand to a living thing to know it is there. The warmth was the only honest heat in the house that morning. The hearth downstairs had not yet caught, and the air held the grey cold of a spring that had not decided to be spring.

The register was a heavier volume than the rate-book that had come in oilcloth. This was Tindle's current list, the one the clerk kept each year and sent a copy of to the archdeacon. I set it beside the scraped rate-book on the table and opened both at the place where the second list had been. The register smelled of fresh ink and the wax of the archdeacon's seal, a clean smell, the smell of an account that means to be believed, and the rate-book beside it smelled of mice and river and the years, so that the two books together on the one table were the smell of a parish that had been honest once and learned to be lawful instead.

The rate-book, at the back, held fifteen lines in the fine hurrying hand. Fourteen names, and the fifteenth scraped to translucency.

The register, in its proper order, held the rate-payers by dwelling, the same as the rate-book's front. I ran my finger down the column until I came to the fifteenth entry from the top of the second list, and the slot was empty.

Not scraped. Empty. The clerk who kept the register had left a gap where the name should have been, as if he had come to that line each year and set his pen down and taken it up again without writing, for forty years, and the gap had become a kind of entry in itself, a blank that everyone pretended was filled. The empty slot was cleaner than the scraped line, and the cleanliness was the crueler thing, because a scraping is an act of anger or fear, a hand that could not bear the name, but an empty slot is a hand that came to the line every year and chose, calmly, not to begin. The one is a wound. The other is the decision, renewed annually, never to make the wound in the first place.

I read the fourteen names above it. They were plain Tindle names, Hallow, Pettifer, a family of Quines who had held the same croft since the survey. The fifteenth, wherever it had been, was gone from both books: scraped from the old rate-book, left blank in the new register. The woman at the gate with the basket had been written out of one and never written into the other. I had seen the two ways a person can be removed, and the second was the one that left no scar to read, only the smooth page where a name should have been, and the smoothness was a kind of scar too, the kind that heals over so well you cannot find the place to press.

I put my thumb to the empty slot in the register. The register was a live record in the sense that it was still in use, but the slot itself was dead, no hand had touched it this year, and the blank was older than the ink around it. The read came, thin and short, the way a cold room gives up its cold.

A hand setting a pen down. The same desk, the same cross-bar window throwing the T-shaped shadow. Not the man who had scraped the name. A different hand, steadier, an older hand, setting the pen into its rest year after year at that line and not writing, because to write would have been to say the name, and to say the name would have been to undo the scraping, and the scraping had been lawful. The vision held the smell of the room, wax and damp stone, and a small sound, the click of the pen against the rest, repeated across years until the click became a habit and the habit became the way the parish remembered her, which was not at all. Then it was only paper again, and the empty slot stayed empty under my thumb, and the cold of it was the cold of a thing that has been chosen not to be, over and over, until the choosing was the only thing left.

I had expected, when the read came, some small warmth, a person remembered, a name spoken in a kitchen, something that would have told me who she had been. The read had given me only the annual setting-down of a pen, which is the coldest thing a blank can yield: not hatred, not even neglect, but the quiet yearly choice not to begin. A person who is hated is a person who was known. A person who is neglected was known once and forgotten. But a person who is met, every year, with a pen set down and not a word, was never begun, and the not-beginning was lawful, and the lawfulness was the lock Edward had named, the one that admits no key.

I closed the register.

A person who is not in the book is not a person the parish must feed, or house, or count. That is the use of leaving a slot empty. It is lawful, and it is cheap, and it is the reason a woman can stand at a gate with a basket and no one in the record can say she was entitled to be there. I knew the shape of that use. The Letter of the Dead had said I was owed a hearing, and the hearing had been given, and the giving of it had cost the year I had been given back, so that my own line in the book had been a fight and a winning and a cost, and hers had been a setting-down of a pen, year after year, at a line that was already empty.

I had been entitled to be there. The Letter of the Dead had said so, after the fight, after the board, after the words spoken in the room where they had tried to say I was not. I knew what it was to be a line someone else controls, and to win the line back, and to know the winning had cost the year I had been given. And this woman had not won. She had been scraped, and left, and the parish had paid for her in a way that did not require them to name her, for forty years.

I sat with the two books open, the scraped line and the empty slot, and I thought about the kind of person who scraps a name and the kind who leaves the gap where it was, and whether the second was the more cruel because it was the more patient. The scraping is done once, in a passion or a panic, and after it the name is gone and the doer can repent or forget. But the empty slot is renewed each year by a steady hand that has made its peace with the not-writing. The peace is the part that cannot be repented. The man who set the pen down never struck a blow, and a man who never struck a blow has nothing to be sorry for, in the eyes of the law that wrote *It is entirely lawful* beside the bare place.

***

The knock came at the door below while the register lay open, and it came again before I was down the stair, and the knocking was the knocking of a person who has been kept waiting by doors before and does not trust them.

A woman stood in the yard. She had a basket on her hip and a paper in her hand. The paper had been folded and unfolded until the folds had gone soft. The corners had furred.

"The office is shut," I said. "The man who keeps it is on the bounds."

"I know where he is. I waited at the bakehouse and he did not come down." She held out the paper. "They have counted my wash-house."

I took the paper. It was a rate notice, in a fair copy hand, with a list down the left side. Against the wash-house there was a mark.

"Who counted it?"

"The assessor came up the lane and stood at my gate and counted what he could see. He counted the wash-house because the wash-house has a window and the window faces the lane. He did not come in. He did not ask whose it is."

"Is it yours?"

"It is my brother's, and my brother lives at the back of it, and no one has counted him."

She said it without heat. The basket on her hip was covered with a cloth. The cloth was clean. She shifted the basket while she talked, the way a person shifts a weight they have decided to carry rather than set down.

"The assessor's book is not the parish register," I said.

"It is the book they take the money from."

"That is true."

"Then it is the book that matters." She looked past me up the stair, at the light on the landing. "Is that his room up there?"

"He is on the bounds."

"Then I will come when he is in. I will keep coming. And he will count the wash-house or he will write down why he will not."

She took the paper back. She folded it along its old folds and put it inside her sleeve. Then she went out through the yard with the basket on her hip. At the gate she set the basket down. She lifted the cloth and looked at what was under it. Then she took it up again and went into the lane. The lane took her. The gate did not shut behind her.

The yard was quiet. The boy was sweeping at the far end by the wall. He had stopped sweeping and stood with the broom across his body until she was gone, and then he began again at the same stroke he had used before. The yard smelled of bread.

I went up the stair. The register was open where I had left it. I sat down and put my hand flat on the empty slot, and the slot was still empty, and the paper that had marked it was folded in a sleeve and going down the lane.

***

Edward came in at noon with the smell of the north field on him and the mud of the ditch on his boots. He saw the two books on the table and the place I had been sitting and he did not ask what I had found. He hung his coat and stood at the window, which in the audit office opened, and looked out at the bakehouse yard, where the boy who had brought the parcel was now sweeping. The boy did not know he was watched. Edward watched him the way he watched a column, for whether the swept pile added to the swept pile or sat beside it.

"You went to Tindle in your head," he said.

"I went to Tindle in your shelf."

He came to the table and looked at the empty slot, and then at the scraped line, and then he sat in the other chair and set his notebook on his knee, not opening it. "The register is this year's. The rate-book is old. Same parish, same list, same gap. She is in neither."

"She is in the margin of the old one. *It is entirely lawful.*"

"Then the gap is lawful too. A lawful gap is still a gap."

I looked at him. He had not been to Tindle. He had not read the books the way I had. But he had seen the two empty places and he had put them together the way he put columns together, and he was not wrong. He sees the shape of a thing and I see the thing, and between us the thing and its shape are both accounted for, which is the whole of why I can ask and he can write and neither of us has to say the word *go* to make the journey start.

The two empty places sat on the table between us, the scraped line and the blank slot, and I thought of the woman they enclosed the way you think of a person by the chair they left empty, not by the face you never saw. She had been scraped from one book and never entered in the other, and the two removals together made a person who existed only in the space where the parish had agreed she would not, and the agreement was forty years old and renewed every year by a steady hand that set the pen down without writing. A crossing-out can be read around. A blank slot can be read as a blank, which is to say not read at all, which is the more complete erasure, because the eye slides past a gap and the mind fills it with nothing, and nothing is the one thing that cannot be contested in a court or a kitchen. The woman at the gate had been given nothing to contest with. The lawfulness of her removal was the lock, and the blank slot was the silence on the other side of it. The two together had kept her out for four decades, and would keep her out until someone with a thumb and a second life came to the door and asked, out loud, who had been taken out.

"A person who was not even left a name," I said. "Someone has to ask once for her. Not to fill the slot. To ask who she was, before the gap becomes the whole of what she was."

"You mean to go to Tindle."

"I mean to ask." I did not say where. The asking could begin with a letter, or with a walk, or with the clerk who had sent the rate-book. But Edward heard the word *go* in it anyway, because the word *ask* in my mouth had always meant *go*, since the first time he had watched me walk into a room that did not want me, and he had not tried to stop me, only written down that I had gone.

He opened the notebook. He did not write. He only turned it to a fresh page and set the pen beside it, the way the older hand had set the pen down at the empty slot, year after year, and not written.

"That is the second," I said.

"The second what?"

"Entry about her. The first was the read. This is the decision to ask. You write the decisions too."

"I write what you do. You have decided. I write the decision." He closed the notebook without writing, because the decision was not yet a thing he could set down as done. "When you go, take the rate-book. The register they will not give you. The rate-book they sent, so the rate-book is yours to carry."

I had not thought of carrying it. But he was right. The book had come to the house and no one had asked for it back, and a parish does not send a thing to a woman unless it has decided to let her keep it. Tindle had given me the scraped line and the margin that called it lawful, and I had carried both without once asking who had been taken out of the page. That was the question. I would carry them back to Tindle and put the question there. My hands, on the table, had curled into fists without my knowing it, the way they do when a thing lawful and cruel sits in front of them and they want to do something with it that is not lawful in return. I opened them and laid them flat. The flat hand is the one that carries a book, and the fist is the one that throws it. I meant to carry.

I closed both books. The register went back to the shelf where it belonged, because it was Tindle's and I am not a thief of records. The rate-book I wrapped again in its oilcloth, because it was mine now, the way a question becomes yours when you decide to carry it.

The window in the audit office opened. Edward had left it open, and the breeze came in with the smell of bread, and I stood at it a moment with the oilcloth parcel in my hands, and I thought about the woman at the gate, and the basket, and the hand on the wood, and the annual setting-down of the pen. The parcel was heavier than it looked. The boards were warped and the mice-chewed corners held the weight wrong, so that it pulled at my wrist. The pull was the first of the small bodily tellings that would follow me to Tindle: the wrist that knew the book was mine to carry, the thumb that would find the seam through the leather, the fist that wanted to throw what could not be thrown at. A person scraped from one book and left from the other is not a person the parish must answer for. That is the whole design. And the design is lawful, which is the part that made my hands want to do something with the parcel that was not lawful in return, not to fill the slot, not to write the name, but to ask, out loud, in the place where she had been taken out, who she had been.

"I will go," I said to Edward's back, because he was at the window and not listening, or listening in the way he listened, which was to let the words land and not turn around.

He did not turn. "Take the book," he said. "And write nothing in it but the question."

That was the whole of what he said, and it was the whole of what I meant to do.

---

## Four · Tea and the Account

The kettle came to the boil at the hour Edward came in from the field, and I poured the tea without being asked, because the pouring was the part of the evening I could do without a word, and a word was the part of the evening he was not ready for until his boots were off and his hands were warm. The steam rose between us and carried the smell of the leaves, a plain smell, the cheap black he liked, and I watched it go up and break against the low ceiling of the room, the way a small honest thing goes up and meets the day and does not come back changed.

He took the cup. He did not look up from the letter he was reading. His fingers closed around the china the way they closed around a pen, as if the object might try to get away, and he drank without tasting, the way a man drinks who is still somewhere else. The cup was warm against my own fingers as I set it down. The warmth stayed in my hands after I let go. A service done without thanks leaves a warmth in the doer that the receiver never feels. I have learned not to mind the unevenness. The pouring is its own kind of asking. It does not require an answer, and therefore it cannot be refused.

I set my own cup down and watched the steam. The oilcloth parcel with the Tindle rate-book sat on the side table where I had put it, and the lamp made a soft square of light on its tied cord, and I had the feeling of a thing that had been sent to me and was now waiting to be returned, the way a question waits to be answered. The red cord had darkened where the oilcloth had wept, and the darkening was a small record of the journey the book had made, river to cart to carrier's boy to my table, and the journey back would be the asking, and the asking would be the only thing I could do for a woman whose name had been lifted to the wood.

"I went to Tindle in your shelf," I said.

He set the letter down. "You found the gap."

"The gap, and the scraping, and the words in the margin. A woman was taken out of the book and the taking was lawful, and the parish has paid for her in a way that does not require them to name her, for forty years."

He turned the cup in his hands a quarter turn, and then another, and did not drink. The quarter turns were the thing he did when a column would not add. A small rotation of the object, as if to show it a different light. I had seen him do it a hundred times over other people's accounts, and twice over mine. The sight of it now, over tea, told me he had already set the two empty places side by side in his head. He had found them to make a shape.

"You read her."

"I read the book. The book is dead. I did not read a living person. That is the rule I keep, the one I have never broken."

"You read the empty space she left." He said it the way he said most things, without weight, as if stating the weather. "The book is dead. The gap in it is not a person. But you read the gap, and the gap is hers."

I had not thought of it that way. I had thought of the read as the woman at the gate, the basket, the hand on the wood. But he was right, and he was usually right about the shape of a thing even when I had seen the thing first. The read had not given me the woman. It had given me the place where she had been taken out, and the place was hers whether she filled it or not. A scraping removes the name but leaves the subtraction, and the subtraction is a kind of presence, the way a missing tooth is present in the mouth. He had read that in me before I had read it in the book, because he reads the empty spaces in people the way I read them in paper, and the reading of a space is the one reading neither of us has to touch dead things to do.

"I read the place," I said. "Not the person."

"The person is in the place. That is what a scraping is. It does not remove the woman. It removes the record of her, and leaves the record of the removal. You read the removal. The removal is hers."

The tea had gone cool. I drank it anyway, because the cooling of it was the only thing in the room that was behaving as it ought, and I wanted one honest object to attend to. The coolness was a clean thing on the tongue, no betrayal in it, and I let the plainness of it stand against the lawful cruelty of the margin, because a person needs one true object in a room full of lawful wounds, and the cooling tea was mine.

He watched me drink. "You mean to go to Tindle."

"I mean to ask who she was."

"Those are the same journey, said by a person who does not like to say *go*."

"I do not mind the word *go*. I mind the word *why*, when it is asked of me and I have no answer that satisfies the asker."

He set the cup down. "You have an answer. You told me. A person who was not left a name. Someone has to ask once."

"Someone. Not necessarily me."

"It is always you. It was you for your mother, and for your aunt, and for the boy whose name Nunn had let be taken. It is you because you are the one who was given back, and the given-back person cannot bear a line that will not add."

I did not answer that, because it was true and because it was the kind of true that sits in the room like a third person, and I had not yet decided whether to introduce myself to it. I had been given back. The first life had not been used, and the second had been the chance to use it, and the using of it had so far been a string of other people's names that someone had tried to take out of books. My mother. My aunt. The Nunn boy. And now this woman at the gate, whose name had been scraped and whose gap had been left, lawfully, for forty years. The given-back person learns one thing above all: that the gift was a second chance to ask, and the asking is the only use the gift was ever for, and a chance not used is the one sin the second life will not forgive, because it is the sin the first life was taken for.

"I will go," I said. "But not to fill the slot. To ask."

"Take the rate-book. It was sent to you. In Tindle they will know it came back, and they will know why you carry it, and the carrying of it is the asking."

He opened his notebook. He wrote two lines, and then he read them back, and he nodded, the way he nodded at a total that came out even. The nib had scratched once, paused, scratched again, and the two scratches were the sound of a thing being set down that could not be argued with, and I had married that sound, in the end, more than I had married the man, because the sound was the promise that whatever I found, someone would write it without disputing it.

"The third," I said.

"The third about her. The first was the read. The second was the decision. This is the intent to go. You move in threes, Mercy. Read, decide, go. The book moves in ones. It sits until someone carries it."

"That is the difference between a person and a record."

"That is the difference between you and the gap. The gap sits. You do not."

I poured him a second cup, because his was empty. The pouring was the one service I could perform that did not require him to answer a question. He took it without looking up, the way he had taken the first. I let the small correctness of the gesture stand between us. The larger incorrectness of the thing we were not yet saying could stand where it was. The gesture was its own kind of account. The tea given. The cup taken. The not-looking-up that meant he was still somewhere else, and would come back when his hands were warm. The coming-back was the only promise he had ever kept without saying it. A man who writes things down will always come back to the page. A man who loves a woman who walks into rooms that do not want her will come back to find her gone and the door still swinging. He will write that down too, without blame.

The thing we were not saying was that he read my empties too. Not with a thumb on dead paper. With a cup taken without looking up, and a notebook opened at the page where the decision was not yet written, and a quarter-turn of china that meant he was still somewhere else and would come back when his hands were warm. He had read the space where I had not yet said *go*, and he had found me there, and he had not filled it. He had only left the page open.

I have been given a second life, and in that life I married a man who writes things down and does not argue with them, and who reads the empty spaces in me the way I read the empty spaces in books, without ever presuming to write the name. That is the whole of what he does, and it is more than most hands would do with a gap.

The pouring of the tea was a thing my mother had done, in the house before the door that would not open, and I had learned it from her the way a person learns a prayer, by repetition and not by understanding, and I had not thought of that until the cup was in my hand and the steam was rising and the motion was already done. She had poured for a man who did not look up, in a kitchen that smelled of the same cheap leaves. The pouring had been the only service she could perform that did not require him to answer a question he did not have an answer for. I had become her in the doing of it without meaning to. A person becomes their mother in the small honest gestures, and not in the large ones. The woman at the gate had had no one to pour for her. The basket on her arm had held something wrapped in cloth. I had not been able to read what. Pouring Edward's second cup, I put it to myself as food, or a letter, or only the weight of a thing carried to a door that would not open. The same door my mother's kitchen had held. The same door I had pushed my shoulder against that morning and found sealed. A person who is not in the book is not a person anyone pours for. The parish had paid for her in a way that did not name her, which is the coldest service of all, the kind that feeds a body and forgets a name, so that the body lives and the name does not, and the living is the proof that the forgetting was lawful.

The parcel waited on the side table, and my wrist remembered its weight before my hand did, the way the body keeps the account the mind would rather not tally. The thumb found the seam through the oilcloth. The seam was hers. The finding was mine. I would carry both to Tindle, the book and the wound in it, and the carrying would be the asking, and the asking would be the only service the parish had not yet performed for a woman it had fed without naming. Edward had said to write nothing in it but the question. I would write the question. He would write the answers, if any came, in the notebook that now held the first three entries about a person who had no entries of her own. Three reads. Three silences. Three writings-down. And the woman still at the gate with her basket and her red hand on the wood.

The gate would open, or it would not. The question would be asked, or it would not. I had been given a second life because the first had not been used, and I would not be the hand that left the slot empty a forty-first year. The thumb knew the seam. The wrist knew the weight. The rest of me would learn the way to Tindle, and the learning would be the walking, and the walking would be the asking, and the asking would be the whole of what a given-back person is for.

***

The knock came at the back door, low on the panel. Edward lifted his pen and did not set it down. I took the lamp and went.

The carrier's boy stood in the rain with his cap in his hand and his coat buttoned wrong, a button in the wrong hole and the collar standing up crooked.

"The cord," he said.

"The cord is on the parcel."

"The clerk counts it. He counted it out and he counts it in, and the count came short. He said if it came short I was to bring back the cord and not the book."

"Then the book stays tied until it goes back."

He looked past me at the table. Rain ran off the eaves behind him and made a line in the mud at his heels. He wiped his face with the flat of his hand.

"He will ask what held it," he said. "He will ask what you tied it with, and I will have nothing to say."

I set the lamp on the shelf and opened the drawer and took out my mother's box. The clasp was brass and the lid had warped. Inside, the wool lay in hanks, red, a thicker red than the parish cord, ravelled at the ends where she had wound it off a sleeve. I took the shears and cut a length and shut the box.

"Take this back to him."

He held it away from his coat the way he had held the parcel away from his coat in the morning. "It is wool."

"It is wool, and it is red, and it will answer his count until the book comes home. Tell him the cord stays on the parcel until the parcel is opened in his office, and the wool goes now as surety."

"He will not know what to write."

"He will write what he likes. He is a clerk. The writing is the part he is sure of."

The boy wound the wool around his fingers, over the little finger first and under the thumb, the way he wound the cord. The wool was too thick for the winding and stood up in loops. He put it inside his coat and buttoned the coat over it, and the buttoning took him a while.

"The cart goes east at first light," he said. "The driver will not stop at a gate for nothing."

"Then he will stop for a fare."

"He will ask it."

"Let him ask."

He went. The rain took him at the corner of the house. I stood in the door with the lamp in my hand and the smell of wet wool on my fingers. The lane was empty. The parcel on the table was still tied in the parish red.

Edward had not moved. He wrote a line and read it back and set the pen down.

"Wool for cord," he said.

"Wool for cord."

"He will enter it as a loan."

"He may enter it as he likes. It is my mother's wool, and it goes to Tindle before I do."

He wrote that down too.

***

"I will take the book," I said.

"Take it."

"And I will write nothing in it but the question."

He looked up then, finally, from the second cup, and his eyes were the colour of the river under cloud, and he said, "Write the question. I will write the answers, if any come. That is the division of the labour, and it has always been."

The lamp made its square of light on the oilcloth cord, and the rate-book waited, and the notebook waited, and the woman at the gate waited in the only place she had been left, which was the margin where someone had written that her taking had been lawful. My thumb found the seam through the oilcloth without my meaning it to. The thin place in the page called to the thin place in the thumb. The small answering pressure was the bodily telling that would follow me the whole way to Tindle. The thumb that knew her by the wound in the paper. The hand that poured without being asked. The wrist that already carried the weight of a book that was not mine to keep and not mine to give back unasked.

I drank the rest of my cool tea. The evening was honest, and the gap was unlawful in nothing but its patience, and I would go to Tindle, and I would ask, and the asking would be the whole of what I could do for a woman who had been told, by a lawful hand, that she was not entitled to be written.

That was the account of the day, and it added, and the adding of it was the only warmth in the room.

---

## Five · The First Wrong Guess

Tindle lay eight miles east. Eight miles is a short distance to a person who has been given time she did not expect to have. I followed the stone road as it softened under the wheels into something the colour of old tea, and then gave way entirely to clay at the bridge. The clay took the wheels and would not let them turn as they had. My driver cursed it under his breath and leaned his weight on the reins, and the horse dropped to a walk that was barely a walk. The clay smelled of the river, of mud and rain and the green rot of standing water, and it rose in wet ropes around the spokes and flung itself in clods against the undercarriage. Each turn of the wheel cost us, and the horse's haunches worked and the harness creaked. The slowness suited my purpose. A road that takes its time gives a person room to think, and I used the room. The parish of Tindle kept its secrets in clay as much as in ink, and the road into it was the first of those secrets, slow on purpose, meant to wear a person down before she ever reached the door. The air carried the thin cold of an early dark. The light leaned long by the time the parish office came into view, its door already drawing shut and a young man reaching for the lock.

I told the driver to wait and stepped down into the clay. It pulled at my boots the way a hand pulls at a hem, reluctant to let go, and the evening pressed close and damp against my cheeks. The young man at the door had the look of someone who had been told to close up and meant to, and the parcel in my hands changed his mind before a word passed between us.

He had the thin wrists of someone who had lifted nothing heavier than a ledger. He looked at the oilcloth parcel in my hands, then at my face, then at the parcel again, and the recognition came to him before I had opened my mouth.

"You are the woman from Collow," he said. "The one who reads things."

"I am the woman from Collow who was sent your old rate-book. I have come to return it, and to ask about the fifteenth line."

He did not reach for the key. "The fifteenth line is not a thing the parish discusses."

"It is a thing the parish scraped. The scraping is discussed by the margin, whether you speak of it or not."

He gave the small flinch the young give when a true thing is set down that they had hoped to avoid, and he hid it by straightening his shoulders. "The old clerk who kept that book is dead. The new one, me, found it in the loft and sent it to the audit office because it did not sit right. I did not know what was in the margin until it was gone."

"Who scraped the name?"

"We do not know. It was done before my time, and before the old clerk's time. The gap has been in the register longer than any man in the parish has been alive."

"Forty years."

"Longer, perhaps. The old rate-book is from the year of the war with France. The name was gone before that book was closed."

I had come ready for a refusal. Or a lie. Or an elder fetched to refuse and lie more politely than a boy could manage. Instead he had given me the one answer I had not braced for. No one now living had done it. No one now living recalled who had. The scraping was older than the parish's memory of the scraping, and that is the coldest sort of lawful, a wrong kept so long that the keeping of it has worn smooth into custom.

"A woman," I said. "Standing at a gate with a basket."

He blinked. "You saw her."

"I read the book. The book is dead. What came up was a woman at a gate. That is all I will say of it, and all I know."

The key turned then. He let me in. The office was a single room with a shuttered window and the close smell of lamp oil and damp stone. He lit a lamp against the failing light. The flame threw his shadow tall on the wall. He set the oilcloth parcel on the table. The current register lay open at the very place I had held empty in my mind since morning. His hands were clean and uncertain when they met the book, the hands of a person doing a task no one had trained him for, and the register beneath them was a heavier thing than he knew. The lamp drew a circle of yellow on the table and left the corners of the room to the dark, and in that circle the open register lay like a mouth held open, waiting to be told what to say. The slot was still blank. He had not filled it. The old clerk had not filled it. The gap had outlived them both and was reaching for a third keeper.

The blank ran the width of the line, a clean absence where a name should sit, and the paper there had none of the thinning the old book carried. This slot had never been filled. The other had been filled and then taken out. Two kinds of empty, and I had come to learn the difference between them.

"You think she was a pauper," I said. "A child left at the gate, perhaps, never claimed, written out because no one would pay for her."

"That is what the old clerk believed. He used to say she was a foundling. The basket was for the child."

"A basket at a gate is a foundling's mark. But the woman I read was not a child. She was a woman full grown, with a hand on the wood as if she meant to open it or close it and had not decided. And the basket held something wrapped in cloth, not a child."

He had no answer to that. He had inherited a belief and a gap. The belief was a foundling. The gap was a woman. The two did not meet, and he had never been asked to make them meet, because no one had come to ask before me. I understood that silence. A question left to rot in a parish book is the one waste I cannot abide, and I had come to ask it for a woman who had not been asked in her own life.

I set my thumb to the empty slot in the register, there in his sight, because the book was live but the slot was dead, and the dead part was the part I had come to read.

Three seconds.

The woman at the gate. The basket on her arm. Behind her the gate was of pale wood, the kind that marks a boundary between a yard and a road, and the paint on it had peeled to the grain. The basket was shallow, woven, the sort a market woman carries, and the cloth inside it was grey with use. She unwrapped the cloth, and inside she found no child and no bread. A small iron ring, a man's ring, the kind a labourer wears to keep a wound from a rope or a handle. The iron came up cold under the cloth, and the cold ran from the slot into my thumb as if the page itself remembered winter. She turned the ring once in her palm, the way a person turns a thing they are not sure they are permitted to hold. Then she wrapped it again. The cloth was drawn tight. The knot was worked with the care of someone covering the one possession another person has a claim to. The smell of river-wet wool rose from the basket as she did it, and her hand stayed on the knot a moment longer than needful, as if the wrapping were also a holding. She did not look up from the ring. Her mouth was set the way a person's mouth sets when they are performing a small obedience they do not believe in. The gate stood open behind her, and beyond it the road ran grey and empty to a line of trees that had lost their leaves. No one came. The ring accounted for the stillness better than any child would have, for a child begs a parish to open its door, while a pledge asks nothing and receives the same.

I lifted my thumb from the slot.

"The ring," the clerk said. He read my face. "You saw the ring."

"A ring, then. A man's work-ring, iron. She kept it as if it were hers to keep and not hers to keep, both at once."

He shook his head. "That is not a foundling's mark. That is a pledge. A woman who carries a man's ring wrapped in cloth is carrying a debt, or a promise, or a thing pawned that she means to redeem."

I had guessed wrong. I had read the basket and the gate as a foundling, the easy mercy of it, the orphan the parish could pity without cost to itself. I had let the basket mean tenderness to me, a love that set a child down at a gate because it could not keep what it carried. But the ring was a pledge, and a pledge is not mercy, it is a claim, and a woman who carries another's ring to a gate she is not entitled to open is not a foundling. She is a person standing at the edge of a right held by someone else.

A pledge names a debt and a debtor in the same breath. Whoever had owned that ring had owned a hold over her, and the hold had outlasted his writing of her name. The parish had not forgotten her out of kindness. It had kept her out, which is a different and colder thing. A name scraped is a name the parish has decided it need not answer to. A pledge is the reason it decided. The two sat together in my mind the way a lock sits with its key, separate and made for each other, and I had only the lock in my hand and the key still lost in the loft of forty years. The clerk held himself still in the lamplight, careful, the way a person holds still after being shown that the story he inherited was built on a thing he had never seen. He had no words for it. I had the words, and they were colder than he was ready for.

The reader of this, if there is one, would have guessed with me. Basket, gate, foundling. The ring undoes it. She was not abandoned. She was bound.

"Thank you," I said to the clerk, because he had given me the undoing, which is worth more than the guessing.

"You will ask the elders," he said. "They know more than they will say. Old Pettifer was alive when the gap was new. He will not speak of it. He will be in the pit at the inn tonight, and a woman with a rate-book is a stranger he will want to look at."

I wrapped the parcel again. The rate-book was mine to carry, and I would carry it to the inn, and to old Pettifer, and to whatever end the asking reached. The orphan theory was wrong. The pledge theory was right. It was the colder of the two. A pledge means there had been a hand that held the claim. A claim that outlives the holder is the kind that earns a name scraped from the page.

***

"The book came out of the loft," I said. "Show me where it lay."

He looked at the door he had not yet locked. "The vestry does not favour visitors above the office."

"I am the woman who was sent the book. I have brought it back. I would see the place it kept."

He took the ladder from the wall and set it under the hatch. He went up in his good coat and pushed the hatch, and the hatch gave with a sound of dry hinges. Dust came down in a slow sift and settled on the register below.

"Hold the light higher," he said. "It goes dark at the top."

I held the lamp at the foot of the ladder with my arm straight. The light went up the rungs and made a yellow room of the roof space. He crawled in on his knees. The boards took his weight and complained. Something moved in the straw at the far end, and stopped moving.

"The box is here," he said. "I brought the book down in it."

The box came over the edge, corner first, and he came after it. It was a parish box, the lid warped, the lock broken open long ago. I set the lamp on the table beside it.

"Empty it."

He turned it over. Out came a nest of straw and paper, a pen with the nib gone, a tallow end, a stone that had been used to weigh a paper flat. The dust of it went into the lamplight and hung there. Nothing else.

"Nothing of hers," he said. "I told you. It was only the book."

"Nothing of hers in the box." I put my thumb in the corner where the dust lay thickest and the wood had kept its grain. "The box is the parish's. The book was the parish's. She was in neither. She was in a line."

His hands were clean. The dust on his cuffs was the only dirt on him.

"The vestry meets," he said. "If they ask whether I let anyone above the office, I cannot say no and be believed."

"Then do not say no. Say she came back with the book and asked to see the loft, and you took her up, because the loft was where it lay."

"That is the truth."

"That is the truth in the order that will keep your place." I took up the parcel. "A man who tells the truth in the wrong order is a man looking for another place."

He carried the ladder back to the wall and set it where it had stood. The mark of it on the whitewash showed that it had stood there a long while.

"The hatch was open when I found it," he said. "I have thought since that the last man up there did not trouble to close it."

"Then he meant the book to be found."

"He may have meant nothing."

"A man who keeps a book forty years does not leave a hatch open by accident." I set my palm flat on the oilcloth. "He kept it and he left it open, and they do not agree."

The lamp had begun to smoke at the corner of the glass. The smoke went up in a thin line and bent toward the door.

***

I walked to the inn with the clay clinging to my heels and the lamp-smoke of the parish office still at the back of my throat. My reading thumb stayed curled against my palm as if the wrap were still in it, and I had to straighten it twice on the road before it would lie flat. The cold of the slot had not left it. The road was dark. The inn was warm with lamp.

The inn stood at the end of the lane with a light in the lower window and the sound of men's voices rolling out through the open door. The pit, they called the public room, and the clerk had said old Pettifer would be in it. I had a stranger's rate-book and a scraped name and a ring that was not a child, and I meant to set all three before a man who had been alive when the gap was new.

Old Pettifer. The clerk had spoken the name the way people speak of a wall, as something that has always been there and will not move for a knock. I had knocked on such walls before, and learned they answer only when ready, and sometimes not even then.

The lane to the inn was narrow and the hedges on either side were black with the year's last rain. A dog barked once from a distant yard and was answered by none. I kept the parcel close and the thumb that had read the slot curled tight. The longer I walked, the colder that empty line grew, and the cold went up the wrist and into the arm. The slot meant to follow me to the pit. It meant to sit at the table unasked.

The inn grew as I neared it. The light widened to a square of gold on the wet ground. The voices inside rose and fell without shape, a tide of men settling into their evening the way men do. They were unaware that a question older than most of them was about to walk through their door. Somewhere in that room old Pettifer sat with his ale and his silence, a man who had been alive when the gap was new and had chosen, across all those years, not to close it. I meant to be the closing. I had no answer for it yet. I had only the ring, and the wrap, and the certainty that what I had taken for mercy was a claim wearing a basket's shape.

I thought about the woman at the gate with the iron ring wrapped in cloth. The hand on the wood. The yearly setting-down of the pen at the empty slot, done for longer than the clerk had been alive. The three of them belonged to one story, and the story was not the soft one I had guessed. I had wanted it to be soft. The wanting had been the error, not the reading. The reading had only shown what the basket held, and the basket had held iron from the first.

I stood in the lane a moment. The clay cooled my boots. The lamp-smoke sat on my tongue. The parish had its answer and did not know it. I had the question, and the ring, and the long walk still ahead.

A foundling is forgot. A pledge is kept, and kept away. She had been kept away, lawfully, for longer than the parish remembered why.

That was my first wrong guess, and the cold read had corrected it the way cold reads do. Not with warmth. With a ring, and a wrap, and the quiet fact that the basket had never been for a child.

---

## Six · Before the Fold

The inn at Tindle kept its old men in a pit by the hearth, the way some houses keep a dog. They sat with their pipes and their silence. They had the long practice of men who have nothing left to say to one another, and they had made a peace with it. Old Pettifer was among them. He had the look of a man who had outlived the questions that once troubled him. When I came in with the oilcloth parcel under my arm, he did what the others did not. He looked at the parcel, then at me, then at the parcel again. He let the looking stand as his question. The not-asking was the question, and we both knew it, and the knowing sat between us like the heat off the fire, present and unremarked. The fire cracked low.

I took the bench across from him. A pot of small beer stood between us, thin and sour, and I set the parcel on the board and did not open it. The rate-book was mine to carry, not to display. I kept it closed. A man who has held a silence for forty years will not break it because a stranger sets a book on the table, and I had no wish to be the one who asked him to.

The smoke from his pipe lay in a low band under the beams. The beam above was black with it. It smelled of the river and of old leaf, the same smell the oilcloth had carried into the house that morning. The beer was the colour of weak tea and tasted of the same. I drank and waited. The beer was cold. Waiting was a skill I had come by slowly, and I did not count it as any lesson anyone meant to give. The other men in the pit did not turn their heads. They sat as old men sit. They had learned, each in his own year, to let a stranger be, and the letting-be was its own courtesy.

Pettifer's hands were brown and mapped with work, the nails rimmed with the black that comes from handling rope and ditch-water. He turned the pipe in them the way a man turns a coin he means to spend and then thinks better of. The fire threw his shadow up the wall behind him, large and idle. The inn was warm in the way a small room with many bodies is warm, a warmth that sat on the skin and asked nothing.

"You are the Collow woman," he said at last. "The one who reads."

"I am the one who was sent your old rate-book. I have come to return it."

"A book is not sent. A book is lost, or kept, or taken. Sent is what we say when we mean taken and dare not say taken."

He was sharper than the clerk who had given me the orphan theory and the ring. Pettifer gave no comfort. The clerk had spoken of a foundling left at the gate, a mercy of the parish, a name that had simply worn away with the years. Pettifer gave me the word taken, which is the word the margin had denied. The margin said lawful, not taken, and the two are not the same, and he knew the difference and meant me to hear it. The clerk had wanted to be kind. Pettifer wanted only to be straight, and straightness is the rarer gift.

"The fifteenth line," I said. "Scraped to the wood. The gap in the register. You were alive when it was new."

"I was a boy." He set the pipe in his mouth though it had gone out, a habit older than the tobacco. "I remember the woman, not the line. She was not from the parish, not rightly. She came with a man who was not her husband, and she left with nothing, and the child ..."

He stopped there. The pipe had gone out and he busied himself with the tinder, and the busyness was the rest of the sentence. The tinder caught at last. I did not press it.
