Pressing would have been to take from him what the parish had already taken from the woman, and a man who has held a silence for forty years will not break it for a stranger with a rate-book.

"You remember the woman," I said. "That is more than the register does."

"I remember a basket. And a ring. And a man who was not her husband, who left the ring and took the child, or took nothing and left the ring. I was a boy, and the orders were to look away."

The orders were to look away. That is the whole of how a name gets scraped and a gap gets left. Not out of malice. Out of orders, and a boy told to look away, and a clerk who set the pen down each year at the empty slot because looking at it cost less than writing did.

I had touched the book three times now, and read what it would give. The woman at the gate had come back cold, the ring colder still, and the margin colder than either. The warmth I kept expecting, the small human answer that would make the scraping a mercy, had not arrived. The rate stood at one warm for every two cold, and the warm had not yet come. A book keeps its own weather, and this one was a cold season.

He spoke of the basket as if it weighed in his memory still. A willow thing, he said, the weave loose, and in it something that had caught the low light. The ring he remembered by the metal, plain and cheap, the sort a man gives when he means to come back and does not. The boy had been told to look away, and he had looked away, and the looking-away had outlasted the woman, who left with nothing, and the child, who left with the man who was not her husband, or who was left with the ring. Pettifer could not say which, and the not-saying was honest, the honesty of an old man who will not fill a gap with a guess.

The inn was warm and the beer was thin and the oilcloth parcel sat on the table like a thing that had been sent and taken both at once. The parcel sat between us the whole while. I left him to his pipe.

***

The woman who kept the inn came out from behind the settle with a jug in her hand and filled the cup at my elbow without being asked. She did not go away.

"That parcel is tied in parish cord," she said.

The pit went quiet. The quiet was not surprise. It was the quiet of men who had waited for someone to say it.

"The book came to me in it," I said. "It goes back in it."

"The office counts that cord. My mother wound cord for the office, and the lengths were counted out and counted in." She set the jug down on the board. "A stranger does not carry parish cord unless the parish sent for her."

"Then the parish sent for me."

Old Pettifer put out his hand and moved the cup from my elbow to the middle of the board. He did it the way a man moves a piece on a board he has played on all his life.

"She has not asked it yet," he said. "Let her get to it."

"I have asked it. You have not answered."

The woman looked from him to me and back again. "Is it the book with the line in it."

"It is."

"Then it is no business of mine." She took up the jug. "I have poured in this room since I was tall enough to lift the jug. No one has said the line aloud at this table, and no one will. You will get nothing here but the beer."

She went back behind the settle. The door shut behind her. The room made the small sound large.

The fire took a piece of wood and broke it. Sparks went up the chimney and came down as ash on the board. Pettifer brushed the ash off the wood with the side of his hand.

"The parish pays for the beer," he said. "It does not pay for the asking."

"Who pays for the asking?"

"Nobody has." He turned his cup on the board and left it turned. "That is why the line is bare. A bare line costs nothing and asks nothing. A parish will keep a thing bare as long as the keeping is free."

"Somebody kept it bare. A pen does not set itself down."

He stopped turning the cup. The stopping was the first thing I had cost him, and the cost showed in his hand and not in his face. He put his palm over the cup, flat, the way a man covers a dish he means to keep covered.

"Drink your beer," he said. "The room is listening. I have said what I will say in front of it."

I drank the beer. It was thin and sour. The parcel sat on the board between us with the parish cord on it. That cord was the only thing in the room the parish could still count.

***

I walked back to Collow in the dark. The road was empty. The night was deep. The carrier's cart had gone at dusk and the eight miles were shorter than the waiting would have been, so I chose the road. Clay pulled at my heels with each step, the river wind came off the water and found the gap at my collar, and the wind cut. I thought about the woman at the gate, the ring, the child, the man who was not her husband, the orders to look away.

The dark held the shape of the fields without showing them. My boots found the ruts by memory, the same memory a person keeps of a floor she walks in the night. The cold came off the river in sheets. The coat I wore was my mother's, the wool gone shiny at the elbows. I kept it close because the evening had teeth. A dead woman's coat keeps her hands near mine when the questions come that she would have known how to ask.

A woman bound by a pledge, holding another's ring, with a child the parish would not name. The scraping had not been a foundling's mercy. It had been a man's claim, made lawful, and the child had been the part the parish could not bear to write, and the woman had been the part it could not bear to keep. I turned this in my mind the way you turn a key that will not fit, testing each tooth against the lock, and the lock did not give. The lock held. The road ran straight and the stars were few. My breath came white before me and then was gone, the way a want goes when no one asks after it.

Edward was awake when I came in. The fire still burned. He had kept the fire in against the night, though he said nothing of it. He sat by the lamp with the notebook open and a letter he was not writing. The pen lay beside the page, as if he had stopped mid-thought and chosen not to finish it. He looked at the parcel and at my face and at the parcel again, the way Pettifer had, but where Pettifer's look had been a question he would not ask, Edward's was a statement he would not make. The lamp put his face in half-light. He did not smile. The lamp smoked. When I sat he did not move away, and the heat of him came through the wool of his sleeve, close and unspoken.

"You found the child," he said.

"I found that there was one. Pettifer remembers. The parish does not."

"The parish remembers by not writing. That is its kind of memory."

I sat at the table. The lamp was low and the oil in it had begun to thicken the light, so that the corners of the room drew back into themselves. I took a half-sheet of paper from the stack we kept for the accounts, the cheap stuff that drank ink and dried slow, and I set it before me and dipped the pen. The page was white. The nib took the black and held it, a small full mouth of it, and I let the wetness steady before I set the point to the page.

I wrote: What I want.

The words came out straight and plain, the hand steady, the way it is when you set down a thing you have said to yourself a thousand times and never spoken. The ink lay wet in the grooves of the letters, black and final. I lifted the pen to write the next word, and the next word would not come.

I know the shape of what I hold in this life. A husband who reads my empties and does not fill them. A mother who lived eleven months past the day she should have died. An aunt whose book I carried to a board and won. A woman at a gate in Tindle whose name was scraped and whose child was taken and whose ring was a pledge she could not redeem. I knew what I wanted. I wanted the woman's name set down where the scrape had left the wood showing through. I wanted the child's fate written in a hand that did not flinch. I wanted the man who was not her husband to be made to say, in writing, what he had done, and to say it where the parish could not look away.

But the next word on the half-sheet would have been her name, or the child, or him to answer, and each of those was a want I held for someone else. The paper said What I want, and the want on the paper was meant to be my own. My own was the one want I had never been asked to name. The not-being-asked was the reason the pen would not move.

I had been given back because the first life had not been used, and the not-using of it had been that no one asked, at the end, what I wanted. The second life had been the chance to be asked, and the chance had not come. Here was the paper saying What I want, and my hand would not finish it. To finish it would have been to ask myself. I had spent two lives not being asked. I had learned, by marriage and by use, to leave the question open the way Edward left his notebook open, with the answer not yet written. To ask yourself the question no one else has asked is the hardest asking of all.

The pen left the paper. My hand rose from the page and then stopped, the fingers still curved as if around the stem, the wrist locked at the height of the lift. The nib hung a breath above the word want, and the arm would not lower it. My arm would not move. The small muscles of the forearm stayed in their hold, the way a door stays ajar when the hand that opened it has not decided to push through. I held there. The lamp drew down to a gutter and a single drop fell from the nib and struck the paper on the tall stroke of the t, and it spread into a small dark bloom across the letters, a thing the page had not asked for and could not refuse. I set the pen down without writing the rest. The rest stayed empty.

I did not fold the paper. Folding would have been a decision, and I had not decided. The sheet lay open. I left it on the table, the three words and the bloom, and the empty space where the rest of the sentence would not come. Edward did not look at it. He reads empties by not looking, the way he reads most things, and the not-looking was its own kind of respect. He knew better than to look. What he gave, he gave without a name, the way he gives most things.

The hand stayed in the air a moment after the pen was down, the fingers slow to straighten, as if the arm had forgotten it was no longer writing. The bodily shape of an unfinished business is not a tremor and not a pain. It is the hand refusing to close, held open above a page that asks a question no one has put to you, the fingers gone still at the very top of the reach, where the word would have begun. I closed my own hand at last and put it in my lap, and the want sat on the table between us, named and not named, the way a room sits between two people who will not speak of the one thing in it.

The lamp burned low and the fire that Edward had kept in held the chill off the floor. I watched the bloom dry. It went from wet black to flat brown, a coin pressed onto the word want, and the word kept its meaning under it, altered and not altered. Outside the river wind moved the bare branches, and the branches knocked a slow complaint against the wall, and the complaint was the only sound the house allowed.

In the morning the half-sheet was where I had left it. The mark was dry. The bloom had dried to a flat brown mark on the tall stroke of the t. I put the sheet in the drawer with the accounts, not folded, not written, the want named and not named, the way the woman at the gate had been named and not named, scraped and left, lawful and taken, both at once. The drawer closed with the sound of a thing put away that has not been finished, and the finishing of it was a day I could not yet see.

That was the first time I wrote what I wanted and did not finish it. It would not be the last. The fold would come later, when the want had a shape I could keep, when the hand that had risen and stopped could be trusted to come down on a clean line. For now it was only the three words and the bloom. The hand that had stayed in the air above them. The not-asking that had been the whole of the matter. The question left open on the page the way Edward leaves his notebook open, with the answer not yet written and no one yet asked to write it.

---

## Seven · The Parish's Rule

The road to Tindle ran along the river. The thaw came late. The road was long. The river ran brown with the thaw, and the clay at the verge sucked at the wheels until the hired horse leaned into the collar and the whole conveyance grunted forward one slow inch at a time. One inch. Then rest. I had the oilcloth parcel on my lap. The red cord of the Tindle office was still knotted where the clerk tied it. Under the cloth the rate-book sat with its boards warped and its fifteenth line scraped to translucency, the margin set down in a hand that had wanted to be lawful more than it had wanted to be kind. The law had come instead. The kindness had not.

Edward's letter was in my pocket. He had written it the night before, in the small plain hand he kept for the audit office, the hand that set down facts and left the feeling for someone else to supply. It said I was his wife and therefore entitled, under the statute he cited by number, to see any book the office had cause to question, and the cause was the scraping, and the scraping was the question, and the question was the woman at the gate. The letter made a true thing into a weapon. The paper weighed nothing. The words weighed much. I kept it in my pocket and did not take it out, because carrying a lawful letter is its own kind of courage and I had needed the courage for the road.

I expected cooperation. That was the warm part of the day, and it did not last. I expected that a clerk's letter and a husband's name and a scraped line would open the vestry the way a key opens a lock. I expected the men who kept the parish books to show me the gap in the register and tell me who the woman had been. A gap is a hole. Holes get filled. I had come to fill one. I expected to be believed. I expected to be helped. The expecting was the only warm thing in the cart. The clay pulled. The river wind came through the half-open window and took the heat from my knees. The horse breathed in laboured pulls. The cold outlasted the expecting.

The vestry stood behind the church, a low room with a flagged floor and a window too small to let in the light it promised. The wine was not kept there, only the rates, and the rates were kept in a press against the wall where the damp came through and the ink on the oldest sheets had gone the colour of weak tea. I came in with the parcel and the letter. The vestrymen were already at the table, five of them, with their hands flat on the wood as if the wood might rise and leave without them. None of them stood. None of them brought a chair. The stove smoked. I stayed. The room was warm from a stove in the corner, but the warmth did not reach the chair that was not offered, and I stood with my parcel and let the standing be its own answer.

An elder named Hallow spoke for them. Not the Hallow of the rate-book, a younger man of the same croft, with the broad shoulders the family carried and a face that had not yet learned the stillness of men who had answered by not answering for forty years. "The old rate-book is yours, ma'am, as it was sent," he said. "The gap in the register is not a matter the parish discusses, as the clerk told you."

I set the parcel on the table. The red cord made a small bright knot against the dark wood. "I am not here to discuss the gap. I am here to ask who the woman was, at the gate, with the ring."

"That is the same question wearing a different coat."

"Then answer it in this coat. A woman bound by a pledge, with a child the parish would not name. You were a boy when it was new, Mr. Hallow. You remember."

He would not.

He did not remember, or said he did not, and the looking-away came then. The men looked down. The four other men found their hands the most interesting thing in Tindle. That is what men do when the question has been answered by silence for so long that the silence has become the answer and the answer has become the law. The orders to look away had been given to them, or to their fathers, by men who had learned that what is unspoken is unrecorded, and what is unrecorded did not happen, and what did not happen cannot be questioned. The chain of it was the parish's rule, and the rule was lawful, and a lawful rule is the hardest thing to unmake because no one person made it and no one person can pull it down alone.

I let the silence stand. I waited. The dead waited too. A person who reads dead things learns to wait, because the dead do not hurry and the living will fill a silence if you leave it open long enough. The learning had come to me without being given, which is to say it had been earned.

Hallow's father's cousin broke it. A man with the soft hands of a shopkeeper, a man who sold nails and twine and knew the price of everything and the cost of nothing, and he had been quiet through the looking-away, a man who knew the wall better than the men at the table did. "The wall," he said. "The old assessment. Before the fire took the loft, it was on the wall."

He meant the tax assessment, the great sheet the parish pinned in the vestry so every rate-payer could see what they owed and what the parish owed them back, a sheet as long as a man's arm and revised each year by the same hand that kept the book. The loft fire had taken the loft and the roof beams and the smell of stored apples. The vestry wall was plaster. Under the new assessment someone had pinned the old one, yellow with age, the corners curling, thin at the edges from the years the pin had held them. The pin through the top had rusted to the colour of old blood.

I went to it. My hand stopped a hand's width from the paper, the small halt of a person before a thing that will speak, and then I let the hand fall to my side because the speaking was not for my hand yet. The men did not stop me. They had not stopped the cousin from speaking either. The parish's rule was to not discuss, not to not know, and the knowing was written on the wall where everyone had looked at the wound and read only the scar.

The old assessment was a list of expenditures, the parish's outgoings, year by year, set in columns tight as furrows, the ink brown and the paper gone soft at the folds. I ran my finger down the column for the year of the war with France, the year the rate-book's second list had begun, and there, at the bottom, below the bridge and the school and the pump, was a line that had no name attached to it.

*For the woman at the gate, unclaimed.*

The words were in the same fine hurrying hand as the rate-book's second list, the letters leaning as if the writer had been ashamed to be seen at the work, and the writing of it was the admission: the parish had paid, for forty years, for a woman they would not name. My thumb found the line without my meaning it to, and the paper under the pad of my thumb was colder than the air, colder than the stove-warmed room, as if the wall behind it drew the heat out and kept it for itself.

I put my thumb to the line. The assessment was dead, the ink older than the men in the room, older than Hallow, older than the cousin with his soft hands, and the dead part was what I had come to read.

Three seconds.

The loft was cold. The candle low. The loft above the vestry, before the fire. A candle guttering in a dish. The same hand, younger, the wrist not yet gone to fat, writing the line at the bottom of the sheet, and above it the year's true expenditures in a neat row, and below it this one, set apart, *unclaimed*. The word came as a classification, not a confession, the sort a parish gives a road it does not maintain: not owned, not abandoned, only not claimed. The plaster behind the sheet carried the smell of damp and of the candle the writer had burned, a smell of tallow and cold stone. It rose into my nose while the vision came. The ridge of the ink stood under my thumb, a seam of thread pulled tight. The cold of the page travelled up my arm slow and certain until my elbow ached with it. The not-claiming was the whole of the lawfulness. No name. No claim. A person unclaimed is a person the law has not touched, and the law that has not touched a person has not wronged her, and the not-wronging is the surest wrong there is, because it leaves no mark to be contested and no hand to be blamed.

The read ended. My thumb came off the paper. The cold remained. The cold stayed in the joint a moment longer, a small theft the dead had committed and returned. Then it was only plaster and paper and the five men and the one cousin. Hallow's cousin kept his eyes on the floor as though the floor owed him an answer. The parish's rule stood in the room like the fourth wall of it, the wall that holds the other three up.

"You paid her," I said. "Not her name. The woman at the gate. Every year, unclaimed. For forty years."

No one denied it. I had my answer. Denial would have been to discuss, and the rule was not to discuss, and the not-discussing was the discussion, and the silence was the admission, and the silence had been lawful for forty years, which is longer than most admissions are allowed to live. I had come expecting cooperation and found evasion. The evasion was colder than the wall. The cold was the only answer Tindle would give, and the giving of it without words was the cruelest part, because a spoken refusal can be argued with and a silent one cannot be reached.

***

Hallow put both hands on the table and pushed himself up. "The wall has been read," he said. "The wall will be kept."

The cousin with the soft hands went to it. He took the pin between his thumb and forefinger and worked it loose. The sheet lifted at the corner. The paper made a small sound against the plaster, dry as a moth.

I crossed the room and put my palm flat under his hand.

"Stand away," he said.

"The sheet stays where the parish pinned it."

"It goes back in the press. It has been read."

"Then it goes back torn."

He pulled. The old pinhole gave, and a short tear ran down from it, and the corner came away in his fingers. I held the rest down. The tear stopped at my palm.

Hallow said, "Leave it."

The cousin left it. He stepped back and wiped his fingers on his coat, and the wool took a grey mark off the rusted pin.

The boy came in with a scuttle and set it down by the stove. His hands were black to the wrist. He stood with his mouth open.

"Bring the lamp," I said. "High, and to the side."

He brought it. He held it the way a boy holds a thing he has been told not to drop, and the light went up the sheet and found the bottom line.

I read it out loud. "For the woman at the gate, unclaimed."

The words went into the room and stayed there. A man at the table moved his foot on the flags and stopped it.

"Say it again," the cousin said, "and it becomes a thing said in this room."

"It is already a thing written on this wall."

"Writing on a wall is not the same as a man saying it."

"Then I will say it. You may keep your mouth shut. The wall keeps the rest."

The boy laughed, a breath of it, and put his hand over his mouth. Nobody turned to look at him. That was the kindest thing the room did all morning.

I took my hand off the sheet. The corner hung loose. I put the pin back through the old hole, pressed it through the plaster, and turned the point down with the heel of my thumb until it lay flat to the wall. It would not come out again without a knife.

"You have bent parish iron," Hallow said.

"Then it will hold."

I wrapped the parcel and put the cord around it and pulled the knot tight with my teeth. The boy kept his eyes on my hands and did not offer to help, which I took for sense in him.

At the door I turned. "The line stays legible," I said. "If it goes, I will know the hand that did it. I will know the room it was done in."

Nobody answered. The stove ticked. The cousin had his hands behind him now, out of sight, the way a man puts away a tool he has been caught using.

The boy carried the scuttle back out. The door swung and did not shut, and the coal settled in the iron as he went down the path.

***

My thumb kept the cold of the ink long after I lowered my hand. I curled the fingers closed around nothing, the manner of a person closing a hand on a coin that has been taken, and the emptiness in the curl was the woman at the gate, whom they had fed and not named, and the not-naming was the part that would follow me home. It was the unfinished business of a life set down yearly at a line that was not her name, and I would carry the curl in my hand the way I carried the dead woman's coat in winter, as a thing borrowed and not returned. The curl stayed.

The parcel was under my arm. The path was ahead of me. The rate-book was mine. The assessment was on the wall where it had always been. The woman at the gate had been paid for and not named for longer than the men who paid her had been alive. The paying of her was the parish's way of keeping her both fed and gone, which is the cruellest mercy a body of men can devise. To give without giving. To owe without owing. To keep a person alive by the annual setting-down of a pen at a line that is not her name. The book in my hand had not changed. The book in my head had. The change was small. The change was whole.

***

I walked out into the Tindle clay and the river wind. The wind pushed. I pushed back. The wind took the warmth the stove had lent me and gave nothing back. I thought about the difference between a gap and a payment as the clay pulled at my boots. The register had a gap, a hole where a name had been lifted to the wood. The assessment had a payment, a line set apart and called unclaimed. Between them the woman existed, not named, not claimed, but paid for, kept alive by a parish that would not say her, the manner of a person keeping a fire alive by feeding it things that are not wood. The fire is real. The warmth is real. The not-wood is the only lie, and the lie is lawful.

Edward was at the audit office window when I came back, looking out at the bakehouse yard, the notebook closed on his knee. He did not ask what the morning had turned up. He only held the window open for me to pass, one hand upon the frame and a step aside, and the courtesy was the whole of his question. I answered it by setting the parcel on the table, which was the whole of my answer. He said nothing. Neither did I. The two of us stood in the room with the book between us and the woman between us and neither of us spoke her, because he had learned not to ask and I had learned not to tell, and the not-asking and the not-telling were the marriage we had made, lawful and cold and sufficient.

The book had changed. Not the book in my hand, the boards warped, the fifteenth line scraped to translucency, the margin set down in a hand that had wanted to be lawful more than kind. The book in my head. A scraped name and an empty slot had been a mystery, a thing unknown and therefore free to be anything. A payment marked *unclaimed* was a confession written by the hand that had done the scraping. The confession was that they had taken her out and then they had fed her, lawfully, for forty years. The feeding was the part that made the taking forgivable. The forgiving was the part that made it unbreakable, because a wrong admitted can be righted and a wrong fed cannot. That was the parish's rule, and it was older than the men who kept it, and it would outlive them, and the only thing that could unmake it was a name, and the name was the one thing they had been careful, and lawful, never to write.

I had been given a second life because the first had not been used, and I knew the shape of a loss before it came, the quiet the room holds before the door closes. The woman at the gate had not been given a second life. She had been given a payment, and the payment was not the same as a life, and the not-sameness was the thing I would carry in the curl of my hand until I found her name. That was the only question Tindle had left me, and the only one they had been lawful enough never to answer.

---

## Eight · The Turning

The assessment on the vestry wall had given me the payment. The register had given me the gap. The answer to who had paid, and for how long, lay in neither book; it was in the encoding beneath the ink, in the small marks the parish clerks had used to show which croft bore which share.

I borrowed the current ledger of parish expenditures from the clerk. The clerk gave it. He slid it across the desk without lifting his eyes to mine, as a man passes a thing he would rather not be seen handling. The cover was cracked leather, the leaves foxed at the edges, and the iron clasp had rusted to the colour of old blood. The clerk's office smelled of mice and ink and the cold stone of the vestry wall behind him, where the assessment hung in plain sight and explained nothing. He gave me the ledger the way a servant gives a key he is not permitted to use, with a look that asked me to be quick and to be quiet, and I was both. I set the ledger beside the old assessment and the rate-book, and I read the three together the way Edward reads a column that will not add, by looking at the part that is wrong. The wrong part spoke.

The accounting was plain to a trained eye and deaf to a careless one. The vestry assessment was clean, the sort a parish shows to a visitor. The rate-book was old, left in a cupboard until it was forgotten. The ledger was alive, written each year by a hand that would rather not be known. The hand hid. I ran my finger down the assessment's total column, then the rate-book's, then the ledger's, and the three did not meet until the fifteenth line, where the assessment showed a sum paid and the rate-book showed a name gone and the ledger showed a mark that explained the difference. The clerks had done their work in the gap between the books, where no single officer could be blamed and no single household could be thanked. The gap hid them.

The unclaimed line had a mark beneath it. Not a name. A symbol, the kind a clerk uses when the payer changes each year so that no one household carries the whole of an awkward debt. The mark was a circle with a stroke through it, narrow as a coin, a clerk's mark. It appeared, year after year, in the accounts of seven crofts in rotation: Hallow, Pettifer, Quine, and four others whose names I had seen in the rate-book's second list. They were Mott, Ash, Creevey, and the croft the clerks called simply the Lower Field.

Seven households. Forty years. The stroke moved from one to the next, Hallow to Pettifer to Quine and around, so that each bore the woman's upkeep for five or six years and then passed her to the neighbour. I counted it on the page. Forty divided by seven leaves five, with a remainder of five. The remainder was carried as a fraction is carried in a sum that never quite closes. The first five crofts in each cycle took her six years and the last two took five. Then the wheel turned again. The math held. The amounts were small. A half-crown here. A loaf there.

The ledger told me what each had given in its own turn. Hallow paid the bread in the hard months, when the field was frozen and the child's mother had least. Pettifer paid the half-crowns that bought the shoes and the midwife when the time came. Quine paid the firewood, carried on a handcart the boy delivered. Mott paid the salt and the lentils from her garden. Ash paid the mending, the shirts taken in and let out as the child grew. Creevey paid the candle-ends and the rope for the sill. The Lower Field paid the rest, the unspoken difference that no one else would name, and the seven together made a life that no one of them could have made alone, and not one of them wrote down her name.

I had expected a single parish officer, or a fund, or a charity, the cold arrangement that keeps a ledger straight and a conscience quiet. I had not expected seven crofts taking turns, as seven families take turns sitting with a dying woman, except that this woman was not dying and had not been born to any of them and was not, by the register, anyone at all. A dying woman is mourned by her own. This one was kept by strangers who could not name her, and the keeping asked nothing back.

I put my thumb to the ledger at the first year of the mark. The ledger was live, but the entry was old, and the old part was what I read. The leather was warm from the clerk's hands; the ink beneath my thumb was cold, the cold of a thing written before I was born and left for someone like me to find.

Three seconds.

A kitchen, not the vestry. The smell of bread came first, a coarse brown loaf still warm from a hearth I could not see. Then the figure of a woman, widowed by her apron and her hands, the knuckles swollen, the forearms roped from years of lifting what the parish no longer lifted for her. She set the loaf on a sill of dressed stone. The stone was cold under her palm, and the cold came up through my thumb as if the sill were beneath us both. A child came to the door. The child took the loaf without a word. The child was not hers, a stranger, and the taking was quiet, the soft pad of bare feet on a flagged floor. Then the door closed, and the kitchen held only the woman and the empty space where the bread had been. The bread was gone. Her hands were the colour of the dough she had not kept for herself. She wore a shawl pinned at the shoulder with a thorn, because the pin was spent elsewhere. She kept the thorn.

The loaf was not charity. Charity has a giver and a receiver, and this had neither; it had only a sill and a hand and a turning of the year that brought the next croft's loaf. The woman looked at the sill after the child had gone. She looked at her own empty table, the bare boards, the one cup turned down so the mice would not get into it. She did not write it down. To write it down would have been to say the child was fed by the parish; the parish had not fed her, and seven women had, in turn, lawfully, without a name. The woman's mouth moved with the rhythm of counting, and the counting was the sole record she kept, and the record lived in no book. No book held it. When the child had gone she stood at the sill a long moment, not praying, not weeping, only numbering the loaves she would never eat, and the numbering was the only prayer she allowed herself, and it was a prayer with no name in it.

The read ended. I took my thumb off the ledger, and the kitchen went with it, and the smell of bread left the room and the clerk's foxed pages came back.

Seven households, in turn, for forty years, each feeding a child who was not theirs, because the mother at the gate could not and the parish would not. The feeding left no named record. A named record would have been a confession that the parish had failed a woman it had scraped from its book. I closed the ledger. The mark stared up from the leaf, a circle with a stroke, patient as a wheel that has learned not to stop. It did not stop.

I understood the payment now. The gap and the payment were the same mercy, administered by seven hands in rotation. The rotation was the genius of it. No one household bore the weight. No one parish officer could be asked. The woman at the gate was kept alive by a wheel of loaves on sills, unclaimed, unnamed, lawful. The mercy cost almost nothing and asked almost nothing, and the asking it did not ask was the whole of its safety. A name in the book is a thing the parish must answer for. A loaf on a sill with no name is a thing the parish need never see. They never saw.

But I did not understand why. A gap in a register is cheap to leave. A payment, even a small one, is a thing someone must choose, every year, for forty years. The loaf on the sill was a choice made seven times over, renewed each time a croft passed the turn, and the renewal took a kind of faith that no statute requires and no officer signs for. Why had seven crofts chosen, in turn, to keep a woman who was not theirs and a child who was not theirs alive on paper and at the sill? Why had no one, in forty years, let the name vanish and the loaf stop? No one did.

The cheapness of the gap was the puzzle. To leave the fifteenth line bare is to do nothing. To keep a loaf on a sill is to do something, quietly, forever. Seven households had chosen the harder road without a word between them that any book recorded, and the silence of the choosing was the part I could not account for. The silence remained. A wrong left alone costs no one but the wronged. A wrong fed, in turn, by seven hands, costs the seven, and the costing was the proof that they had chosen it.

I turned the why over as a clockmaker turns a broken wheel, looking for the tooth that had failed. There was none. The wheel had not failed; it had been built to turn without a tooth that named the woman, and the building of it was the act of mercy and the act of concealment at once. Old Mrs. Pettifer, a girl when the woman came, had seen the scraping and had answered it with bread, and the bread had outlived the girl and the woman and the reason, and the outliving was the part anyone now tended. The bread remained.

I asked the clerk. He set his pen down, as a man sets down a tool he will not need for the question I had brought him, and the pen rested, and he shook his head.

"Old Mrs. Pettifer began it," he said. "Before she died. She said the woman had been wronged and the parish would not right it, so the parish would feed her, in turn, and the turn would not end while she lived. And when she died, the turn did not end, because she had taught her daughter, and the daughter taught the next, and the seven loaves became the seven crofts, and no one now living knows why we do it, only that we do."

"Old Mrs. Pettifer," I said. "The mother of the man at the inn."

"The same. She was a girl when the woman came. She remembered. The rest of us remember only the loaf."

I wrapped the parcel. The rate-book was mine, the ledger was the clerk's, the assessment was on the wall, and the woman at the gate was kept alive by seven crofts who did not know why, only that old Mrs. Pettifer had said the turning would not end, and it had not, for forty years, and the not-ending was the lone warmth in a mercy that had begun in a wrong and continued in a forgetting. A loaf on a sill with no name attached is the one thing the law cannot forbid and cannot claim, and the not-ending was lawful only by being invisible. Invisible, and safe.

The clasp of the ledger closed under my hand with a faint iron sound. The clerk took it back without a word. I carried the rate-book out in the oilcloth, and the red cord from Tindle bit into my palm as a question bites into the thinking, a thin steady pressure that did not let up. It did not.

***

The inn stood where the road bent. Its door opened straight into the taproom, so that a wet person came in level with the benches. Benches, a trestle, a fire of split wood, coats on pegs and steaming. The room was full. The room was quiet.

The man behind the trestle had a knife in his hand and a loaf under it. He was broad and grey. He did not put the knife down.

"I am told your mother was Mrs. Pettifer."

"She was."

"Then you know what she began."

He cut. The knife went through the crust and rocked on the board. He set the slice on a plate and pushed it along the trestle to where I stood.

"Eat," he said.

"I have come about the loaf on the sill."

"The taproom is for drinking."

A man at the bench said, "The clerk has been talking." Another said, "Let the woman ask." The innkeeper put his hand flat over the nearest man's tankard. The man drank. That was the end of it.

I put the parcel on the trestle. I untied the cord. I opened the rate-book at the back and turned it so the fifteenth line faced him. The paper showed the grain of the wood through it. The drinkers leaned in. Nobody spoke. He did not look down.

"It says lawful," I said. "Your mother did not think it was."

"My mother is dead."

"She set the loaf out before the rest of them. That is what the clerk says."

"He says a good deal for a man with a pen."

"Then say a better thing."

He wiped the knife on the cloth at his waist and laid it down. The fire took a new piece of wood. He let it burn. "She set it out," he said. "She never said why. I carried it when her knees went. I carried it in rain. I never asked her, and she never told me, and that is the whole of what I know."

"Who carries it now?"

"The house that has the turn."

"Whose turn?"

He took up the loaf and cut a piece from the end. He set it on the sill of the small window by the door, where the glass was thick and the light came through green. Then he put both hands on the trestle. "You are asking the wrong house," he said. "Ask the ones who still carry it."

"Name them."

"No."

The room went quiet again. The men had decided, without a word, not to speak. A bench leg scraped. The wood spat in the grate.

I tied the cord. I wrapped the parcel and put it under my arm.

At the door I stopped. The bread on the sill was cut square at the end where the knife had gone through. The crumb was fresh. The sill was swept. Somebody had swept it for me.

"Your mother taught the daughter," I said.

The innkeeper turned his back. He took up a tankard and began to wipe it. The cloth went round and round inside the tin. It did not stop until I had the door shut behind me.

***

Edward was at the window when I came in. He had lit the lamp where the light would fall on the table, as he does without a word. He had the notebook open, the third book, the one he keeps for what he will not speak, and a line written in it. He added another as I set the parcel down, and he closed it without showing me.

"The seventh," I said.

"The seventh about her. The read of the ledger. You have read the book, the register, the assessment, and the ledger. Four dead things, and the woman is still not named."

"Four dead things, and a turning of seven loaves. She is kept alive by the not-naming. The not-naming is the mercy."

He looked at me as he looks at a column that will not add. He did not disagree, because it did add, in its way, and the adding of it was the cruelest part. A woman could be fed for forty years by seven households who had forgotten why, and the forgetting was what kept her alive. To remember would have been to name her. To name her would have been to ask the parish to account for her, and the parish had accounted for her only by scraping her out. The forgetting was a wall built of small loaves, and the wall kept her fed and kept her hidden, and the two were the same wall, and the wall was the mercy and the prison at once.

I sat with the lamp. The half-sheet with *What I want* was in the drawer, not folded, not finished. I had written the three words and stopped, as I had stopped at the window that would not open and at the door of my mother's house at eighteen, and the stopping was a shape my hands knew before my mind did. My fingers closed on the edge of the drawer, curved around a page I had not written, and they stayed there a moment after I meant to open it, the closing repeated twice, a question held in the hand and not spoken. The drawer held the half-sheet as a wound holds a splinter, small and constant and easy to leave. The hand that withholds learns one motion and uses it twice, and I had learned it in two lives and was learning it again in the writing of three words I could not finish.

The turning of the seven loaves would not end while old Mrs. Pettifer's daughter lived, and the daughter lived, and the not-ending was the last warmth left in a mercy that had begun in a wrong and continued in a forgetting. I would find the wrong. The forgetting I could not undo. But the name, the name I could ask for, and the asking was the whole of what old Mrs. Pettifer had not done, and the whole of what I meant to.

He had not asked what the seventh line said. He never put the question of what the lines said; his care was only that I had read, and the care of that was the lone openness he gave, and it was enough, and it was not enough, and the two were the marriage.

A name scraped from a book is not a person removed. It is a person handed, still warm, from one croft's sill to the next, and the hand that passes her will not be the hand that names her, and that is the longest mercy and the coldest.

---

## Nine · The Mother's Shadow

The clerk had given me the fact the way a person gives a key: plainly, without saying what door it opened. Old Mrs. Pettifer had begun the turning. She was the first, forty years back, to set aside the portion for the line that had no name. The fact sat in my mind like a key in a lock, not turning it, only showing where the turning would be.

Three books lay on the table. The Tindle rate-book, its fifteenth line scraped to translucency. The rotation record, seven households spanning forty years. The wall-list from the parish office, with the empty square where the fifteenth name should have stood. The lamp leaned low over all of them.

I set my hand above the rotation record. It stopped there, an inch above the page, the way a hand stops above water it means to enter. Then I put my thumb to the earliest entry.

The book was dead. It had been an account before it had been a thing, and now it was only a thing, and I am permitted to read dead things. Three seconds.

The cold of the page came up through my thumb first. A winter cold, the cold of a kitchen where the fire had gone down to grey. Then the smell: barley, and a swept hearth, and under it the dry scent of a loaf just out of the tin. The fire had gone down. The bread was still warm. Her daughter slept in the corner. The widow kept her back to the room. The portion was thin. She took the smaller half without a word. A woman's hand moved in the dark of the read. A widow's hand, broad at the knuckle from work. She broke a loaf in two and set the smaller piece on a board, and her breath held the way breath holds when a person does a thing she means no one to see. The sound came last, a knife scraping the tin, a dry drag against the grain. Then only paper again, and the smell of barley fading, and my thumb on the cold line where Mrs. Pettifer had written the first mark in a hand that leaned, ashamed to be seen hurrying.

I had expected a parish order. I had expected the turning to begin with a vote, or a vestry, or some man's name set at the head of the list with the weight of office behind it. The read gave me a widow in a cold kitchen, breaking her own bread. That was the first cold receipt.

***

My mother had done the same kind of thing. Not loaves on a sill for a woman she did not name. My mother mended a shirt for a dead man, a man whose wife could not, because the wife was the dead one, and the shirt needed mending all the same. She walked twelve miles a week for twenty years to sit with a sister who could not thank her. The parish had not fed my mother's sister. My mother had. In turn, alone, for eleven years, and the turning of it ended only when the sister died. The road ran along the river. Ice lined the banks. She walked it in all weather, and the sister never knew her name was on no list. The shirt she mended went to a grave she had never seen.

I wore my mother's coat when I thought of her. The wool had gone shiny at the elbows. The smell of it was cold earth and the river at dusk, the smell of a woman who walked long distances and came home after dark. I had it on now, though the room held no evening cold worth a coat. Her hands were near mine. The questions that came to me about my mother were questions she would have known how to ask, and wearing her hands near mine was the only way I had of asking them. No one had thanked her. The parish had not asked.

The lamp caught the sheen on the elbow. I set my own hand there, over the worn wool, and the shape of her arm was still in it. The wool was heavy with rain. The cuffs were worn to threads. The long muscle from the walking. The flat place at the shoulder where the basket had hung. Those hands had mended. The needle went in straight. The thread was grey. She sang nothing, and spoke to no one, and the shirt was whole by morning. The dead man wore it under the ground.

She wrote nothing down. To write it down would have been to claim a sainthood she would have denied with her last breath. The not-writing kept it clean. Old Mrs. Pettifer told no one, and the not-telling was what made the loaves hers. The pattern was the same in both women. A woman, widowed or burdened, who sees a wrong the parish will not right, and who rights it in the smallest possible way, and tells no one. The not-telling is the whole of the righteousness.

I sat with the lamp and the three books and thought of old Mrs. Pettifer and my mother as two women who had done the same thing in two parishes forty miles apart.

And then I did the thing I had not done before.

I placed the woman at the gate beside them.

The unnamed one. The one the parish had scraped from the rate-book down to the wood. The one they had both, in their ways, kept alive. Mrs. Pettifer with her loaves. My mother with her walking. Each fed a woman neither had named. Each kept a person present in the only way the parish had left open.

A woman is scraped from a book, or fed in turn, or given back a second life, and in each case the hand that does it does not ask her what she wants. That line would have gone in a book of my own, had I kept one, and it was true, and it was the kind of true that admits no argument.

But the placing went deeper than likeness. It went to the place I do not let myself look at, the place where the second life began.

No one asked my mother what she wanted when she took up the mending. The asking would have cost the parish something, and the parish does not pay. No one asked old Mrs. Pettifer, either, when she set the first loaf on the sill. She simply did the one thing the parish had left undone, and the doing was its own permission. And I. I had not been asked what I wanted when I was given back. The giving came from a hand that decided I had not used the first life and meant to see the second used better, and the deciding was done without me in the room.

The not-asking was the shape of both my lives.

I knew that shape well. I died at eighteen. The first life was taken back for want of use. The want of use was that no one asked, at the end, what I wanted. The second life was the chance to be asked, and the chance did not come. I had been given back, and the giving carried the same silence the first taking had carried. No one had put the question to the woman at the gate, either. That was why she stood at the gate with a ring that was not hers to keep and a child the parish would not name.

A person who has been counted out once and counted back in learns a particular patience. She learns not to waste the afternoon on a thing that will not open. She learns to check whether the door gives before she lifts her hand. And she learns, deepest of all, an intolerance. An intolerance of waste, because she has been wasted once and given back. An intolerance of the un-asked, because the not-asking was the very motion of her dying and her returning. I mend the latch at once. I sweep before the dark. The hearth is never left cold. These are small accounts, kept against the waste of a life that was given back.

That intolerance was why the widowed woman's pattern rang in me the way a struck bell rings in a room long after the hand has left it. Mrs. Pettifer kept small accounts because no one had asked whether she would. My mother kept small accounts because no one had asked whether she would. The woman at the gate had been kept by both, and none of the three had been asked what she wanted. The not-asking was the inheritance I had been given back to receive a second time.

I understood the pattern because I was its child. Not in blood. In the bone of having been unmade and remade without a question put to me. The widowed woman broke her loaf in the cold because the parish had left a woman unfed and would not name the debt. My mother walked because the parish had left a sister untended and would not send a body. I read dead books because someone had left a name scraped to the wood and would not say who she had been. Three women. Three parishes. One motion repeated: the hand that should have asked, did not, and the woman made the small account in its place.

The pattern settled in me. I had read it in a dead book, and in a dead coat, and in the silence of my own given-back life. The two women and the unnamed one stood together now, and I stood with them.

I took the half-sheet from the drawer.

*What I want.* The bloom from the dropped nib was still there, a small dark flower on the word *want*, where the ink had welled and spread before I set the pen down. I had not finished it. I had not folded it. I had not burned it. I had only kept it, the way the parish had kept the woman, unnamed, unclaimed, held in the dark.

My hand came to rest over the word. The palm warmed the paper. I let it lie there, flat, the fingers spread. I was not writing. I was covering the place where the sentence had stopped, as if the weight of my hand could hold the unfinished thing in the world without completing it. The warmth went into the page and met the cold that had been there since the lamp leaned. The two stayed against each other, neither winning. The want and the not-want had stayed against each other in me since the day I wrote the first word and could not write the second.

That was the unfinished business of the chapter, and it lived in my hand. The pen lay beside the sheet. The ink had dried to a dull shine. My thumb found the bloom again, and pressed, and did not write.

The mother's shadow fell across the paper. My mother had mended and walked and written nothing. Old Mrs. Pettifer had set loaves and taught her daughter. The woman at the gate had been kept by both, and none of the three had been asked what she wanted, and the not-asking was mercy and cruelty both at once. Mercy, because someone had fed the unwritten. Cruelty, because the feeding stood in for the asking that was owed and never came.

I put the half-sheet away. The want stayed buried. The shadow did not leave.

***

The knock came at the back door, low down, the way a person knocks who is not sure of his welcome. I took the lamp and opened it.

The boy stood in the rain with his shoulders up about his ears. He had no cap. The water ran off his hair and down his face, and he wiped it with his sleeve and made it worse.

"The clerk wants the cord," he said.

"The cord is on the parcel."

"He counts it. He counts what goes out and what comes back."

"Then he should have counted it before he gave it."

"He did. He wants it counted in."

I brought him in and shut the door. He stood on the flags and made a puddle, and then he moved his feet off it and made another.

"Stand still," I said, "and I will find it."

I set the lamp on the table and took up the parcel and worked the knot loose with my thumbnail. The cord came away. The oilcloth fell open, and the rate-book lay with its boards up, warped, and the fifteenth line under the light.

The boy leaned in. He put out a hand.

I caught his wrist. "Wet," I said.

He wiped his hand on his coat and held it out again, palm up, and I turned the book so the back leaf lay flat.

"That is the place," he said.

"That is the place."

"Where the name was?"

"Where the name was?"

He put his finger down beside the line and not on it, careful, the way a boy puts a finger to a thing that is asleep. "It is thin," he said. "You can see the table through it."

"You can."

"Who did that?"

"A man with a knife and a reason."

He took his finger back and put it in his mouth, as if the paper had been hot, and then he was ashamed of that and put his hand behind him.

"Read the margin," I said.

He read it slowly, his lips moving before his voice came. "It is entirely lawful."

"What does it mean?"

"That it was allowed."

"Allowed to take a name."

"Yes."

He thought about it. The rain went on at the window. "My grandfather says a scraped page is a clean page," he said. "He says the clerks are proudest of the clean ones."

"Does he say why?"

"He says a clean page never has to be read again."

I put the cord in his hand. He wound it then, over the little finger and under the thumb. He counted it against his fingers, and the counting satisfied him.

At the door he turned. "Was she anybody?" he said.

"She was somebody."

"Then why is she not in the book?"

"Because a man with a knife wanted a clean page."

He went out into the rain. His boots had left prints on the flags, and the prints were already going, the wet drawing back into the stone. I took the lamp to the door and latched it, and the latch held.

***

The lamp was low. Edward did not stir. His notebook stayed closed. The seventh line waited. The eighth would not. Edward was asleep in the next room. His notebook, the third he had kept since we married, was on the table where he had left it. The seventh line about her was written. The eighth was not. He only writes what I do, and I had not yet done the eighth thing. He leaves the smell of cold earth on the step when he goes out. He sets the latch as if he expects to be called back. He trimmed the lamp before he left. The glass was clean. He writes the things I do without asking why I do them. The not-asking in him is the one kind I have learned to trust. It is a leaving-alone and not a leaving-out.

The eighth thing, I knew, would be to go to the man who had scraped the name, or to his descendant, and ask why. The why was the only part old Mrs. Pettifer had not answered. She had only fed. Feeding is not asking. The not-asking ran in the family. She kept the small account. She did not ask the parish why the account had to be kept by her.

I had been given back to break that trade, or so I had told myself. The second life was the chance to be the one who asks. The one who puts the question the first life never got. But the paper said *What I want*, and the want would not come. The not-wanting was the same not-asking turned inward. I was my mother's daughter in the one way I had not chosen to be. She kept small accounts and wrote nothing. I kept a small account on a half-sheet and could not finish the first line. The shape of the two silences was the same.

The mother's shadow was long. It fell across the gate, and the woman at it, and the ring, and the child, and the loaf, and the half-sheet in the drawer that said a thing I could not finish. I would go to the chandler. That was the eighth. I would carry it. The shadow would go with me, because it always did.

I had expected, when I began, to find a warm thing in the widowed woman's care. A mother's hand. A parish that loved its own. The read gave me the cold instead, twice over. A widow alone in a dark kitchen. A mother alone on a twelve-mile road. Each did the one thing no one had asked her to do, and no one had thanked her for it. The warmth I reached for was not there. The cold was the only thing the books would give me, and the cold was the truth.

The mother's shadow was not a sorrow. It was a ledger, kept in the cold, and I was the page it had been written on twice. The first writing had been scraped to the bone. The second was mine to keep, or to leave blank. I had chosen, without choosing, to keep the blank, and the keeping was its own small account.

I closed the rotation record. The translucency of the fifteenth line in the rate-book caught the lamp one last time, a pale seam in the page, and I did not touch it again. The woman under it had been kept by two strangers and asked by neither. I would be the one to ask. That was the account of the chapter, and it did not yet add. The not-adding was the part that would send me walking. The walking would be the asking. The asking was the only thing I could do for a woman whose name had been lifted to the wood.

The shadow went with me when I went to bed.

---

## Ten · The Eighth, Unwritten

The fire had burned down to a shell. The coals sat under a grey skin, and the heat that reached me came thin and low, the kind that warms the front of a person and leaves the back cold. I had let it go that far on purpose. A high fire asks for attention, and I had none to give it.

The embers showed through the grey skin in three small red eyes. The hearthstone at the fire's edge was warm to the touch but cold a hand's breadth out. The cold crept in under the door from the yard. I had lived through colder rooms. A cold room is only a room, and a room can be survived, and survival is its own kind of warmth if you do not name it. The fire asked nothing of me in its low state. It only burned, and the burning was enough.

I had only the paper. The one word on it had not come true.

The window at my back was shut against the night, the same window that would not open in the spring, and I did not turn to test it. A door that will not give teaches you not to ask it twice. The room smelled of cold stone and the ash that had been the day's fire. My shoulder carried the old ache of a frame pushed and left. I rubbed it once with the hand that was not on the paper. The ache answered, a wound keeping time with a weather it remembers. The night outside was the same night that had taken the woman's name, and the sameness was a thing I had stopped trying to name.

*What I want.*

The bloom under the word *want* had dried the colour of old tea. A small wound the paper had taken and not shown, the way a door takes a blow at the hinge and still closes. I had written the three words a fortnight before and stopped at the third. The ink had done what ink does when no hand returns to it. It sank. It dulled. It closed over the cut without healing it.

I read the three words as you read a door that will not open, by the shape of the closing. The want would not come. It had not come the first time. It had not come in the nights between. It would not come now. The not-coming was not a failure of feeling but a failure of having been asked.

A want you have never been asked to name stays a shape without a word, the way the woman at the gate stayed a gap without a name. I had been given the paper and the space to fill it. No one had put a question to me. A questionless page is only a mirror held up to a person who was never spoken to.

I had been asked few things in my life that mattered. The questions put to me were put by men with pens, and they were questions meant to record me, not to hear me. A person schooled under such asking learns to keep the answer small. Or to keep it silent. I had kept mine silent. The silence had grown a skin; coals close over a fire, and the word beneath had gone quiet without going out.

I had written the words on the night the loaves had spoken to me. The keeping had come clear that night, and the want had not. The pen had stopped at the third word. My hand had hung above the page, not writing, the refusal itself a kind of sentence. I had not folded the sheet then either. The not-folding had been the first of the nine days, though I had not known to count them.

My thumb found the brown bloom. The pad of it settled on the stain. I left it there, the habit of a tongue against a chipped tooth that will not stay away. That was the unfinished thing made bodily. Not a hand raised to fold. Not a hand raised to burn. Only a thumb on the place where the word had been asked of no one and answered by no one.

The paper was cold. The fire stood four feet off and gave nothing to it. I pressed a little, hoping pressure alone might bring the word up. Of course it did not. The not-coming stayed.

The house was quiet. The fire made the only sound, a low papery breath in the coals. I kept my hand on the table, open, the thumb still on the bloom. The quiet held the room, a lid on a box no one had opened. Nothing in me moved toward the fold. Nothing moved toward the flame. The want stayed a shape without a word, and the shape was the only company the paper had.

I set the half-sheet down. I did not fold it. Folding would have been to keep it, and I was not yet sure I wanted to keep a thing I could not finish. But I did not burn it either. Burning would have been a verdict, and I had no verdict to give. So it lay on the table, the three words and the bloom neither kept nor destroyed, as the parish had left the woman, neither named nor gone, set in the margin with a lawful word beside her and the wood showing through where she had been.

The lawful word stayed in my memory. Small. Set there by someone else. Impossible to forget while I walked.

The room held the warmth of spent coals. The half-sheet did not. One pan on the stove stays hot. The other goes cold. I knew which pan I was.

***

Edward came in from the cold. He had walked the bounds again, and his boots were wet through. He crossed first to the hearth and set a split log on the embers without a word. The fire leaned up to take it. The room brightened by the degree he had known I would want before I had thought to ask for it.

Then he set his boots by the fire to dry. The steam came up off the leather in a thin curl that smelled of river and thaw. He hung his coat on the peg without shaking it out. His mind was still on the line he had been walking. He sat with the notebook on his knee. He opened it to the seventh line and read it back to himself. He did not write the eighth.

"You have not asked the eighth," I said.

"I write what you do. You have not done the eighth."

"That is not the same as asking."

"I know it is not the same. The not-same is the point." He closed the notebook on his thumb and set it on the table. "When you go to the chandler, that will be the eighth. I will write it then."

"How do you know I mean to go to the chandler?"

"You mean to ask why. The why is not in the books. The why is in the hand that held the knife, or the hand that taught the daughter to set the loaf. You have ruled out the daughter. That leaves the hand."

He was right, as he usually was. He stated it without weight, the manner of a man calling the weather. The rightness of it was the part I could not argue. Only endure. I had meant to go to the chandler. The seven loaves had told me the what and the who of the keeping, the seven households that had paid across the years for a name no one would speak. The scraping had told me the how of the taking. The knife went under the ink and lifted the letters from the page so the paper forgot it had carried them.

I had read the first of the seven loaves cold, and the cold had stayed with me past the telling. The seven had agreed with one voice. Someone had meant her to remain, and the meaning had outlasted the hand that scraped her out. A keeping done in silence is still a keeping, and the parish had kept her the only way it could, by paying for a name no one would speak. The coins had come from seven houses, and the seven had not spoken of it to one another, and the not-speaking had been the seal on the keeping.

The why was the only gap left. The why lived in the hand that had held the knife, or in the son or grandson of that hand. A hand that learns one motion uses it on whatever it means to erase. The woman at the gate had been erased by a hand that knew only the one stroke.

"I will go," I said.

"To Tindle, to the house of the man who took her name."

"To the house. Not to accuse. To ask."

He looked at the half-sheet on the table. He looked at me. He did not look at the half-sheet again. He reads empties by not looking, and the half-sheet was the emptiest thing in the room. "You will not fold it before you go."

"I have not decided to keep it."

"The not-deciding is the keeping. A thing you have not burned is a thing you mean to come back to. I have counted your not-burning it for nine days."

Nine days. I had not counted them. He had, the fashion of a man who counts the lines in his notebook without being told to. The half-sheet had lain in the drawer for nine days. Taken out twice. Never folded. Never burned. The not-burning was the decision, and he had read it without my saying, as he reads most things I have not said. A closed book tells him what an open one tells other men. He had learned me as a clerk learns a column, by the shape of the blanks.

"I will come back to it," I said.

"You always do. The question is whether you will finish it."

That was the eighth thing he did not ask. He was right that the not-asking was the point. If he had asked what I wanted, I would have had to answer. The answering would have been to write the rest of the half-sheet. The writing would have been to admit that the want was the one thing I had been spared the naming of.

He knew the question. He had known it since the night I wrote the first word and stopped. He had not asked it then. He did not ask it now. The not-asking was its own kind of care, the care of a man who will not put a question to a person who has never been asked one kindly. He waited. The waiting was its own kind of reading, the kind done with the book closed.

He had learned, my husband, to read a page by the lines it would not show him, and he read me in the same fashion. The eighth question sat in the room between us. Written in no book. Asked by no tongue. He let it sit. A man who will not ask you what you want is sometimes the only man who has understood you were never asked. The noticing was the gift. The not-asking was the form it took, and I could not hate the form for being the shape of a mercy I had not been given before.

***

Edward fetched the chisel from the box under the stair and knelt at the window with his coat still on. It was the same chisel he had used on the latch plate in the autumn, and the screw he had set then had held through the winter and had now given up, quietly, the way a thing gives up that was never asked to hold much. The paint had gone hard in the seam, and he set the blade into the join and struck the butt with the heel of his hand, and a curl of paint came away and lay on the boards, the way a scab lies when it comes off whole. He struck the butt again. The blade went deeper. The sash lifted and stopped, and the frame held it the way a jaw holds a word.

"Hold the candle nearer."

I held the candle nearer. The wax ran over my thumb and set there, and the heat of it was a clean pain I could put my attention on while he worked the chisel along the seam, and the paint fell in flakes. His knuckle caught the frame. The blood came up slow. He did not stop for it.

"The wood has swelled," he said. "It will want the frame taken out and planed, and the frame is older than the house."

"Leave it."

He sat back on his heels and wiped the chisel and set it down. The cold came in through the gap and leaned on the fire, and the fire leaned away and came back.

"You could ask me to take the frame out," he said. "It would cost more than the window is worth."

"I know what it would cost."

"The asking is free."

"The asking is the part that costs."

I set the candle on the sill and put my palm against the sash and pushed, and the sash went up under my hand and came down again the moment I let it, and the coming down was the same answer the frame had given in the autumn. Edward did not say anything. He closed the chisel and put it back in the box.

"It is not the frame," I said. "The frame was made to shut, and it shuts."

"Then it is the hand," he said, and he said it the way he read a total, without weight. "A thing made to shut will shut. A thing made to open waits for a hand that means the opening."

He went to the table and opened the notebook and wrote a line and read it back, and the nib scratched and stopped, and he did not write the next. I stayed at the window with my palm on the sash, and the cold was honest and asked nothing.

The eighth line stayed unwritten. The window stood open a little, which is not open and not shut, and the difference between the two was where the night had gone.

***

The fire shell cracked and sent a last tongue of light up the chimney. The room was warm. The half-sheet was cold. The woman at the gate was colder. The decision to go to the chandler was the only warm thing, because it was an action, and an action is the one thing a want can become without being named.

I could not say the want. I had proven that across nine days and two readings of the page. The proving was its own kind of answer, the answer that says a thing by leaving it unspoken. But I could walk to Tindle. The walking would carry the question I could not speak, as a letter carries the hand that wrote it though the hand is not in the room.

I wrapped the rate-book in its oilcloth. The red cord of the Tindle parish office went twice around it. The knot sat where my thumb had rested on the scraped line a fortnight before, as if the book remembered being read and had folded itself to the shape of my hand. The parish of Tindle would see it return. The heir of the man who wrote the margin would see me carry it. The carrying would be the asking, old Mrs. Pettifer setting her loaf on the sill to ask, my mother taking up her mending to ask. A person asks without words when no one has taught her the words. The asking goes out in the thing carried, not in the thing said, and the thing carried in my hands was a book that had already been asked of once and answered by a lawful margin.

The oilcloth smelled of the river still, the same smell the book had carried from Tindle. The red cord bit into my palm as a bridle bites a careful horse. The weight of the wrapped book was honest in my arms, a weight that said where it meant to go and asked nothing of me but the walking.

The mother's shadow fell across the room. It fell across the gate. It fell across the half-sheet. I let it, because it was the only inheritance I had that did not need a name. The not-naming of it was the one mercy the parish had not taken from me. She had mended and asked without speaking. I had read dead things and asked without speaking. The two motions were the same motion, a hand set to a thing that someone else had broken and left. Her shadow did not accuse. It only stayed, a kept coat on the back of a chair after the wearer has gone, present by the simple fact of not being put away.

She had not asked me what I wanted, my mother. She had asked the loaf, the mending, the door left on the latch for a neighbour. I had learned the same asking from dead things, from the scraped line and the seven loaves and the lawful margin. Neither of us had ever been asked in return. The not-asking runs in a house, a floor under the chairs, unseen and bearing the weight. I would not ask the heir to name her. I would ask him only why.

The way to Tindle ran with the river the whole of its length. The river ran cold this time of year, and the cold would be in my face before I reached the heir's door. I did not mind the cold. A cold road is an honest road. It tells you it is long and does not pretend to be short. The parish had pretended the woman was not there. The pretending had been the warmer lie and the crueler one. I would trade the warm lie for the cold road any night of the week.

The eighth was the heir, and I would go to him. The road to Tindle was long and the hour was late, but the going was already begun in the wrapping of the book and the not-folding of the paper. I would carry the rate-book back to the parish that had sent it. I would stand at the heir's door and ask the why that the books could not hold. The answer might be cold. The asking would be warm enough.

The fire gave its last light and went to ember. The room did not go dark so much as it went honest, a face without the flattering lamp. I sat with the half-sheet and the wrapped book and the decision, and the three of them made a small company in the cooling room.

Edward would not write the eighth. My going would. The going would be the answer to a question no one had asked me, which was the only kind of answer I had ever been given, in either life.

---

## Eleven · The Writer's Book

The writer's heir kept a shop at the Tindle end of the bridge, a chandler's, with the smell of tallow and the sour edge of rush that has not dried. The shop was low and crowded and honest in the way a shop is honest, which is to say it sold what it had and no more, and the shelves held the cheap goods of a poor parish: candles by the dozen, twine by the hank, soap cut from a block and sold by the ounce. He was an old man, older than Pettifer, with the same thin wrists as the young clerk but the veins standing blue under the skin, and he looked at the oilcloth parcel and then at me and then at the parcel, the way every man in Tindle had, as if the book were a wound they had all agreed not to name.

"I am the widow Rooke of Collow," I said. "The audit office sent your old rate-book to my husband. I have brought the parish book back, and I have a question about the fifteenth line."

"You are the one who reads."

"I am the one who was sent the book. The rest is talk."

He did not smile, but something in the set of his mouth shifted, the way a door shifts when the latch is lifted but not yet opened. "Talk is what a parish does instead of a thing," he said. "Sit. I will not be long with the candles."

He finished with the customer at the counter, an old woman buying a penny candle and telling him, while she bought it, a long story about a hen that had stopped laying. He listened to the whole of it. He did not hurry her. When she had gone he turned the sign on the door to the side that meant closed, and he took me into the back, where the accounts were kept, and he opened a drawer and took from it a small book bound in stained leather, not a parish book but a private one, the kind a man keeps of things he does not wish the parish to see.

"My grandfather kept this," he said. "He was the clerk who held the pen. Not the one who scraped. The one who wrote the rest."

The scraper's heir, I had called him in my head the whole way across the bridge. He was the writer's heir. I had come for the hand that held the knife and the door had opened on the hand that held the pen, and of the two hands it was the second that had decided what the first one meant. A parish that wants a thing forgotten has no great fear of the knife. It fears the clerk who writes down why.

"The rest being the line in the margin."

"Aye. *It is entirely lawful.* He wrote that, and he wrote why, and he told my father, and my father told me, and I have told no one else, because the telling is the only part that is not lawful, and the not-lawful is the part a man keeps in a drawer."

He set the book before me. It was smaller than the rate-book, and the leather had gone soft with handling, and a corner of the cover had been chewed by something that had lived in the drawer before the book did. The oilcloth parcel sat beside it, the parish's scraped line and the private book, the two halves of the same mercy, one lawful and shown, one lawful and hidden.

"I will read it," I said. "The book is dead."

"The book is dead. What is in it is not. Read it, and tell me whether my grandfather was a cruel man or a careful one, because I have not been able to decide in seventy years."

I put my thumb to the private book. The old chandler did not watch. He turned to the window, the way Edward turns from an empty space, and the not-watching was the same mercy, given by a man who had inherited a silence and not broken it.
