The window looked out on the bridge, and the bridge was grey with the thaw, and a cart went over it with a sound like a long breath let out, and then the shop was quiet, and the tallow smell was thick and warm, and under it the cold smell of the river coming up through the floor.

Three seconds.

A room with a candle, the year of the war. The same desk, the same cross-bar window, the T-shaped shadow. A younger man, the scraper, not the writer, with the knife, going under the letters, lifting the name. The knife made a dry sound against the grain, the sound I had come to know, the sound of a name being taken out from under. Then the writer, the chandler's grandfather, taking the pen, and the pen was heavier than the knife had been, and his hand was not steady, and the words came up in the air in front of me: *It is entirely lawful*, and beneath it, smaller, in a hand that had pressed too hard and torn the paper at the tail of the last word: *she was claimed by a man not her husband, and the claim was entered, and the entry was struck by order, and the striking was by the law of settlement.*

The read held a smell I had not expected, not the wax and damp of the vestry but tallow, the same tallow as the shop, as if the book had carried the shop's smell back into the room where it was written. And a sound under the scratching: a child, somewhere beyond the desk, a child making the small fretful noise a child makes when it has been left too long in a room not its own. Then the three seconds ended, and it was only paper again, and the cold leather under my thumb, and the chandler's back at the window, and the bridge beyond him.

I took my thumb off the book.

The chandler turned from the window. "Well?"

"Your grandfather was a careful man who wrote the truth as the law gave it to him. The scraping was done by another. Your grandfather only set down why."

"Why, though? Why scrape a name?"

"Because she was claimed by a man not her husband. The claim was entered in the register. The entry was struck by order. The striking was lawful, under the law of settlement, which says a woman settled elsewhere is not a parish's to keep."

He nodded, as if this confirmed what he had suspected and dreaded both at once. "She was not of Tindle. She was of somewhere else, claimed, and the claim took her out of the book."

"Took her out, and left her. The parish fed her in turn, unclaimed, because to send her back to the claim would have been to send her to the man who was not her husband, and the seven loaves were the parish's way of not doing that, lawfully, without a name."

The chandler closed the private book. "My grandfather said the law is a knife that cuts clean. He said a clean cut is kinder than a ragged one. I never believed him. A clean cut still cuts."

He said it without heat, the way a man says a thing he has said to himself many times and never once out loud, and the saying of it seemed to cost him something in the chest, because he put his hand there a moment, flat, as if to hold the words in.

A clean cut still cuts. I turned that over. The parish had cut her clean. No crossing-out, no ragged strike that left the name legible and accusing. A clean cut, a lawful cut, a cut that left the page smooth so that the next reader would not know a name had ever been there. And the smoothness was the mercy the law claimed for itself, that it did not leave a wound to be seen, only a thin place to be felt by a thumb. A ragged cut would have left her name for anyone to read and pity. A clean cut left her a blank, and the blank was the law's idea of kindness, and the kindness was the cold part.

The book had changed. Not the book in my hand, the book in my head. A scraped name had been a mystery, then a confession, then a mercy. Now it was a lawful cutting, done by a man who had claimed a woman not his, under a law that said the parish need not keep her, and the parish had answered the cutting with seven loaves, and the loaves were the only kindness in a thing done entirely by rule. Each time I learned a piece of it the shape of the thing shifted and did not shift, the way a face in bad light shifts and is the same face. The mystery had been easier. A mystery leaves room for a villain. A lawful cutting leaves room only for a procedure, and a procedure has no face to hate and no hand to stay.

***

The knock came while the sign was turned to closed. The chandler went through and lifted the latch, and a boy came in backwards with a barrow, and the wheel had picked up clay in the lane and set it down in a line across the floor.

"Rush and tallow," the boy said. "The carrier says to sign it and I am to go on."

The chandler took the tally stick from its hook. The stick was hazel. The notches ran along one face in a row, and the row was the shop's memory of what had come in and what had gone out. He counted the bundles with a finger and not with the mouth. Then the finger stopped.

"This bundle took the wet."

"It came off the cart at the ford."

"The ford is not my shop."

"The wet is the river's, not mine."

"The river does not pay the carrier," the chandler said, "and I do not pay for water."

The boy said nothing. The clay dried grey on his knuckles. The boy wiped his palms. The chandler set the blade to the stick. The blade went in. The shaving curled off. The notch came out pale.

"Sign it short and I am blamed for the short," the boy said.

"I will sign what came."

"Then sign the wet one and let the carrier argue it."

He cut it shallow. He turned the stick. He shaved the notch level, and the wood went thin under his thumb, and he held it up to the window. The light came through. It came through the place where the notch had been, and the place was a pale seam in the hazel, and a thumb would find it and an eye would not.

"Can it be planed out?" I said.

"It can be planed out," he said. "And then the stick is a new stick and the tally is not the tally, and the carrier will ask after his loads, and I will have a smooth stick and no answer."

He put the stick in my hand. He counted the keg. My thumb found the thin place without being sent. I held the stick steady. The keg was tallow, and the tallow had taken a skin, and the skin had cracked across. He set the keg down. He cut the last notch and hung the stick on its hook, and the stick knocked the wall and was still.

"Every account in this shop is kept in notches," he said. "Ink can be argued with. A notch cannot."

The boy took the barrow and went out. The door came to behind him. The shop was quiet again. The chandler took the besom. He swept the clay into the gutter, and the line went, and the boards were boards again. He put the besom back.

"A shopkeeper sweeps," he said. "It is the first thing he learns and the last thing he gives up."

***

I wrapped the rate-book. The private book went back in the drawer, because it was the chandler's, and a private book is not mine to carry, even when it holds the why. He watched me wrap the parish book, and his eyes were the eyes of a man who had kept a drawer shut for seventy years and had now opened it once and would shut it again, and would be glad of the shutting.

"You will tell your husband," he said.

"I will."

"And he will write it."

"He writes everything. It is the only way he has of holding a thing without breaking it."

The chandler nodded, and something in his face loosened, the way a knot loosens when the strain comes off it. "Then it will be written somewhere that my grandfather was not a cruel man. I have wanted that written. Not for him. For me. A man should not have to carry a cruel grandfather if the grandfather was only careful."

"Careful is not the same as kind," I said.

"No," he said. "It is not. But it is the only thing the law asks of a man, and my grandfather gave the law what it asked, and he gave the woman the margin, and the margin was the one place he could tell the truth without breaking the law, and he told it there, and he left it for a reader like you."

Edward would write the eighth when I told him. The writer's heir had given me the why, and the why was the law of settlement, and the law was the cruellest mercy of all, because it cut clean and called the cutting kind.

I walked back over the bridge with the clay on my heels and the wind off the river, and I thought about the clean cut and the seven loaves and the woman at the gate with the ring that was not hers to keep. The bridge was old and the stones were worn in the middle where four hundred years of feet had gone over, and I put my own feet in the worn place, and the worn place held them, and the holding was a small comfort I had not asked for. I knew that the why was the law, and the law was lawful, and the lawfulness was the whole of why no one had ever asked her what she wanted, because a person cut clean by law is not a person the law must ask.

The wind came up the river and took the warmth off my face, and I let it. There was a cold in the day that no coat would keep out, and the cold was not the weather. It was the shape of a thing done entirely by rule, and the rule had no face, and a thing with no face cannot be asked to explain itself, and cannot be forgiven, and cannot be made to write a name back into a book. The margin had been the one place the truth could live, and the truth had lived there in a hand that pressed too hard, and the pressing was the only anger in the whole of it, and the anger had been the grandfather's, and he had put it into the pressure of the pen and not into the words, because the words had to stay lawful, and the pressure did not.

At the end of the bridge I stopped and turned and looked back at Tindle. The town sat low against the water, the roofs grey and the church tower grey above them, and the smoke went up straight from the chimneys because the wind had dropped, and the straightness of the smoke was the only honest thing in the view, because smoke goes up whether a town is kind or lawful. I thought about the child. The read had given me a child's fretful noise beyond the desk, and the chandler had not mentioned a child, and Pettifer had said a child had been taken, and the two together made a shape I could not fill. A child taken by a man who was not the husband. A child left with the ring. The parish had struck the woman and kept the ring's claim and lost the child somewhere between the striking and the feeding, and no book had thought the child worth a line.

I put my hand in my pocket and touched the slip of paper with the letter on it. The paper was soft from folding, and the softness was the softness of a thing handled too often, and I had handled it too often in two days, and the handling was the itch, and the itch was the doubt, and the doubt was the law's best work. A person who doubts the name will stop asking, and a person who stops asking is a person the law does not have to answer. I took my hand out of my pocket and did not touch the paper again, and the not-touching was a small discipline, the kind a person learns when she has been given a second life and cannot afford to spend it on an itch.

The ring. Pettifer had remembered the ring by the metal, plain and cheap, the sort a man gives when he means to come back and does not. A pledge, the chandler had called it. A claim entered. The law had a word for a ring given and not kept, and the word was not theft, because a ring given is given, and a claim entered is a claim, and the law does not ask whether the woman wanted the ring or the claim or the man. The law asks only whether the entry was made, and the entry had been made, and the making of it had taken her out of the book as cleanly as a knife takes a name out of a page. The ring was the knife's handle. The man had held it, and the parish had held the law, and the woman had held nothing but a basket with something wrapped in cloth, and the cloth had been the only soft thing in the whole of it.

I walked on. The road went up from the bridge and the town fell behind me, and the clay was dry now and the walking was easy, and the easiness was its own kind of cruelty, because a road that lets you walk away from a lawful cutting is a road that has made its peace with the cutting. I had not made my peace. I had a slip of paper and a thread and a name with the middle taken out, and the middle was the part I would have to find, and the finding would take me back across the bridge and into the book before this one and into the letter I had not answered, and the going back was the thing I had been avoiding since the third letter came, and the avoiding had been a kind of keeping, and the keeping was over now. The fold in the drawer at home was waiting for me to come back to it, and the thread in my pocket was waiting to be pulled, and the two waitings were the shape of the spring, and the spring had begun.

---

## Twelve · The Original Line

I came to Tindle expecting a crime.

A scraped name in a parish book. A gap where a register entry ought to stand. A woman fed in turn by seven crofts and never named among them. These are the marks of a thing done in the dark, and I had come to set a light to the dark the way I had set one to my aunt's book and my mother's account, certain that what had been hidden would show its shape under looking. The road into Tindle ran along the river and the river was the colour of a peeled willow branch, pale and cold, and the certainty rode with me the whole way, a small hot thing in my coat pocket, the certainty of a wrong that only needed naming.

The chandler's shop front held rows of dipped candles. Pale tallow stood beside the darker beeswax, arranged by height the way a choir is arranged by voice. The smell of rendered fat came through the curtain into the back room. The shop was quiet. Dust hung in the light from the one small window. The room was cold. The candle on the shelf had burned to a nub, and the chandler's silence was its own kind of testimony, the kind a grandson gives a grandfather he has decided not to judge. He had grown up with this book in the house and had never opened it to a stranger, and the not-opening had been a habit, and the habit had been a loyalty, and the loyalty was to a man who wrote a lawful thing and called it comfort, and the calling of it comfort was the part the grandson could not repeat without a tightness at the mouth.

The chandler set a private book on the deal table and left it within my reach. He did not sit. He stood at the window with his back to me, the way a man stands who has decided the room is mine to search and his part in it finished. His hands were the hands of a man who dips wax for a living, red at the knuckles, calm. He gave me the book and then gave me the room, and the giving of both was the most he would offer, because some grandchildren inherit a trade and some inherit a silence, and he had come into both.

Two books lay between us. One was the parish rate-book, its fifteenth line scraped to the wood. The other was the private book his grandfather had kept, and the private book was the thing that undid my certainty before I had finished turning its pages.

The book was dead, a record before it was an object, and the dead are the only things I am permitted to read. I put my thumb to the corner of a leaf and let the grandfather's hand come up through the paper, the fine even strokes, the careful slope, the man who wrote for the parish and wrote for himself in the same patient hand.

The dark, it turned out, had never been dark.

The scraping had been written down. The chandler's grandfather set the act in his own hand, with the reason placed beside the bare place in the same fine script he used for everything he meant to stand: *It is entirely lawful.* The entry did not conceal a wrong. It recorded a right, done by rule, and the rule had a number and a date and the weight of a page that had been consulted before the knife went in. He had written the words without flinching, the way a man writes a receipt, and the plainness of the hand was the part that sat heaviest in the room. The hand did not shake. A cover-up hides. This had been set down to be found. The date was the ninth of November in the year of the war with France, and the clause he cited ran three lines beneath the scraped name, copied out in full so that no later reader could claim the page had been misread.

I had guessed wrong a second time.

The first wrong guess had been the foundling, the thing I took for an orphan's neglect. The second was the crime. There was no crime. There was a law, and the law had cut clean, and the clean cut had left a woman at a gate with a ring that was not hers to keep and a child the parish would not name. A man with a blade had not stolen her. A clerk with a statute had. The difference was the whole of the room, and the room was colder for it. I had built the whole journey on the shape of a hidden deed, and the shape was wrong, and the wrongness of it was its own kind of chill, the chill of a person who has packed for rain and walked into snow.

I sat with both books open. The rate-book's thinned line ran like a pale seam under my thumb, and I set my thumb to it again, because the first read had promised a woman and returned a wound, and I wanted to know whether the wound had a face. The page was cold. The seam was cold. The question under my thumb was colder still. The knife waited.

Three seconds.

The desk came up first. The cross-bar window behind it threw a shadow shaped like a T across the page. A man leaned with a knife, lifting the letters from the paper the way you lift moss from a stone, patient, thorough, unhurried by guilt. The blade edge carried the taste of rust into my mouth, a dry iron flavour that belonged to the knife and to nothing alive, and the cold of the page pressed up through the pad of my thumb the way a cellar door presses cold into a bare foot. I had gone in hoping for a face to pity or to blame, and the page gave none. In place of the woman at the gate, the law stood, made visible: a leaf of the statute, the law of settlement, the clause that said a woman settled elsewhere is not a parish's to keep, written out in the same fine hand as the rate-book's second list, and the hand was the chandler's grandfather's, and the writing of it was the kindest and cruellest thing in the room, because a law set on a page is a face you cannot argue with. The statute leaf smelled of nothing. The knife tasted of rust. The only warmth anywhere in the three seconds was the memory of a woman the law had already put outside, and she was not in this read at all. The law filled the silence.

The read ended. I took my thumb from the line.

There had been no face. Only the law, and the law has no face, and the having of no face is what makes it lawful, and the lawful is what makes the wound untreatable, because you cannot bind a cut that was made by a thing with no hand to hold to account. I had expected warmth from the read, a person, a name, something to lay a feeling against. The read had given me the law, and the law is cold by its nature, and the cold of it was the third cold read of the book. The count I keep, one warm for every two cold, held: the woman had been warm, the ring had been cold, the law was colder still, and only the woman had carried any heat, and she had been the first of the three and the last. The ratio was the only honest thing the book had offered me, a small arithmetic of disappointment that added the same way every time. The book kept nothing else.

The chandler kept his eyes on my thumb as it came away from the paper.

"You saw the law."

"I saw the page. The law has no face."

"That is what my grandfather said. A lawful thing is a thing no man need answer for. He said it like a comfort. I never found it comforting."

Neither had I. The page offered no comfort. A thing no man need answer for is a thing that leaves a woman at a gate with a ring and a child and no name, and the not-answering is the whole of the mercy in it, because if a man had answered he might have been made to feel, and feeling is the one thing the law is built to prevent. A crime leaves a throat to seize and a name to call to account. The law leaves a clean edge and a closed door, and behind the door the woman stands with her hand on the wood, and no one who put her there is required to meet her eye. Malice can be named, and fought, and undone. Lawfulness only sits, settled, and sleeps. The villain at least knows he has done a thing. The clerk has done nothing he can be made to own. The law owns it.

I packed the rate-book back into its oilcloth. The private book stayed in the chandler's drawer, because it was his and because the why of it was not mine to carry, only the question. The question sat plain as a stone: a woman claimed by a man not her husband, settled elsewhere by the settlement clause, struck from the register by a law that asked no one's leave, and then fed in turn by seven crofts who had forgotten why they fed her. That was the whole of it, and the whole of it was lawful, and the lawfulness was the wound with no face, the cut that no bandage and no pity could reach because there was no hand that had made it to be sorry. The ring on her hand was a dead man's, or a living man's who was not her husband, and the child at her skirt was a parish charge the moment the clause was read, and the clause was read by a man who then went home to his supper, and the supper was lawful too.

Edward would write the eighth when I told him the why. The why was the law. The law was the cruellest mercy, cutting clean, asking nothing, answering nothing, leaving a woman at a gate with a ring that was not hers and a child the parish would not name. He keeps the entries without comment, and the keeping of them is the nearest he comes to saying he believes me, and I have learned not to ask him to come closer than that. The notebook is his act, and the act is its own plain language, set down each time in the same hand, the way the grandfather set down the law.

***

The lane from the shop to the bridge foot ran narrow between a wall and a ditch. The cart had stopped in it with the near wheel down and the clay holding it. The thaw had gone into the ground, and the horse stood with its head low and its legs braced, and the carter had his hands on the spokes and his boots sunk in the wet.

"Off with the load," he said. "She will not pull it out of that."

The boy came up behind me with the barrow and set the shafts down. They took the sacks off together, each to a shoulder, and stacked them against the wall, and the stacking was careful because the wall was wet and the sacks were the parish's. The cart came up as the weight came off. The wheel showed its rim.

"Now she pulls," the carter said, "if the wheel will hold."

It would not hold. The horse leaned into the collar, and the wheel went round in the clay and came up greasy, and the cart sat down in the same place, and the carter said a word under his breath and put his shoulder to the tailboard.

A woman came up the lane with a basket and stood at the verge while we struggled. Then she set the basket down. She took the horse's head. The horse quieted for her. The basket had a cloth over it, and the cloth had a shape under it.

"Put a board under the wheel," she said.

The boy fetched a board out of the ditch and laid it in the rut. The carter put his shoulder to the tailboard again, and I put mine to the wheel, and the clay took my hands. The cart came out. The horse went forward. The wheel rolled up onto the hard, and the cart stood on the crown of the lane.

We loaded the sacks again. The woman took up her basket and wiped the cloth where the clay had come on it, and the wiping was done carefully, the way a person wipes a thing that is not hers to spoil.

"The loaf will want a clean cloth," I said.

"It goes to a sill at the turn."

"Whose sill?"

"It is not mine to say," she said, and she took up her basket and went on toward the bridge.

The carter lifted the board out of the rut. The clay ran back into the place where the wheel had been, and the rut closed over, and by the time the sacks were squared on the cart there was no mark in the lane of the stopping, only the wet on the wall where we had stacked them.

***

I walked back over the bridge with the wind off the river taking the heat from my hands.

The clean cut stayed with me. So did the seven loaves, the seven crofts each putting a loaf at a door that was not a home, feeding a woman the law had decided was nowhere's to keep. They had forgotten the reason and kept the rite, which is the shape mercy takes when the law has already spoken: a kindness with no object and no end, performed because it was always performed, the way a church bell is rung after the service has ended and no one remembers who first pulled the rope. Seven houses. Seven loaves. Forty years of a thing done because it had always been done, and the doing of it was the only warmth the parish had left to give, and the giving of it changed nothing, because a loaf at a gate is not a name in a book.

The only warmth in the whole of it had been the woman herself. She stood at the gate in the read, her hand on the wood, looking past me at the house she had been told not to want. The looking-past was the only part of her that had carried any heat, and the heat had been a want she had been given no one to name to. The law had taken her name and her place and left her that one unasked want, and the want was the only true thing in the room, because a want you cannot speak is the one a statute cannot reach and cannot grant. A parish can strike a name from a book. It cannot strike the wanting out of a person who stands at a gate with her palm flat on the wood, looking at a house she was told not to desire, and desiring it anyway, silently, lawfully, beyond the reach of any clause.

I had been given a line back once, by a man who decided I had not used the first and might use the second. She had been given nothing, not even the decency of a crossing-out that leaves the name legible beneath, accusing, alive in the strike. A scraping says the name was never rightly there, and the lawfulness of the scraping says it twice, and the two together leave her outside with no handle to turn and no one to ask why the handle was taken. My second line was a gift. Hers was a deletion. The two are not the same shape, and the not-sameness is the thing I carry back across the bridge, heavier than the oilcloth.

That was the second wrong guess corrected, and the correcting of it was colder than the guessing, because a crime can be undone and a law cannot, and the law had been the knife all along. I came to Tindle for a villain and found a clerk, and the clerk's hand had been steadier than any villain's, because a villain fears the looking and a clerk invites it, sets the reason in the margin in a fine even hand, and sleeps without the trouble of a conscience, since conscience is for men who choose, and a clerk will tell you he only wrote what the page required. The page required a woman to vanish. He vanished her, and the vanishing was correct, and the correctness is the part that will keep me awake, because a wrong can be righted and a right that wounds cannot.

The river moved under the bridge, grey and indifferent, the same colour as the lawful page. I buttoned my coat against the wind and kept walking, carrying the scraped line back to the house where Edward would weigh it in one hand the way he weighs most things, and write the eighth, and ask me nothing. The asking was the only question the law had left unanswered, and the only one I had not yet learned to leave alone. The wind rose. My hands numbed. My breath came white. The bridge was long. The river ran. The road bent away from the water and the houses began, and the houses were lit against the early dark, and none of them was the gate, and none of them was hers, and the not-being was the point, because the law had decided she belonged to no house and no parish and no page, and a person decided out of existence by a clause is a person the candles cannot warm. The child at her skirt would grow without a name in the book, fed by seven loaves and claimed by none, and the growing would be lawful too, and the lawfulness would follow her the way a shadow follows a body into every room she was told she could not enter.

The page in the oilcloth was grey and faceless and colder than any crime I had come to find, and I had come all the way to Tindle to learn that the lawful thing is the one that leaves no one to forgive.

The bridge planks were slick with the river's breath. I crossed to the far side where the road home begins, and the scraped name went with me, lawful and faceless and settled, the cleanest cut in the book, and the cleanest cut was the one that would not close. The house was dark. The gate was not.

---

## Thirteen · The Meaning of Lawful

The vestry kept its law in a book the size of a small coffin. I asked the clerk for it before I asked him for the key, because the key meant nothing without the will to turn it, and the will I had. He brought the book on a board, the way a person brings a weight he will not trust to one pair of hands, and set it under the window where the light came down grey from a sky that had promised rain and meant to keep the promise.

The statute book was live. The book would outlast us. I knew it the way I knew the river was live, by the plain fact that it moved and governed and would outlast the hand that opened it. I do not read live books. The dead ones I read, the records and ledgers and rate-books that have finished their work and gone quiet. This book had not finished. So I opened it with my eyes, and the opening was the one rule I had never broken, and the keeping of it was the only clean thing in a room full of scraped lines and empty slots.

The rule had a cost I had not counted until the Tindle book arrived. I could lay my thumb on the dead record of the strike and meet the blade in the memory it left, but I could not lay it on the living law that commanded the blade, because the law was still awake and governing and outside my gift. The cruelty, then, was the one part of the matter I would read with my eyes, and the eyes are a colder reader than the thumb, because the eyes ask nothing of the thing they read.

The binding was cracked leather gone pale at the hinge. The pages inside were a thinner stock than the records used, a paper meant to take amendment rather than entry, so that each act sat in its own block with margins wide enough for a later hand to write a later will. The ink was black still, the king's name at the head of every leaf, and the whole smelled of the vault it lived in, dry and cold and a little of mice, the same mice that had nested in the Tindle book, the rodents of the law keeping the same lodgings in every parish.

The index ran in a cramped hand, the letters crowded as commuters at a gate. I found the register acts by their year and turned to them, and there, set out in the order the parliament had voted them, sat Class Nineteen.

I already knew Class Nineteen. Edward had shown it to me in the first week of the marriage, not as a kindness but as a fact he thought I ought to carry the weight of, the way he carried the weight of most facts. Class Nineteen governed offices held by proxy. It barred them. A parish might not keep a name on its register by appointing a man to stand in for the man who ought to have stood. The stand-in was not lawful. The office held by a hand that was not the owner's hand did not count, and the name it held for was a name the law declined to recognise, so that the space on the register where a person should sit stayed empty of lawful claim. I had read that clause with my thumb, in the dead copy the audit office kept, and I understood it then the way a person understands a wall: it was there, it would stay, and it would not let you through.

So Class Nineteen was closed to the woman. The door was shut fast. Her place on the register could not be kept by a proxy. That was the first door, and it had been shut before she was born, by a clause meant for offices and applied, by the cold habit of administration, to a person who had no one to stand for her.

The proxy bar existed to stop a parish from keeping a name by nomination, from filling a vacant place with a creature of its own making and calling the filling lawful. That was its purpose, and it was a just one, as such bars go. But a just bar has a second face. Because no proxy could hold the woman's place, the place could not be kept at all, and a place that could not be kept by any hand but the lawful holder's became, by the plain reading of the statute, a place that no one lawfully held. The bar meant to protect her from a false keeper became the hinge the true erasure turned on. The law does not mean to be cruel. It means to be consistent, and consistency is what does the harm, because consistency touches everyone and asks no one.

The second door was the one I had come to find. It sat outside Class Nineteen, in the plain text of the register statute, two leaves past the proxy bar, set down without flourish because the law does not flourish when it gives a parish leave to erase. The words were these:

*When no one lawfully holds a place, the parish may hold it and may lawfully strike the name off.*

I read them three times. The clause did not say why no one held the place. It did not ask. It required only the absence, and the absence the parish had, because the woman had been settled elsewhere, claimed by a man who was not her husband, written into another book in another parish, and the first parish had been left with a name and no lawful holder. The place was empty of a lawful holder. The clause fit it the way a shoe fits a foot that is no longer in it.

A clause that requires an absence is a colder instrument than one that requires an act. An act can be contested after the fact, a hand identified, a will disputed. An absence simply is, and the parish had an absence on its hands the moment the woman was settled elsewhere, and the clause reached into that absence the way a hand reaches into an empty glove. No one had to decide. No one had to write her out. The writing out was what the clause did by itself, once the parish certified the place was empty, and the certifying was a form, and a form is the kindest mask a removal ever wears.

The cold of the page came up through my fingers. Not the cold of a dead thing, which is a remembered cold, but the cold of ink that is still law, still able to be cited against a person forty years after the hand that wrote it had gone to dust. I closed the book and asked the clerk for the dead removals register, the one the parish kept apart from the live statute, and he brought it. My thumb found the seam. I put it to the leaf where the woman's parish ought to have been written.

Three seconds.

The vision gave no face. The register was dead, and I am permitted the dead. The striking came up the way such things come, complete and finished: a hand at a high desk, winter light, the cross-bar of the window behind him throwing a shadow like a letter T across the page, the same shadow the Tindle book had kept. The scrape of the blade under the name came first, a dry dragging against the grain that reached my thumb before anything else, and then the breath held, and the second hand came in, his own, writing the bare place clean. The smell of the vault rose as the vision came, cold stone and mice, and the cold of the page under my thumb was the cold of a room where a person does a thing he does not want to be seen doing, and then it was only paper again, and the smell of mice, and my thumb on the thinned line where a woman's name had been lifted out.

The vision gave me nothing the statute had not already told me. That was the unsettling part. A thumb-read of a dead record ought to add a face, a motive, a sound of breathing, something the cold print lacked. This one added only confirmation, because the strike had been done exactly as the clause directed, without rancor and without haste, the way a person performs a task he has performed forty times and expects to perform forty more. The blade had gone under. The law had been obeyed. The obedience was the whole of what the dead page had to say.

The dead register agreed with the live statute. The parish had held her place because no one lawfully held it, and had struck her name because the clause gave the right, and the right was the whole of the matter, because a right in an act needs no face to exercise it.

The clerk had not moved from the door. He said nothing about what I had read. The finding lay open on the table between us, and the table was a public place, and the parish rule was the old one: do not discuss what is written where anyone may read it.

"She was struck," I said.

He nodded. "If no one held her place, the parish held it. The statute gives the power. The old clerk taught me."

"Struck, not crossed. The name taken out from under, the way Tindle did it. That was this clause, the one outside Class Nineteen."

"The parish may strike the name off. Lawfully. The clause was the warrant. The word is in the book, two leaves past the proxy bar."

The clerk's hands were the colour of the binding, pale at the hinge where the veins showed, and he held the statute board the way a man holds a thing he respects and does not love. He had learned the parish's law the way he had learned his letters, by repetition and without question, and the not-questioning was the part that let the old clerk's teaching pass whole into him, so that he could repeat the strike-clause by rote and never once have weighed whether the woman ought to have been struck. The parish's memory was a kind of forgetting, kept warm by seven crofts and an old Mrs. Pettifer, and the forgetting was the only part the law could not reach.

I asked him then about the feeding, because the question had sat in me since the rate-book first showed its bare line, and the asking was the only way to learn whether the strike and the feeding were enemies or twins. "If she was not yours to keep, why seven loaves in turn, for forty years, unclaimed?"

The clerk's mouth went to a line. He had no answer that satisfied, only the answer the book gave: the statute permitted the strike, and a second clause, set lower on the same page, permitted the parish to hold the struck person in trust, and to feed the trust, and the feeding was lawful because the holding was lawful and the holding followed the striking by the same hand. The parish had struck and fed, both by leave of the act, and the act did not require the feeding, only allowed it, and the allowing was the part that let seven crofts keep a woman alive on paper without once naming her, because no one need answer for what is merely permitted. The law allowed the trust.

***

The door of the vestry opened before the clerk had set the statute on its board, and a woman came in sideways with her apron in her fist and a paper in her hand, and she stopped at the sight of me.

"I waited until you were alone," she said.

"You are not alone now." He took the paper and turned it over. "This is not the day for it."

"The overseer said to come before the weather turned, and I have brought the money."

He went to the chest in the corner. The lid had warped. He turned the key and lifted the lid in both hands, and it came up with the sound of wood letting go of wood. The certificates lay in bundles, each tied, and the ties had writing on them. He read the ties and drew a bundle out.

"Which parish."

"His father's."

"His father is dead."

"His father is dead, and the boy was born here, and I was born here, and the overseer says the certificate must say where he is settled, and I cannot tell him where he is settled, and the man who hired him will not take him without it."

He untied the bundle and laid the blank on the table. The top was printed, the rest empty, and the emptiness was wide. He asked her the name, and she gave it, and he wrote it. He asked the father's name, and she gave that, and he wrote it, and the nib caught at the tail of the word, and he wrote the letter again.

Then he read it to her, slow, and she put both hands on the table and listened with her head down.

At the name she stopped him. "That is not how it goes."

"It is how it goes in the book."

"The book has it short. We say it long."

"Then the certificate is short, and it must match the book, or the man will say the paper is for another." He set the pen down. "Short, or none."

She looked at the paper a while. Then she said, "Short."

He blotted it and set the seal. The wax was red. It smoked at the edge, and he pressed the iron down and held it, and the wax took the impression and went dull.

She took the certificate before the wax was cool and put it inside her apron, against her stays, and the paper bent to her and stayed bent.

"Will it serve?" I said.

"It will serve where it is read," he said. "It says what the book says."

"And where the book is wrong?"

"The book is not wrong." He shut the chest and turned the key and put it in his pocket, and the pocket was inside his coat, and the coat was on the hook, and he did not put it on. "A clerk who mends the book makes a book no one can rely on. Then the overseer writes what he likes, and the poor man has nothing to show."

The woman went out with her hand flat over her apron. The door came to. A flake of wax lay where the certificate had lain, and the clerk put it in the fire. The fire took it.

***

I left the vestry with the wind off the river and the clause still in my head, the words in the order the parliament had voted them, untouched by any hand but the one that set them. Edward had been right, as he usually was about the shape of a lawful thing. The scraping had been procedure. The margin in the Tindle book had said *It is entirely lawful*, and the statute said the same in longer words and with the king's name at the head of it. The woman had not been stolen. She had been administered, and administration is the harder loss to name, because it leaves no thief.

Edward had said it in the first week, of another matter and another book: a crime has a doer, and a cutting has a clause. The doer you can name and pursue and, if the law is willing, hang. The clause you cannot, because the clause is the law, and the law is the thing that names the doers and lets the clause go free. I had near-laughed at him then, because the distinction had seemed one of his cold comforts. It was not a comfort. It was a wall, and the woman stood on the far side of it with no handle to turn.

The wind took the words from my mouth before I had shaped them, which was its own kind of lawful, the weather administering the parish's silence to me on the open road. I thought of the private book the chandler's grandfather had kept, the one that said *It is entirely lawful* beside the bare line, and I understood at last that the old man had not been defending the act. He had been marking it, the way a person marks a door he cannot lock, writing the one phrase that admits no argument so that the next reader would know the door had been closed on purpose and by rule. The lawfulness was the mark. The mark had no name.

The road to Collow ran along the water. The river made no sound. I walked it with my hands in my sleeves, and I thought about the clean cut and the seven loaves and the trust no one remembered making, and I understood the cruellest part: a law that needs no malice needs no malefactor. There is no face to accuse. The man who claimed her had a name, and the clerk who struck her had a name, and the seven crofts that fed her had names, but the clause had none, and the clause was the only one that mattered, because the clause is what outlasts the hands.

A person can be unmade by patience. A crossing-out leaves the name beneath it, still saying itself. A scraping leaves nothing, which is a colder accusation, because it says the name was never meant to have been there at all, and the statute says the same in the voice of the king, which is the voice that admits no argument. The woman at the gate, the basket, the hand on the wood, those were hers, and the name was not, and the lawfulness of the taking was written next to the empty space where she had been entitled to be, and the entitlement meant nothing once the place was held by the parish and the name struck by the clause.

Edward was at the window when I got back. The fire took the chill. He had the fire built up against the evening and the lamp trimmed, and the notebook lay open on the table with the pen across it, ready the way he kept things ready without saying he had prepared them. The tea stood drawn in its pot, wrapped in the cloth against the cold, the cup set where my hand would find it without looking. He did not ask what the day had given me. The lamp gave good light. He asked whether I had, and that was the question that let me sit, because the fact of the looking was the part he cared for first, before the showing.

I set the oilcloth parcel down. The lamp caught the edge of the notebook.

"The thirteenth," I said.

He wrote. The pen did not pause. The pen moved in the small quick hand he used for facts that would not change, and he did not lift the page to show me, and the not-showing was its own kind of service, because the entry was mine and he kept it for me without claiming it.

"Thirteenth about her," I said. "The register statute. Class Nineteen bars a proxy-held office, so her place could not be kept by a stand-in. Outside it, the clause: when no one lawfully holds a place, the parish may hold it and may lawfully strike the name off. No one held hers. So the parish held it. And struck her. Four dead things I have read by thumb, the rate-book, the removals register, the assessment, the ledger, and the one live statute by eye, and the woman is still not named, and now I know the door that took her, and the door has no handle."

He closed the book without a word and set it aside, and the aside was the answer, that the thirteenth was written and needed no comment, because a fact recorded is a fact housed, and the housing was all he had promised me.

I went to the drawer before I lit the lamp higher. The half-sheet with *What I want* lay in it, not folded, not burned, the words set down and left open the way the woman's place on the register had been left open until the parish filled it with an absence. I did not read the sheet. The words stayed face down. I only touched the corner, where the ink had pressed through, and then I closed the drawer, because some wants are a place no one lawfully holds, and the holding of them is the one clause the statute does not give.

The thirteenth entry sat in his book. The woman sat in no book at all. The lawful had done its work, and the work was finished, and the finishing left nothing to answer for, which is the whole of what lawful means.

---

## Fourteen · Why Feed Her

The road to Tindle ran pale with the week's thaw, the ruts full of water that had been ice two days before. I walked it on a Thursday because Thursday was the day the men met to set the rates, and a rates meeting is the one time the parish elders sit in one room with their books open and their reasons visible, if you know where to look. I carried the rate-book under my arm. Edward had sent it back from Collow with his marks in the margin and his silence over the rest, as he sends most things back to me: handled, noted, returned without comment on the part that matters.

The vestry stood behind the church, a low room with a small window and a long table worn pale by the elbows of men who came to decide what other people owed. Snow lay in the gutter still, grey where the carts had passed, and the air inside held the smell of cold stone and the wax the clerk used on the ledger covers. A stack of older books leaned against the wall, their boards grey with the dust of years no one had swept.

Hallow kept the chair at the head. He was the elder who had spoken for the parish each time I had come, a man built narrow by long sitting, with hands that closed around a pen as if the pen owed him something. The other six were crofters and a smith, men who paid their rates and trusted Hallow to say the parts they would rather not say. They looked up when I entered and then down again, as men look at a woman who has come to ask a question they have agreed among themselves not to answer.

I set the book on the table. The men studied their hands. A parish meeting has its own weather, and the weather that day was the sort that comes before a question no one wants asked.

"Your books show a line struck from the register forty years back," I said. "The law took her name. The parish has fed her since. Seven crofts, in turn, every week, a loaf or its worth left at a sill no one claims. The law struck her. Why has the parish paid for forty years to keep feeding a woman the law says is not there?"

Hallow's mouth made the shape it made when he held an answer he did not wish to give. He did not look at the book. He looked at the wall behind me, where the parish's own list of the lawfully removed hung framed in a cheap black wood.

"The law struck her," he said. "The trust fed her. The trust is lawful. The question is not one the parish discusses."

"The trust is lawful," I said. "The forgetting is not. Seven crofts feed her in turn and none of them knows why, only that old Mrs. Pettifer began it. The beginning was a person. The continuing is a forgetting. The forgetting is the part that keeps her alive, because to remember would be to name her, and to name her would be to ask the parish to account."

The smith shifted his weight. A crofter at the near end drew a line in the condensation on his cup with one finger and then rubbed it out. The forgetting was mercy and cruelty both, and they knew it, and the knowing was the part they would not speak, because to speak it would have been to name her, and the naming was the one thing the lawful cutting had been built to prevent.

A cousin of old Mrs. Pettifer sat at the far end, a man with her same blunt chin and a coat mended at the cuff. He had said nothing in the meetings before. He spoke now, slow, as a man pays out a story he was told not to tell, and the cost showed in the pause before each sentence, a coin kept back that he spent anyway.

"She was not of Tindle. She came with a man who was not her husband. She went away with nothing. And the child... the parish would not have the child. Old Mrs. Pettifer said the woman had been wronged, and the wrong was lawful, and the lawful wrong is the only kind no one can undo, so the parish would feed her, in turn, and the turn would not end."

He stopped. His jaw worked. Hallow said nothing to stop him and nothing to help him, and the silence between the two men was a quiet agreement made without a word spoken in the room where the word would have counted.

"Why not let her vanish," I said. "The law struck her. The gap is cheap. Why pay for a woman no one will name."

The cousin looked at me as the old look at a person who has asked the one question they have spent forty years not asking. The room held its breath around the question. The smith set his cup down with care, as if the cup might break if he confessed by dropping it.

"Because striking her off is not the same as her being gone," he said. "The law can take a name from a book. The law cannot take a person from a gate. She was still there. Old Mrs. Pettifer said so. She said the parish had struck her and the parish would feed her, because to strike and not feed is to murder by clerical means, and the clerical murder is the one the law will not forgive, because the law did not command it."

The words stayed in the room, heavy. Hallow's face did not change, but his hand, which had been flat on the table, closed into a fist the size of a walnut and then opened again. The elder's honesty, when it came, came sideways, through a dead woman's cousin, and the elder himself never gave it directly, which was an honesty kept slantwise, the sort that lets a thing be said without ever being owned.

I put my thumb to the page before me, the dead tally where the unclaimed loaves were entered week by week in a hand that had belonged to no one living. The paper was cold and thin, as an old record goes cold, and under the thumb a smell rose, stale bread and the woodsmoke of a kitchen hearth, the ghost of a woman setting a loaf on a sill she would never cross. Three seconds. Then only the cold page and the smell of the room. The dead book had kept her better than the register had. The register forgot her. The tally remembered her in the only way the parish allowed, as a number against a line that had no name.

***

The door went while the clerk was sanding the tally, and a woman came in with a loaf in her arms, wrapped in a cloth, and she set it on the table in front of Hallow and stepped back with her hands at her sides.

"I am to leave it at the sill," she said. "Only the sill is not there."

Hallow did not touch the loaf. "Which sill."

"The one at the turn. The house is down. They took the roof off in the frost and the wall came after, and there is no sill and no door and nothing to leave a loaf on."

The men looked at the loaf. The cloth was grey and had been washed until the weave showed, and the loaf had gone hard at the crust, and where the cloth had pressed it there was a flat place.

"Whose turn is it," Hallow said.

"Mine, this time."

"Then leave it at the next sill."

"The next sill is mine."

The smith turned his cup. The clerk stopped writing. The pen stood in the air, and the drop at the nib went down and made a shadow on the page and did not fall.

I lifted the corner of the cloth. The bread was hard as a brick and it had a smell of cold ash, and the woman kept her eyes on my hand and not on my face.

"It is hard," I said.

"It went out soft," she said. "I have baked the turn since my mother, and it never came back hard before."

Hallow put his hand flat on the table.

"The loaf goes on the sill," he said. "What becomes of it is not the parish's account."

"It was taken before," the woman said. "Every time it was taken. The cloth came back washed and laid on the step, and I never learned who took it, and now the sill is down and I have carried the loaf here because the vestry is where the account is kept."

"Set it at the church porch," Hallow said. "It will be taken there."

"Will it."

"That is not the parish's account either."

The woman took up the loaf. She held it against her front the way a woman holds a thing she is tired of carrying and will not put down. At the door she stopped.

"There is no name on the cloth," she said. "There never was a name. I have carried the turn since my mother, and no one has ever told me who takes it in."

"Then you have carried it correctly," Hallow said.

The door came to. The smith set his cup down and let his hand stay on it. The clerk sanded the line he had written and shut the book, and the sand went over the edge of the table and lay on the boards in a small pale scatter.

The loaf had left a mark on the table, a ring where the crust had sat, and the clerk wiped it with the side of his hand and then with his cuff, and the ring came up and the wood showed darker under it.

***

The law did not command the feeding. Old Mrs. Pettifer had. The law struck; she fed. The one was lawful, the other was mercy, and the mercy had outlasted the woman who began it, because she had taught her daughter, and the daughter had taught the crofts, and the crofts fed in turn, lawfully, without a name, because the name would have been to discuss what the law struck, and the discussing was the parish's rule not to do.

A pattern opened in front of me as a room opens when a door you thought locked gives an inch. My mother had done this same thing, in our own parish, a small wrong she could not undo and so fed around the edges of, month by month, as if a loaf placed where it was not owed could balance a scale no court would touch. Mrs. Pettifer had done it here, forty years before, for a stranger who had come with the wrong man and left with nothing but a child the parish refused. Two women, neither able to strike back at a lawful thing, both answering the law with bread. The mercy was the only weapon a woman could aim at a statute, and they had aimed it where they stood, without leave and without a name.

This parish had become a family of a different make. Not the kind with a hearth and a shared name. A family built on a silence kept together, seven crofts who did not know one another's reasons and did not ask, bound by a loaf passed hand to hand to a woman none of them had met. They fed a person who did not exist on paper, and in the feeding they became the only household she had. The found family is meant to be a mercy. Here it was a mercy and a cage both, because the family that will not name you is the family that cannot lose you and cannot free you either.

My mother's small mercies had made a family of two, her and me, bound by the things she would not name aloud. Mrs. Pettifer's had made a family of seven crofts and a ghost. The shape was the same under the difference of the size. A woman feeds what the law has thrown out, and in the feeding she gathers whoever will help her, and the helping makes them hers, and the silence makes them stay. I had come looking for one erased name and found the outline of my own household drawn in another parish's hand.

The law owned the scraping, and the law owned the gap. Old Mrs. Pettifer owned the seven loaves, and the parish owned the forgetting, and the not-naming was the whole of both, because a woman you will not name is a woman you need not answer to, and the not-answering is the mercy and the cruelty both at once.

"She lived on paper," I said.

"She lived on paper," he said. "The parish chose to let her. Not in the register. In the turning of the loaves, and the unclaimed line, and the not-discussing. She lived there, lawfully, unnamed, fed. That was old Mrs. Pettifer's answer to the lawful wrong. Not to undo it. To outlast it."

To outlast it. That was the mercy. Not to strike back at the law. To feed a woman the law had struck, in turn, forever, unnamed, because the outlasting was the only vengeance a parish could take on a lawful cutting, and the lawful cutting could not forbid the outlasting, because a loaf on a sill with no name is the one thing the law cannot reach.

The loaf was the whole of the argument, and the loaf was a small thing. I thought of the size of it. A loaf the size of two hands, set on a sill once a week, and a woman fed by it for forty years, and the feeding had kept her alive and the keeping alive had kept her nameless, and the namelessness was the price of the bread. The parish had paid her in bread and charged her in name, and the charging had been lawful, and the bread had been mercy, and a person cannot eat a name and cannot be nourished by a law, and so the parish had chosen the bread and let the name go, and the choosing had been kind and the kindness had been the erasure. I put my hand flat on the tally, on the column of unmarked weeks, and the paper was cold, and the cold was the only thing the dead could give me, and the cold was enough. It had to be enough. A person who reads the dead learns to be satisfied with cold, because cold is what the dead have, and the cold of a lawful mercy is the coldest cold there is.

I have never been able to abide a thing wasted. A loaf set where no mouth will meet it looks like the purest waste, the sort that turns my stomach as spoiled grain does, and I have walked past enough spoiled grain in enough poor parishes to know the shape of that turn. But the loaf on that sill was not waste. It was an argument the parish made to no one and won against no one, a quiet refusal to let a lawful erasure finish its work. The woman who could not be named was fed because feeding her was the one reply the law had left open, and a reply left open is the only kind worth making, because it costs the law nothing and costs the law everything it cannot see.

The rate-book went back into its oilcloth. The vestry men went back to setting the rates, and the not-discussing resumed, and the woman lived on, on paper, in the turning of the loaves, and I had my answer, and the answer was old Mrs. Pettifer, dead, and her daughter, living, and the seven crofts, forgetting, and the forgetting was the mercy, and the mercy was lawful, and what the lawfulness left behind was a wound with no face.

***

Edward wrote the entry when I told him, that evening, after the lamp was lit and the house had gone quiet, as it does when he is in it and not speaking. The fourteenth. He took the notebook from the shelf where he keeps it, the one with the stiff board covers and the pages he numbers himself, and he wrote without asking what the number should mark. He did not say what it was. He only closed the notebook and set it aside, as he sets aside the questions he will not ask, and the not-asking was the mercy he gave me, and the mercy was the one thing in the room that had a face, because his face was the face the law did not have, and the having of it was the difference between a cutting and a person.

He had brought the tea in before I sat, two cups, the one he always sets at my place though I had not asked for it, and he had gone back to his own chair without a word about the carrying of it. That was the shape of his service, done and withdrawn, no account offered, no receipt asked. I drank. The cup was warm where his hand had been, and the warmth was the only open thing he had given the room.

The paper I had written on still lay on the shelf in the parlour, the words "What I want" set down and left unfolded, the crease never made. I had not folded it. I had not burned it. I had only written the words and set them where I could see them, open, as the parish had left its woman open on the page, fed but not named, present but not discussed. The not-folding was its own kind of outlasting, a small one, mine, the sort a person keeps who has learned that some things survive only so long as you refuse to close them.

His face in the lampglow held the stillness of a man who has decided the cost of a question and chosen not to spend it. I did not give him the woman's name, because the woman had no name to give, and he did not ask for one, because the asking is the part he leaves to me. The fourteenth entry sat in his book, closed, faced, accounted for in the only way either of us trusts: written down, and not opened again.

---

## Fifteen · The Fold

I came home from Tindle with the why in my head and the want still in the drawer, and the two did not meet, because the why was the law and the want was the thing no one had asked me to name, and the law does not ask and the want cannot answer. The road home had been long and the clay had dried on my boots by the time I reached the door, and I stood a moment on the step with my hand on the latch before I lifted it, the way I always stood now, because a door is a question and I had learned to let the question be a question before I answered it.

***

The rain had stopped. A voice came out of the lane before I had lifted the latch, and it said my name the way a person says a word she has practised alone, and I turned with my hand still on the iron.

She stood at the gate with a paper held against her front. Her shawl was wet. She had come up the lane in the dark without a lantern.

"You are the one that reads," she said.

"I read what is finished."

"This is finished." She held out the paper. "It was put under my door, and the man at the church is gone to a burial, and the schoolmaster is gone to his sister, and the innkeeper's wife said the woman at the audit clerk's house reads."

The paper had been out in the rain. The ink had run at the bottom. The seal was broken, and the broken seal had left a red smear across the middle of it.

I took it to the window and held it up to the light that came through the shutter. The light was thin. The paper was thin. The words came up out of it line by line.

It was a removal. The overseer's hand on the parish's printed form, her name in the blank, and the name of a parish she had not been born in, and at the foot the words that made it a thing and not a letter: that she was chargeable, and that she was to be removed, and that the parish would pay the carrier to take her.

I read it to her, and then I read it again. At the parish name she stopped me and made me say the name alone, and then she said it after me, slow, the way a person tries a word in a language she does not have.

"That is not where I was born," she said.

"It is where the paper says you were born."

"I was born in the next parish. I have lived here since I married. I have paid my rent."

"The paper does not ask what you paid."

She took the paper back and held it the way she had held it at the gate, and the wet of it came through her shawl.

"Then I am to go," she said.

"You are to go unless you can show where you were born."

"I have nothing to show it. My mother is dead and the chest was burnt when the roof went."

"Then go to the parish and ask for the copy."

"They will want paying."

I had nothing to put against that. A married woman cannot stand for another in a parish book, and a letter from my hand would be a letter from a stranger, and the overseer would read the stranger and not the letter.

She went down the lane with the paper against her front. The dark took her where the wall turns, and after that there was the sound of her feet on the wet, and then not that. The gate was empty.

The iron was cold. The latch lifted under my hand. The door gave. I went in.

***

The lamp was lit. Edward was at the window, the notebook closed on his knee, the way he kept it when he was somewhere else and would come back when his hands were warm. He had kept the fire in. He did not say so. The room was warm in the particular way a room is warm when someone has tended it for another person and then sat down and let the tending go unmentioned, and the warmth sat in the air like a thing done and not claimed.

I set the oilcloth parcel down and I went to the drawer and I took out the half-sheet.

*What I want.*

The bloom on the word *want* had dried to a flat brown. Nine days, and the nib's drop had been the only thing that had fallen on the paper. The page was the cheap stuff that drank ink and held it, and the holding had made the letters sit in the fibre rather than on it, so that the words looked pressed into the paper rather than written, and the pressing was honest, because the words had been pressed out of me and not written easily. I read the three words, and the want would not come, and the not-coming was the same not-asking turned inward, and I had been given two lives not to name it.

I sat with the sheet on the table and the lamp leaning over it, and the fire ticking in the grate, and Edward not looking, and the room holding the shape of the question the way a room holds the shape of a person who has just left it.

But something in me had changed on the bridge. The woman at the gate had been struck by law and fed by mercy, and the mercy had been old Mrs. Pettifer's, and the outlasting had been the only vengeance, and the outlasting was a thing kept, not a thing finished. She had been kept on paper, unnamed, fed, because someone had decided to keep her rather than let her vanish. The keeping was not a completion. It was a decision to leave the thing open and tend it, the way a fire is left open and tended, the way a wound is left open and dressed. Nobody had finished the woman at the gate. Somebody had kept her. And the keeping had outlasted the law that struck her, because the law could take a name from a book but could not take a loaf from a sill.

I took the pen. I dipped it. The nib came up black and full, and I held it a moment over the page, and the wetness of the ink caught the lamplight and made a small dark bead at the tip, and then I set it down and I wrote the next word.

*What I want is —*

The word would not come. Not her name, not the child, not the man who claimed her. Those were wants for someone else. The want on the paper was meant to be my own, and my own was the one want I had never been asked to name, and the not-being-asked was the reason the pen would not move, in either life. I sat with the pen on the paper and the ink bleeding a little at the full stop, and I waited for the word the way you wait for a person who is late and may not come, and the word did not come, and the not-coming was not a failure. It was a fact. The fact was that I had been asked for other people all my life, and the asking had been the whole of my use, and no one had ever stood in front of me and asked what I wanted and waited for the answer without needing it to be about them.

But this time I did not set the pen down with the sentence unfinished and walk away. This time I lifted the pen, and I folded the half-sheet once, along the line where the words ended, and I pressed the fold with the side of my thumb, and the crease came up sharp and pale across the paper, and I put it in the drawer, and the folding was the difference between this night and the first.

I had folded things before. I had folded letters and accounts and the clean linen. But I had not folded a want before. A want folded is a want kept, and a want kept is a want you mean to come back to, and I had spent two lives not coming back to my own. The crease was small and exact, and it held the paper the way a hand holds a thing it does not mean to drop, and the holding was the whole of the decision.

The first night I had left it open, the three words and the bloom, neither kept nor destroyed, because I had not decided. An open sheet is a question still being asked. A folded sheet is a question put away with the intention of asking it again. Tonight I folded it. The folding was a decision, and the decision was to keep it, the way old Mrs. Pettifer had decided to keep the woman, not to name her, not to finish her, but to keep her, on paper, in the turning of the loaves, in the not-discussing, alive.

I had crossed things out before. In the book before this, the one before our marriage, I had crossed a thing out, and the crossing had been a closing, a thing ended, a line struck the way the law strikes a name. I had taken a pen and drawn the line through and pressed until the paper gave, and the pressing had been the anger, and the anger had been clean because it had an end. The fold was not the cross. The cross ends. The fold keeps. The cross says this is finished. The fold says this is not finished and I will not pretend it is. I sat with the difference and let it be the size it was, which was the size of a life, because a person who has been crossed out once learns to tell the difference between a line drawn through her and a crease made in her, and the crease is the one that lets her come back.

I left the folded half-sheet in the drawer. My hand stayed above it a moment, the way it had stayed above the open sheet nine days before, but this time the stay was not a not-deciding. It was a having-decided, and the having-decided was the warmer of the two, because a thing folded is a thing you mean to come back to, and a thing crossed is a thing you mean to leave. The hand did not shake. It hung there, open, above the drawer, and then it closed on the drawer's edge and pushed it home, and the drawer went in with the small wooden sound a drawer makes when it is shut on something kept and not on something finished.

Edward had not moved from the window. He had not looked at the paper. He reads empties by not looking, and the folded half-sheet was the fullest empty thing in the room, and he gave it the mercy of his not-seeing. The window was black now, and his reflection sat in it faintly, the way a man sits in a dark pane, and the reflection was still, and the stillness was his way of being in a room without taking it.

"You folded it," he said, to the window.

"I folded it."

"That is the fifteenth thing you have done that I will write. The fold, not the cross. The keeping, not the ending."

"You write what I do. You have written the keeping."

"I have. The fifteenth. The first was the read. The second the decision. The third the intent to go. The fold is the fifteenth, and it is the first that is for you, not for her."

He was right. The fourteen before had been for the woman at the gate, the reads, the decisions, the goings, the findings. The fold was for me, the first want I had kept instead of crossed, the first time I had decided to keep a thing I could not finish. I did not say so. He did not need me to say so. He had written it, and the writing was his way of saying it, and the saying was the only kind he could do without asking me for an answer.

I went and stood behind him at the window. I did not touch him. I stood close enough that the warmth of him came through the wool of his sleeve to the back of my hand, and I let it come, and I did not say the thing that the warmth was asking me to say, because the thing was still folded in the drawer and I had decided to keep it folded a while longer. He did not turn. He did not ask. The not-asking was the mercy he had given me from the first, and I had learned to stand inside it the way a person stands inside a doorway in the rain, glad of the shelter and unable to say so.

He did not turn, and I did not make him. We stood in the dark pane together, two reflections, and the reflections did not touch, and the not-touching was the marriage in a single image, two people close enough to share a window and not close enough to say the thing the window was for. I thought about the first year, when the not-saying had been a kind of shyness, and the second year, when it had become a habit, and the habit had hardened without either of us deciding it, the way a path hardens when it is walked the same way every day. He had asked me once, early, what I wanted, and I had said a house that keeps the rain out, and he had written it down, and the writing had been a kindness and a way of not hearing me, and I had let it be both, because a true answer would have cost more than either of us had.

The heat of him came through the wool. I put my forehead against his shoulder blade, not to be held, only to be near, and the wool was rough and warm and smelled of the field and the fire, and the smell was the smell of a man who had kept the fire in and not said so. He lifted his hand and set it over mine where it rested at his sleeve, and the hand was heavy and warm and did not pull me closer, and the not-pulling was the whole of his tenderness, the way the not-asking was the whole of his mercy. We stood like that while the fire ticked down. I did not say the folded thing. He did not ask it. The drawer held it, and the holding was the promise, and the promise was the only words either of us could manage, and the words were not words at all.

After a while I stepped back. The window had fogged where our breath had reached it, and the fog was a small cloud on the black, and through the cloud the yard was nothing, and the nothing was restful, because a yard you cannot see cannot ask you for anything. I went back to the table and sat with the lamp and let the warmth of his hand fade off mine, and the fading was slow, and the slowness was the point. A warmth that fades slowly was a warmth that had been given fully, and I had learned to measure the gifts of a silent man by how long they lasted after he had stopped giving them.

I sat with the lamp. The folded half-sheet was in the drawer, kept, not finished, not burned. The woman at the gate was kept on paper, unnamed, fed. The two keepings were not the same, but the hand that keeps learns one motion and uses it twice, and I had learned it from old Mrs. Pettifer by proxy, and from my mother by blood, and from the parish of Tindle by law, and the learning was the only warmth in a mercy that had begun in a wrong and continued in a forgetting.

The fold was the midpoint. Not the end of the asking. The asking would go on, to the face behind the law, to the name beneath the scrape. But the fold was the place where I stopped crossing myself out, the way the parish had stopped crossing her out and begun feeding her, and the feeding was the mercy and the cruelty both, and the both was the only true thing. A person is kept by being fed and is unmade by being fed without a name, and the difference between the two is whether anyone ever asks her what she wants, and no one had asked her, and no one had asked me, and the fold was the first time I had asked myself, and the pen had stopped, and the stopping was the answer, and the answer was that the asking was the thing I wanted, and the wanting of it was the first want I had ever kept.

I blew out the lamp. The drawer held the folded half-sheet, kept, and the oilcloth parcel held the scraped line, and Edward held the notebook with the fifteenth entry, and the woman at the gate held the ring that was not hers, and the child held the parish's not-naming, and the law held the face that was no face, and the keeping of all of it was the only warmth, and the warmth was a thing you fold, not cross.

In the dark, before I slept, I put my hand flat on the drawer and left it there a while, the way a person puts a hand on a door to feel whether the room beyond is warm, and the wood was cool, and under the wood the paper was folded, and the fold was a promise I had made to a want I could not yet name. The wood was cool and the paper under it was folded, and the fold was the only thing in the house that was entirely mine and entirely unfinished, and I slept with my hand still on the drawer's edge, the way a child sleeps with a hand on the rail of a bed she is afraid of falling out of.

---

## Sixteen · The Outline of a Name

The seven crofts that turned the loaves were named in the ledger's encoding: Hallow, Pettifer, Quine, and four others. The encoding was the clerk's, a system of letters and numbers that meant nothing to a stranger and everything to a man who had kept the book, and I had spent two evenings with the tally and the rate-book side by side before the system gave, the way a lock gives when the last tooth finds its place. One of the four was a T.

A letter and a dash. Not a name. The dash was the same dash that had taken the woman's whole name from the register and left the gap, and the gap was the only monument to her, and the monument was lawful, and the lawfulness was the wound with no face. I wrote the letter on a slip of paper, a capital T with a dash after it, and I looked at it a long while, and the looking did not turn it into a name. A letter and a dash is a door held open an inch and then shut, and the shutting was done by a hand that had wanted to be careful and not cruel, and the carefulness was the cruelty, because a careful man leaves you a letter and takes the rest.

I had seen a T before. Not in Tindle. In the book before this one, the one my aunt had made, the names beyond the bridge, the aunt who had been kept alive by a different mercy and a different law. And in the letter at the end of the book before that, the third letter, the one that had come a hundred and eleven miles and said a thing I had not answered. The letter was in the box with the others, and I had not answered it, and the not-answering had sat in me like a stone in a shoe, not enough to stop the walking, enough to make every step a reminder.
