The T in the Tindle ledger was not the same T. Or it might have been. The parish of Tindle and the parish beyond the bridge were not the same, but the war with France had moved people, and a name moved with them, and the name Tace (for I had begun to think the name, though I had not read it) was the kind that survives a removal, because it is short and plain and belongs to no one in particular and therefore to everyone who needs a name to hide behind.

I turned the thought over the way you turn a key that may not fit. The fifteenth line had been scraped. The woman at the gate had been claimed by a man not her husband, settled elsewhere, struck by the law of settlement. If the man had been a T, and the claim had been entered and struck, and the striking had been lawful, then the woman at the gate might have been a Tace, or married to one, or claimed by one, and the name beneath the scrape might have been hers, and the scraping might have been the parish's way of not saying so.

But the evidence was not there. The rate-book gave me the scrape and the margin. The register gave me the gap. The assessment gave me the payment. The ledger gave me the turning. The statute gave me the law. None gave me the name. The name was the one thing every book had been careful, and lawful, never to write. A parish can be made to keep a record of everything except the one thing you want, and the keeping of everything else is the proof that the one thing was known and withheld.

I put my thumb to the rate-book's fifteenth line, not to read the woman but to read the margin, because the margin was the one place the name had been admitted, as a not-naming.

Three seconds.

The same desk, the cross-bar window, the T-shaped shadow. The writer, the chandler's grandfather, writing the margin, and the pen pressed hard, and the words came up: *It is entirely lawful*, and beneath, *she was claimed by a man not her husband, of the family T, and the claim was entered, and the entry was struck by order.* The tallow smell was there again, faint, and the cold of the room, and the small fretful sound of the child beyond the desk, and then the three seconds ended and the margin was only ink again, and the letter was only a letter, and the dash after it was the lawful not-naming.

The name was there, in the read, as a letter and a dash. Not Tace. Not enough. The dash was a door held open an inch and then shut, and the shutting was done by a hand that had wanted to be careful and not cruel, and the carefulness was the cruelty, because a careful man leaves you a letter and takes the rest, and the taking is worse for the letter, because the letter proves he knew the name and chose to give you only the first of it.

I lifted my thumb from the line. The letter was a thread, and it led toward Tace, and the leading was the kind that makes you think you have the answer when you have only the first letter, and the first letter is the cruellest part of a name because it promises the rest and withholds it. A dash is not a name. A dash is the shape of a name with the name taken out, and I had spent the winter learning the shape of things with the thing taken out: a scraped line, a blank slot, a lawful margin, a woman at a gate whose face I had seen and whose name I had not.

I sat with the rate-book open and the lamp low, and I did the thing I had learned to do with a thread. I did not pull it. I held it. A thread pulled too soon breaks, and a thread held is a thread you can follow when the light is better. I had pulled threads before and lost them, and I had learned that the holding is the harder skill and the better one, and the holding was what Edward did with his notebook, and the holding was what old Mrs. Pettifer had done with her loaves, and the holding was the only thing a person could do with a name that had been lawful taken from a book.

Edward wrote the sixteenth when I told him. He did not ask what it was for. He only looked at the rate-book and then at me, and the look was the one he gave a column that would not add, because the letter did not add to a name, only to a suspicion, and a suspicion is not a finding.

"I think it is Tace," I said.

"You think. The books do not say."

"The books never say. The books are lawful. The lawfulness is the not-saying."

He nodded, because that was true, and the true was the only thing he would give weight to. He set the pen down and turned the notebook toward me so that I could see the entry, which he did not often do, and the entry was one line and a space and a question mark, and the question mark was his way of writing a thing he would not decide for me.

"The letter leads to Tace," he said, "and Tace leads to the bridge, and the bridge leads to the book before this one. You have been here before. You know how a name at the end of a thread can pull you somewhere you did not mean to go."

"I know."

"Then go carefully. A name is not a person. You found that out with your aunt. You will find it out again."

He was right, and the rightness of it was the cold kind, the kind that does not warm you and does not let you go. I had found it out with my aunt. I had carried her book to a board and won, and the winning had given her back a line in a register, and the line had not given her back the years, and the years were the part no board could return. A name restored is not a life restored. It is a line restored, and a line is a small thing to a woman who has been a blank, and it is the only thing the law can give, and the law gives it as if it were everything.

I put the rate-book away. The letter was a thread, and the thread was thin, and the thinness was the lawful not-naming, and the not-naming was the wound, and the wound was the woman at the gate, and the gate was the house she had been told not to want, and the not-wanting was the law, and the law was the cruellest mercy, cutting clean, asking nothing, answering nothing, leaving a letter where a name should be.

***

The knock came at the back door before the lamp had caught. The clerk's grandson stood on the step with a sack under one arm and his cap going round and round in his hands.

"The vestry sent me," he said. "My grandfather is the clerk. He said I was to leave the spills and ask after the book."

"Come in out of the wet."

He came in as far as the mat. I left the door open. His boots were wet. The sack stayed under his arm until I cleared the table, and then he set it down.

Spills, cut narrow for the vestry fire. The cut was straight. The bundle was tied with the red cord, knotted small and hard, which is the knot of a man who does not trust a boy's fingers.

"Does he cut them himself?"

"At the desk. What he does not like goes in the fire, and what is left over he cuts for spills."

I turned out a handful. The paper was the parish's printed forms, cut across, so the words came apart in the middle. A strip carried the head of a form and the words *to be removed*, and the rest of the sentence had gone into a strip I did not have. I did not ask for it.

"Does he read them after they are cut?"

"He says a spill is a spill."

"Hold this up to the lamp."

He held it up. The light came through where the printing had bitten, and the letters stood dark and thin, and the words were the end of a form and the beginning of nothing.

"Now turn it."

He turned the strip over. The other side was clean. He held it steady, and the steadiness was the parish's own, the steadiness of men taught to hold a pen and not to ask what the pen is for.

"Tell your grandfather the book is not done with."

"He said you would say that. He said I was to ask what you have found."

"And what will you tell him?"

"That you were sorting spills."

He had made the lie before I asked for it, and the making was the only thing given unasked.

"Does his hand shake when he writes?"

"He warms it at the fire before he takes up the pen." I cut bread for him. He ate it standing, with his back to the door, and he said the rest with his mouth full. "He says a cold hand makes a bad letter."

A man who warms his hand so the letter comes out clean. The care is not the crime and it is not the excuse, and a boy cannot be asked to see the difference.

He finished the bread. He wiped his palms on his coat and wound the cord round his fingers, the way a boy winds a thing he must bring back, and went out. The sack was heavy.

I latched the door. The strip lay on the board with *to be removed* running to the cut edge and stopping there, and the stopping was the parish's way with a sentence as much as with a woman. I put it in the box with the letter and the slip. The lid went down.

***

That night I took out the third letter and read it again. It was short, and the hand was old, and it said a thing I had not answered, and the thing was the same thing the margin said in its way: that a person can be kept and not named, and the keeping is a mercy, and the mercy is a cruelty, and the cruelty is lawful, and the lawfulness is the part that leaves no one to blame. I read it twice and I put it back in the box and I did not answer it, because the answering was a door I was not ready to open, and a door left shut is a door still there.

I lay awake and thought about the letter and the dash, and about the difference between a name that has been taken and a name that has been withheld. A taken name leaves a wound. A withheld name leaves an itch. The wound is the kind you can point to and say, here, the law did this. The itch is the kind you cannot point to, the kind that keeps you awake and makes you doubt whether there was ever a name at all, and the doubt is the law's best work, because a person who doubts the name will stop asking, and a person who stops asking is a person the law does not have to answer. I would not stop asking. I had been given a second life to ask, and the second life did not permit the doubt, because the doubt was the one thing that would have made the first life's loss a waste.

I thought about my aunt, beyond the bridge. She had been kept alive by a mercy too, and the mercy had been a different mercy and a different law, and the keeping had been a keeping and not a naming, and I had carried her book to a board and won, and the winning had given her back a line and not a life. She had not asked me to win it. She had not asked me for anything. That was the thing I had not understood until now: the kept are rarely asked, because the asking would make the keeping a debt, and a debt is a thing the law can see, and the law can strike a debt. A mercy that asks nothing cannot be entered in a book, and a thing not entered in a book cannot be struck, and so the mercy survives by being unrecorded, and the unrecorded mercy is the only kind that lasts.

My aunt had died with her line restored and her years not restored, and the two facts had sat side by side in the same parish book and had not cancelled each other, and I had thought, when the board gave its verdict, that the restoring would be enough. It had not been enough. It had been a line. A line is what the law can give. A line is not what a person needs. I had learned that from her without her saying it, the way I had learned most things from the women in my family, by watching what they did and not by hearing what they said, and what she had done was live the years the law had left her with a quiet that was not peace and not despair but the third thing, the thing a person settles into when she has stopped expecting the books to give back what the books took.

I did not want that quiet for the woman at the gate. I did not want it for myself. The quiet was the shape a person took when the asking was over, and I was not ready for the asking to be over, and the not-ready was the only thing I had that the law could not touch. The law could take a name and leave a gap, and it could strike a claim and leave a ring, and it could feed a woman and leave her nameless, and it could not take the not-ready out of me, because the not-ready was not entered in any book, and a thing not entered cannot be struck.

I got up and went to the window. The night was clear and the stars were the small hard stars of a late frost, and the yard was white with it, and the whiteness made the dark darker at the edges. I put my hand on the cold pane and left it there until the cold went through to the bone, and the cold was honest, and the honesty was the only comfort, because a cold that comes from the weather is a cold no one chose, and a cold that comes from a lawful mercy is a cold someone chose and called kind. I would take the weather's cold over the law's kindness. The weather's cold did not pretend to be anything but what it was.

Edward stirred in the other room. I heard the bed shift and the floor take his weight and then the small silence of a man standing in the dark deciding whether to come. He did not come. The not-coming was the same not-asking, and I stood at the cold pane and let him not come, and the letting was the marriage, and the marriage was two people who had learned to leave each other the doors they would not open, and to stand on their own sides of them, and to call that standing a life.

The outline of a name is not a name. It is the shape of the not-having of one, and the shape was all I had, and the having of the shape was the sixteenth thing Edward would write, and the writing of it was the only mercy the books would give, because a man with a notebook can record a not-naming, and the recording is the one thing the law cannot strike. I put the slip with the letter on it back in the box with the third letter, and I closed the box, and the closing was not a putting-away but a keeping, and the keeping was the same keeping as the fold in the drawer, and the same keeping as the bread on the sill, and the sameness was the only family the struck have.

---

## Seventeen · The Coldest Read

I had read the margin. I had read the law. I had read the ledgers and the assessment and the statute. I had not yet read the name itself, the bare place where the letters had been lifted, because to read the name is to read the person, and the person at the gate had been warm, and the warm reads had been few, and I had been saving the coldest one.

I had been saving it the way a person saves the last of a thing she is afraid to spend, and the saving had gone on too long, and the saving had begun to feel like cowardice dressed as care. I set the rate-book on the table and opened it at the back and found the fifteenth line, and the line lay under the lamp the way it always lay, a pale seam in the page, the wood of the table showing through it, and I sat with my hand flat beside it and did not put my thumb to it yet. The lamp was low. Edward had gone to the far side of the room and had not asked what I was doing, because he knew, and the knowing was the reason he had gone to the far side, and the far side was the only courtesy he could offer a read he could not take for me.

The house was quiet. The fire had burned down to a soft red that made the room feel smaller and older. Outside, the wind had dropped, and the drop had left a silence that was not peace but the absence of a thing that had been pressing, and the silence made the lamp's small sound louder, the sound of a wick drawing oil. I put my thumb to the fifteenth line, on the thinned paper where the name had been scraped to translucency, and I let the read come the way you let a door open when you have already seen the hinge rusted and the frame rotted and the light on the other side is the light of a room that was someone's and is now no one's.

Three seconds.

Not the desk. Not the writer. The gate.

A woman, younger than the one at the basket, standing at the same gate I had seen, the same wood, the same hand on it as if she meant to open it or close it and had not decided. The wood was grey with weather and the gate hung a little and did not sit true in its frame, and her hand was on the top rail and her fingers had gone white at the knuckle with the cold, and she was not a foundling. She was not a pauper. She was a woman with a name that had been entered and struck, claimed by a man not her husband, settled elsewhere, and the striking had come, and she had stood at the gate with the ring that was not hers to keep, and the child the parish would not name, and she had looked past the reader, past me, at the house she had been told not to want.

The house beyond the gate was low and solid and had a good roof, and smoke came from its chimney, and the smoke was the ordinary smoke of an ordinary evening, and the ordinariness was the cruelty, because a house that goes on smoking is a house that has decided the woman at its gate is not its business. I could smell the smoke in the read, woodsmoke and bread, and under it the wet wool of her shawl and the cold iron of the gate, and I could hear a dog somewhere behind the house and a child, not the child at her side, another child, laughing at something in a yard I could not see. The ordinary sounds went on. The gate did not open. The hand on the rail did not lift.

And then the read did what the cold reads do. It promised a person and returned a wound. The woman did not speak. No one had given her the chance. The gate, the ring, the child, the look past me at the house, those were hers, and the name was not, and the lawfulness of the taking was written next to the empty space where she had been entitled to be, and the lawfulness was the coldest thing in the read because it was a mirror.

I had been given a second life because the first had not been used, and the not-using had been that no one asked, at the end, what I wanted. This woman had been struck from the register because a man not her husband had claimed her, and the claim had been lawful, and the lawful had cut her clean, and the clean cut had left her at the gate with a ring and a child and no name, and the not-naming had been the mercy and the cruelty both, and the both was the same both I lived in, the given-back and the not-asked, the kept and the crossed.

She was me, or I was her, or the law had made us the same shape with different dates, and the shape was the wound with no face, and the face was the law's, and the law's face was no face, and the no-face was the cruellest mercy, cutting clean, asking nothing, answering nothing. I had thought, before the read, that I was reading a stranger. The read had corrected me, and the correction was the coldest lesson of the book, and the lesson was that a woman who has been given back a life and a woman who has been struck from a book are the same woman under the law, because the law does not distinguish between the one it restores and the one it erases, and the not-distinguishing is the whole of its power, and the power is a kind of blindness, and the blindness is called fairness.

The three seconds ended. I let go of the line, and my hand stayed on the table edge a long time, because the read had promised a woman and returned a mirror, and the mirror was the coldest thing a cold read can give, which is not a stranger but yourself, scraped and left, lawful and taken, both at once. My hand pressed the table edge until the wood bit into the side of my palm, and the biting was welcome, because the biting was a thing the law had not arranged, and a small pain of my own choosing was the only proof that I was still on my side of the mirror.

Edward was across the room. He had not moved. He reads empties by not looking, and the emptiest thing in the room was the mirror the read had shown me, and he gave it the mercy of his not-seeing, which was the only mercy the law had not taken from me. I did not speak. The read had used up the words. The woman at the gate had been Tace, or a T with the rest taken, or a name the books would not write, and the not-writing was the wound, and the wound was mine now, because the mirror had made it so, and the making was the coldest read of the book, and the ratio held: one warm to two cold, and this had been the cold that the warm could not repay.

I sat with the lamp and let the room come back. The fire ticked. The wick drew. Edward turned a page in something he was not reading, and the turning was a small noise he made on purpose, so that I would know he was there without his having to say it, and the small noise was the kindest thing in the room. I thought about the dog behind the house and the other child laughing. The ordinary life beyond the gate had gone on with its smoke and its bread and its laughter, and the woman at the gate had stood in the cold and watched it, and the watching had been her whole crime, and the crime had been lawful to punish because the law had already decided she was not entitled to want the house. The gate was the law's idea of a boundary. It was a low gate, a gate a person could climb, and she had not climbed it. She had stood with her hand on the rail, and the not-climbing had been the last thing the law had left her, and she had kept it, and the keeping had been a dignity the register could not record and could not strike.

The child. I had not read the child, and the not-reading was a choice I had made without naming it, because a child is the one read that would have broken me, and I had been saving the child the way I had been saving the name, and the saving was a cowardice I was willing to own. The child at the gate had been small, and had held the woman's skirt, and had not looked at the house, because a child looks at the hand that holds it and not at the house it is not allowed to want. The child taken. Pettifer had said a child was taken, and the chandler had not mentioned a child, and the two silences together made a shape with a hole in it, and the hole was the child's, and no book had thought the child worth a line. A name scraped is a wound. A child unwritten is a wound with no place to put it, because a child who is not in a book is a child the parish does not have to feed or school or bury, and the not-having-to is the whole of the design, and the design was lawful, and the lawfulness was the part that let a parish sleep.

I thought about the other child, the one laughing in the yard beyond the house. That child had a name in a book, and a roof, and smoke, and a laugh that carried. The two children had been born within a mile of each other, and one had a line and one had a hole, and the difference between them was not love and not blood. It was a pen. A clerk had set the pen down at the empty slot, and the setting-down had decided which child the parish would feed and which child it would forget, and the clerk had been a careful man and not a cruel one, and the carefulness had been enough. That was the thing I could not get past. Cruelty I could have hated. Carefulness left me nothing to hold, no face to accuse, no hand to stay. A careful man had done it, and a careful man had done it lawfully, and a lawful careful man is the hardest thing in the world to forgive, because he has already forgiven himself.

I put my hand on the rate-book and left it there. The book was cold through the leather, and the cold was the cold of the gate, and the gate was the gate the child had held, and the holding had been the last warmth in the read, and the warmth had been the child's hand on the woman's skirt. I did not cry. I had not cried for a read since I was eighteen, and I was not going to start in a warm room with a lamp and a husband on the far side of it, because a tear is a thing that asks to be seen, and I had decided long ago that my grief would be a thing I did in private and spent in public, and the spending was the asking, and the asking was the only use I had.

Edward turned another page, the small noise he made on purpose. I let the noise be the thing that held me, and I sat with the cold book and the warm room and the hole where the child was, and I did not fill the hole, because a hole filled with a guess is a lie, and the only honest thing I could do for the child was to keep the hole open and not pretend it was full. The keeping of a hole is the hardest keeping there is. A hole does not fold. A hole is the shape of the not-having, and the not-having is the one thing the law is best at making, and the making is so clean that the world walks past the hole and calls the walking peace.

I put the rate-book away. The folded half-sheet was in the drawer, still folded. The woman at the gate was on paper, kept. The mirror was in my head, kept, the way a person keeps a wound they have decided not to show, because to show it would be to name it, and the naming is the one thing the lawful cutting was built to prevent, and the preventing was the cruelty, and the cruelty was the mercy, and the both was the only true thing.

The hand came off the table edge. The lamp was low. Edward had not moved. The seventeenth thing he would write was the read, and the read was a mirror, and the mirror was the coldest, and the coldest was the one that showed me my own face under the scraping, and the showing was the only warmth left, because a mirror, even a cold one, is a thing that looks back.

***

The knock came at the front, the door nobody used after dark, and Edward set the lamp down and did not get up, because the door was mine to answer and he had learned which doors I wanted opened.

A woman stood under the porch with her shawl over her head. The wet had gone into the wool and made it heavy, and she held a paper against her breast the way a person carries a plate.

"You are the one who reads."

"I am."

"Then read this."

She held it out. It had been folded small and opened again until the creases went soft and grey, and the writing was a line that stopped in the middle of a word.

"Whose is it?"

"My sister's. It was under the tray in her workbox. She is dead and there is no name on it."

"And you want the name."

"I want to know whether he wrote it kind."

The rain came off the porch roof in a line and beat on the step between us.

"Whose hand it is will tell you whether the hand was steady," I said. "It will not tell you whether the man was kind. A steady hand writes a cruelty as well as a kindness, and it writes the cruelty better."

"Then read it and tell me what you can."

"I cannot read it tonight."

She did not move. She looked past me at the lamp and the man in the chair, and the looking was the measure a person takes of a house before she asks it for something.

"They said you found a woman in a book."

"I found a gap in a book."

"A gap." She said the word the way a person sets down a coin she does not trust. "A gap is not a name."

I cut a piece of the loaf, because the hand goes to the loaf before the mind has settled, and I held it out to her.

"I did not come for bread."

"Take it anyway."

She came in as far as the table and laid the paper on it, squared to the edge the way a clerk squares a page, and turned and went out before I could lift my hand.

Edward had not moved. "She will come back," he said.

"She will not."

I took the paper up. The fold was warm from her shawl. I went out after her without my coat, and she was at the gate with her hand on the rail.

"Take it back."

"I cannot."

"You can. It is yours, and it is damp, and it will spoil."

She took it back. Her hand was cold. The lane went up behind her into the dark, and the gate was mine, and it hung true in its frame, and I had never thought about that until then.

"Come in the morning," I said, "and I will read it."

"In the morning I will have decided not to."

"Then decide it in the light."

She went up the lane. The paper went with her. I stood at my own gate until my hands were stiff on the wood, and then I went in and latched the door.

The paper stayed outside.

Before I slept I went to the drawer and put my hand on it without opening it. The folded paper was there. The want was there, folded, unfinished, and the cold read had not touched it, because the cold read was about the law and the want was about the self, and the two were the same shape and not the same thing, and the not-same was the only distinction I had left. I had been scraped and given back. She had been scraped and left. The difference between us was not virtue and not luck. It was that someone had decided I was owed a hearing, and no one had decided it for her, and the deciding had been done by a hand that could have decided otherwise, and the could-have was the whole of the injustice, because what could have gone the other way was chosen, and a chosen thing has a chooser, and a chooser can be asked.

I asked the dark, in my head, the question I could not ask her: who chose, and did he know your name when he chose. The dark did not answer. The dark was the gap, and the gap was lawful, and the lawfulness was the face with no face, and I lay in the warm room with the folded paper in the drawer and the mirror in my head and the question in my mouth, and I did not sleep for a long time, and when I did the gate was in the dream, and the hand was on the rail, and the smoke went up, and the gate did not open. I woke with my own hand on the drawer's edge, and the wood was cool, and the fold was still there, and the keeping was still the only thing I had.

---

## Eighteen · Rooke's Silence

Edward had the notebook open on his knee, and the pages had grown. I had watched them grow from the first entry, the read of the desk and the knife, to this, the twentieth, and the growing was the only thing in the house that moved at the pace of the asking, because he wrote only what I did, and I had done twenty things for the woman at the gate, and the twenty were a column that added to nothing, which is the only sum a silence has.

The fire was low and the room was warm at the edges and cold in the middle, the way a room is when one person has been sitting still in it a long time. He had his feet stretched toward the grate and the notebook propped on his knee, and the pen lay in the gutter of the pages where he had set it down, and he had not written for a while. I had been watching him not write. It is a different thing from watching a man write, and harder, because a man who is not writing is a man deciding whether to, and the deciding is the part you cannot see.

I asked him why he kept them. It was a question I had not asked before, because the asking would have been to ask why he watched me, and the watching was the mercy he gave, and the mercy was the one thing I did not want to name. A mercy named is a mercy that can be weighed, and a weighed mercy is a debt, and I had enough debts.

He did not look up from the page. "You asked me once why I write what you do. I said I write it because you move and the book sits. That was the first answer. Here is the second. Every question you have asked, I want to see whether you will get the answer I got when I asked mine."

"When did you ask yours?"

"Before I knew the asking was the only part that would not be answered." He turned the pen over in his fingers, once, and set it down again. "The audit office was new. I was a young man with a new office and a belief that a question is a thing a man can settle, the way he settles a column. A woman came to me. She had been turned out of a cottage that was hers by a paper that was not hers, and she had the paper, and the paper had a seal on it, and the seal was the parish's. I asked the parish. The parish showed me the law."

"And the law had no face."

"The law had a face. That was the worse thing. The face was a clerk's, and the clerk was polite, and the clerk explained the section and the subsection and the date, and every word he said was true, and when he had finished I could not find the place where the wrong was, because there was no wrong in any single word. The wrong was in the whole of it, and the whole of it was lawful, and a lawful whole made of true words is the one thing a young man cannot argue with." He looked at the fire and not at me. "I wrote the woman a letter that said the parish had answered, and the answer was lawful, and there was nothing to be done. I have never written a worse letter. I wrote it in the same hand I use for columns, and the hand did not shake, and that is the part I have not forgiven."

I sat with that. It was the most he had ever told me about the years before me, and he told it the way he did everything, flat, in order, without asking me to feel anything. The woman and the cottage and the seal. A young man with a new office and a belief. The belief had not been broken by a villain. It had been answered, politely, by a clerk with a section and a subsection, and the answering had been lawful, and the lawfulness was the thing that had done the breaking.

"You think I will get your answer," I said.

"I think you will get a lawful one. The lawful answer is the only kind a parish gives, and the giving is the cruelty, because a lawful answer is a thing you cannot argue with, and the not-arguing is the wound that does not bleed."

He had been right, and I had known it for a month. I had asked Tindle a question, who was the woman at the gate, and Tindle had shown me the law, and the law had a polite face and a section and a subsection, and the answer was that no one would name her, and the not-naming was lawful, and the lawfulness was the cruelty. I had wanted a villain. A villain would have been a relief. A villain can be hated and a hated thing can be fought, and a lawful answer cannot be fought because it has already won.

"I have not gotten your answer yet," I said.

"You have gotten the first part. The law. The face comes later, or it does not come, and the not-coming is the mercy, because a face would mean someone to blame, and someone to blame would mean someone to ask, and someone to ask would mean the asking had a point, and the point was the one thing the lawful answer took from both of us."

He said mercy, and I did not correct him, but I did not agree. A face would have been a mercy to me. A face would have given me somewhere to put the weight. The weight had been sitting in me since the first read, and it had no shape, because a weight with no shape cannot be set down, only carried, and I had been carrying it the way a woman carries a child on her hip, without thinking, and the not-thinking was the way a weight becomes a body.

I got up and went to the shelf and took down the rate-book and held it. The leather was cold. The fifteenth line was inside it, a pale seam, and the margin said the taking had been lawful, and the lawfulness had a face, and the face was a clerk's, and the clerk had been polite, and the politeness was the part that made me want to put my fist through the wall. I did not. I set the book back on the shelf and put my hand flat on the wood above it, and the wood was warm where the lamp had leaned, and the warmth was the only thing in the room that did not have to be argued with.

"You are angry," Edward said.

"I am past angry. Angry is a thing you can spend. This is a thing you have to keep."

"Yes," he said. "That is what I have been writing."

He opened the notebook again. The twentieth entry was there, and the twenty-first was not, because I had not yet done the twenty-first thing, and the not-doing was the silence, and the silence was not mercy and not cruelty but the material we were both working in, the way a carver works in wood. He turned the book toward me a moment, and I read the twentieth line, and it was not about the woman at the gate. It was about the clerk with the section and the subsection, and the letter he had written when he was young, and the hand that had not shaken. He had written his own worst letter into the book of my questions, and the writing of it was the closest thing to an apology I had ever been given, and he had given it without saying so, because a man who says so is a man asking to be forgiven, and he was not asking.

I closed the notebook for him. The cover was warm from his knee. "You have carried that letter forty years," I said.

"I have carried it the way you carry the rate-book. It is not mine to put down until someone asks me for it."

"Then I am asking."

He looked at me then, and his eyes were the colour of the river under cloud, and he did not answer at once. "No," he said at last. "You are not asking. You are offering. The asking would be to want the letter back, and you do not want it back. You want me to know you have seen it. That is a different thing, and it is a better thing, and I will not mistake it for the asking."

He was right, and the rightness was the cold kind, and I took it. I had offered, and the offering was the only coin I had that was not a question, and he had taken it and named it and not spent it, and the not-spending was the whole of him. I went to the drawer and touched the folded half-sheet through the wood. The fold was there. The want was not named, but it was kept, and the keeping was the midpoint, and the midpoint was not the end, and the not-end was the only thing I had that the law could not reach into and take.

Edward did not watch me touch the drawer. He reads empties by not looking, and the folded sheet was the fullest of them, and he left it alone. The fire settled. The lamp drew. The notebook lay closed on his knee with the twentieth entry in it and the twenty-first not, and the not-yet was the shape of the spring, and the spring had begun, and the beginning was a thing neither of us said.

I sat down again, not across from him but beside him, and the beside was its own kind of entry, the kind he would not write because it was not a thing I did for the woman at the gate. He moved his knee a little to make room. That was all. The room held the two of us and the closed book and the folded paper in the drawer, and the holding was not peace and not resolution but the third thing, the thing a house becomes when two people have decided to keep the same silence and to keep it together, and the keeping together is the only marriage I have ever understood.

***

He took the penknife off the shelf and a quill out of the box, and he turned the feather over on his knee and looked down the barrel of it the way a man looks down a lane to see whether it runs true.

"The old one has gone soft," he said. "A soft quill makes a fat letter."

"Show me how you cut it."

He cut it slow. The knife went in at an angle and took a curl off the end, and he tried the nib on his thumbnail and cut again, and the cut was shorter than the one before it.

"Give me the knife."

I cut too deep. The nib split up the barrel and the quill was firewood. Ink was on my thumb already, in a line along the crease of it, and the line would not come off until I washed.

"You cut towards the point," he said. "The nib is a thing you take from, not a thing you make."

He wiped the blade on his sleeve and gave me another feather. I cut it, and the nib came out blunt, and it would write a thin stroke and a fat one in the same word.

"Write with it."

"At the top?"

"At the top."

I dipped and wrote my name. The opening letter came out fat and the rest came out thin, and the name on the sheet was the name of a person who had learned to write late and in a hurry, which is what it was.

"Now write it under mine," I said.

He did not take the pen.

"Why not?"

"Because that is the one I would be practising on." He said it without heat, and he did not turn his head, and the not-turning was the courtesy he had. "I have put a name at the top of every letter I have written since that one, and every one of them was a stranger. A stranger cannot be practised on. A stranger is the whole of it."

"Then my name is the one name that will never stand at the top of anything you write."

"No." He took the pen out of my fingers and dipped it and wrote my name under mine in the hand he used for columns, small and level and in no hurry. "That is not a penance. That is a record."

The names sat on the sheet, mine fat and his level, and the space between them was the space a clerk leaves for a correction.

"Records are kept," I said.

"Records are kept."

He sanded it and tipped the sand off into the tray and blew the last of it away, and he folded the sheet along its own crease and held it out to me.

The drawer was cold. I put it in the drawer on top of the folded half-sheet and shut the drawer. The wood went home with a small sound. That was the whole of the entry the night would take.

Later, when the fire had been mended and the lamp turned low, I asked him the question I had not asked in all the months of the book. What was her name. The woman with the cottage and the seal.

He did not answer at once. He turned the cup in his hands, a quarter turn, and then another, and the turning was the thing he did when a memory would not add.

"I never knew it," he said. "That is the part I have not forgiven. She told me her trouble and not her name, and I did not ask, because I thought the name was not the business of the office. The office dealt in papers and seals and the names on them, and I thought a name was a thing a clerk copied and not a thing a woman was."

"So you wrote her a letter with no name in it."

"I wrote her a letter that said the parish has answered, and the answer is lawful. I did not put her name at the top because I did not have it. I have written a great many letters since, and I have put a name at the top of every one of them, and the putting of the name is the only penance I have, and it is a poor one, because a name at the top of a letter is not the same as a name a man troubled to learn."

I thought about that. The old clerk had written Tace small, in a private book, where no one would look. Edward had written a letter with no name at all, and had spent forty years putting names at the tops of letters to make up for it. The two men had done the same thing in opposite directions. One had hidden a name he knew. The other had spent a life learning names he had not troubled to learn. Both had been careful. Both had been lawful. And the woman in each case had gone on standing at her gate, unfed by the carefulness, fed only by the women who had done the unlawful small thing.

"That is why you write the notebook," I said.

"That is one reason."

"What is the other?"

He set the cup down. "Because you will not write it yourself, and someone must, and a thing written by a man who loves you is not the same as a thing written by the law. The law writes to close a case. I write to keep one open. A closed case is a name in a column that adds up. An open case is a name a man keeps saying until someone answers."

He said it without weight, the way he said most things, and the lightness of the saying was the thing that made it heavy, because a man who says a heavy thing lightly is a man who has carried it so long he has stopped noticing the weight. I put my hand on the notebook, on the worn corner where his thumb rested, and the worn corner was warm, and the warmth was the forty years of letters with names at the top, and the names were the only apology he had ever made, and he had made it to strangers because he could not make it to the one woman whose name he had never learned.

"Her name is not in the book," I said. "The book has twenty entries and none of them is hers."

"No. The book is yours. Hers is the letter I did not keep a copy of, and the copy I did not keep is the one thing I would give a year of my life to have back, and I have not been given the chance, and the not-being-given is the whole of what I deserve."

He said deserve, and I did not argue, because a man's deserts are his own to reckon, and the reckoning was the only private thing he had. But I thought, sitting beside him with my hand on the warm worn corner, that a man who has spent forty years putting names at the tops of letters has already served a sentence longer than any board would give, and the sentence was the kind a man serves standing up, in an office above a bakehouse, writing to strangers, and the strangeness of the strangers was the punishment, because a name written to a stranger is a name written to no one, and a name written to no one is the closest a careful man can come to writing the name he never learned. The closest is the only thing a careful man is ever allowed. He had taken it. The taking was the penance, and the penance was enough, and the enough was the thing he would never say out loud.

---

## Nineteen · The Name Beneath the Line

The clerk's descendant, the young man with the thin wrists, had a book of his own, not the parish's, a private thing he kept in the office and did not show, but he showed it to me because I had returned the rate-book and because the returning of it had made me a person who had been trusted with one dead thing and might be trusted with another. He was the grandson of the old clerk, the one who had set the pen down at the empty slot year after year, and he had the old clerk's hands and not the old clerk's silence, and the hands shook a little when he set the book on the table.

"He kept notes," he said. "Things he saw and did not write in the register. He said the register was the law's book and a man needed a book of his own, or the law would eat him."

The book was small and plain, the leather gone soft, the pages numbered in the old clerk's hand. The young man turned to the year of the war, the year the name had been struck, and there at the bottom of the page, beneath the entry for the fifteenth line, was a single word, written smaller than the rest, as if the writer had not wanted it to be read.

*Tace.*

Not a T with the rest taken. Tace. The whole of it. The name beneath the scrape, the name the law had struck, the name the parish had fed in turn, the name the seven loaves had kept alive on paper for forty years, and the name was Tace, and the having of it was the twenty-first thing Edward would write, and the writing of it was the only mercy the books would give, because a man with a notebook can record a name the register will not hold.

The word sat on the page in ink that had browned, and the brown was the colour of a thing kept too long in the dark, and the smallness of the letters was the old clerk's fear made visible, the fear of a man who had written a name where no one would look because to write it where people looked would have been to undo a lawful thing. I put my finger on the word and did not press. The page was dead, and I could have read it, and I did not, because the reading would have given me the old clerk's fear and not the woman, and I had enough fear of my own.

The clerk watched me. "You know her," he said.

"I know the shape of her. The name is the shape made plain. Tace."

"You say it as if it were a key."

"It is a key. The question is what door it opens, and whether the door should be opened."

He did not understand that, or he understood it and did not want to, and he took the book back and held it against his chest the way a man holds a thing that has been in his family too long. "My grandfather would not write it in the register. He wrote it here, where no one would look, because to write it there would have been to undo the scraping, and the scraping was lawful, and the lawful is not to be undone by a clerk."

"He was a careful man. He wrote the law and he wrote the name, each in its place, and the places were not the same."

"The name in the private book. The law in the margin. The woman at the gate in neither. That was his way. He said a clerk serves the law and a man serves the truth, and the two are not the same, and a man may serve the truth in a book no one reads."

I sat with that. It was the most honest thing anyone in Tindle had said to me, and it was said by a man twenty years dead, through a grandson who had inherited the honesty and not the office. A clerk serves the law and a man serves the truth. The old clerk had served both, in two books, and had kept them apart the way a man keeps two accounts that must never be added, because the adding would show a deficit no parish could bear.

I closed the private book. The name was Tace. The woman at the gate had been Tace, or married to a man of that name, or claimed by one, and the claim had been entered and struck, and the striking had been lawful, and the lawful had cut her clean, and the clean cut had left her at the gate with a ring and a child and no name in the register, only Tace in a book no one read.

I had the name. I could say it. I could go to the vestry and say the name Tace, and the saying would have been to undo the scraping, and the undoing would have been unlawful, because the lawful cutting is not to be undone by a woman with a rate-book. Or I could say it to the parish in a way that made the parish account for her, and the accounting would be lawful, and the lawful accounting would take her from the gate and put her in the one place the law keeps for a struck person, which is the gap.

I faced the choice the way you face a gate you have been told not to open. Say the name, and the parish must account for a woman it struck lawfully. Not say it, and the woman stays at the gate, fed in turn, unnamed in the register, Tace only in a book no one reads.

In the book before this one, I had chosen to say a name for my father, to keep his secret rather than the parish's, and the choosing had been to cross a thing out, his sin, struck from the record the way the law strikes a name. The crossing had been an ending. I had pressed the pen until the paper gave and I had drawn the line through and I had felt the line close a door, and the closing had been clean, and the clean had been what I needed then, because the thing I crossed out was a thing I could not carry and would not keep.

Here, I chose not to say. But the not-saying was not for him, as it had been for my father. It was for me. That was the difference, and the difference was the whole of the twenty-first entry, and the whole of the fold. To say the name Tace would have been to give the parish a person to account for, and the accounting would have been lawful, and the lawful accounting would have taken the woman from the gate and put her nowhere, because the law has no place for a struck person except the gap. The gap was the law's mercy and the law's cruelty at once, and I had learned to tell the two apart, and the telling was the thing I had been given two lives to learn.

I would not give her to the gap. I would keep her at the gate, the way old Mrs. Pettifer had kept her, the way my mother had kept my aunt, the way I had kept the folded half-sheet: not named, not finished, but kept. The keeping was not lawful. The keeping was a mercy the law could not see because it was not entered in a book, and a mercy not entered in a book cannot be struck, and the not-being-struck was the whole of its power. I had the name in my mouth and I did not say it, and the not-saying was not a silence. It was a keeping, and the keeping was a thing I did with my whole body, the way you hold a door shut against a wind, and the holding took strength I did not know I had.

I folded the thought the way I had folded the paper. The name Tace went into the drawer of my mind, kept, not said, not crossed. The carrier had changed: in the book before, I had crossed a thing out, ending it. Here I folded, keeping it. The fold was the self, the crossing had been the other. I had become the one who keeps rather than the one who ends, and the becoming was the twenty-first thing, and the thing was for me, not for her.

Edward wrote the twenty-first when I told him the name, and not the saying of it. "Tace," he said. "You will not say it to the parish."

"I will not give her to the gap."

"The gap is lawful. The keeping is not. You choose the not-lawful keeping."

"I choose the keeping. The lawful gave her to the gate. The keeping takes her no further, but it leaves her at the gate, fed, and the gate is a place, and a place is more than a gap."

He nodded. It was true, and the true was the only thing he would give weight to. He wrote the entry and did not read it back, which was his way of telling me the entry was mine and not his to check, and the not-reading-back was a courtesy he had never given me before, and I took it the way I took his tea, without saying so.

"You have kept a name before," he said. "For your father. You crossed it out and the crossing was a kind of keeping, because a thing crossed is a thing you have decided to carry closed. This is different."

"This is folded, not closed."

"Yes. A folded thing you mean to open. A closed thing you mean to leave closed. You have decided she is a thing to open again, and you have decided it without asking her, because you cannot ask her, because she is a name in a book no one reads."

He was right, and the rightness of it was cold, and I took it the way you take cold water. I had decided for her. The parish had decided for her. The law had decided for her. Every hand that had ever touched her had decided for her, and mine was only the latest, and the only difference was that my deciding was a keeping and not a striking. I did not know whether a keeping decided by a stranger was better than a striking decided by a law. I knew only that the striking had a face and the keeping did not, and a face can be asked, and the not-having-a-face was the part I could not forgive.

The clerk's grandson walked me to the door. He had not asked me what I would do with the name, and the not-asking was the first courtesy Tindle had given me that did not come with a price. At the door he said, "My grandfather used to say a name is a debt. He said a parish that strikes a name is a parish that has decided not to pay, and a parish that feeds the struck is a parish that pays in bread and calls the bread the whole of the debt. He said the bread is not the whole. The name is the whole, and the name is the one thing a parish will not pay."

"Your grandfather was a wise man," I said.

"He was a frightened man. The wisdom was the fright turned inside out." He held the door and looked past me at the street, where a cart was going by and a dog was following it. "You will not say it. I can see you will not say it. I have watched three people come to this office with the same look, and none of them said it, and I have decided the look is the name's way of staying alive. A name said is spent. A name kept is carried. My grandfather carried it for forty years in a book no one read, and the carrying was the only office he ever held that he did not hate."

The cart went by and the dog went after it, and the street was ordinary and did not know what had just been kept in it. I thanked him and went out, and the door closed behind me, and the closing was the sound of a parish keeping its own counsel, and the counsel was the counsel of a frightened man turned inside out.

***

The cart stopped at the top of the street, and the woman on it climbed down with a basket on her arm, and the boy handed down a sack and set it on the stones.

She was from the crofts. Her boots were bound at the ankle with cord, and the cord was wet, and the cloth over the basket had been washed until the pattern was a grey ghost of itself. She tried the latch of the office door, and the latch did not give, and she stood with her hand on it.

"He is at his dinner," the boy said.

"I have come the long way."

She came back down the street and set the basket on the sill of the office window and lifted the cloth, and there were loaves in it, floury at the top, the sort that are baked in a batch. Under the loaves was a paper, folded and gone furry along the fold.

"The clerk puts his mark on it," she said. "The overseer counts the marks. If it is not marked, the loaf is not counted, and if the loaf is not counted, they will say the turn has stopped."

"Then wait for the clerk."

"I have waited since the light came." She turned the paper over in her hands. "You have been inside. You have the hand for it."

The paper was a column of strokes, a stroke to a line, and at the head of each line a name in a hurried hand. At the bottom, a line had nothing at the head of it. The line was as long as the others, and the space above it was clean, and the stroke under the clean space had been made year after year by different hands.

The pencil hung from her apron on a piece of string. She put it in my hand and stood back.

I laid the paper on the stone. The wind lifted the corner and the boy put his thumb on it without being asked, and the thumb was black at the nail.

I made the stroke. It went down level with the others, and it was a good stroke, and the clean space above it stayed clean.

She looked at it a while. The cart waited.

"Somebody had a name," she said.

The pencil was still in my hand. The wood was warm from her apron.

"The office is shut," I said.

She folded the paper along its fur and put it under the loaves and drew the cloth over. She did not ask again. She took up the basket and went up the street, and the boy went after her with the sack, and the street was as it had been, and the office door stood shut.

***

I walked to the end of the street and stopped. The sky over Tindle was the colour of pewter, and the smoke went up straight, and the straightness was the same straightness I had seen from the bridge, and the sameness was the thing that made the town a town and not a wound, because a wound does not have smoke going up straight. I put my hand in my pocket and touched the slip with the whole name. Tace. The word was small under my thumb, smaller than the want, and the smallness was the old clerk's fear, and the fear had outlasted him, and the outlasting was the only thing fear was good for.

I had come to Tindle to ask who she was, and I had found out, and the finding had not made her a person, because a person is not a name. A person is a face and a hand and a voice and a habit of standing at a gate. I had the face from the read and the name from the book, and the two had not joined, and the not-joining was the gap, and the gap was lawful, and I had decided not to fill it. I would carry the two halves, the face and the name, and I would not join them, because the joining was the parish's business and the parish would join them only to put her in the gap. A face and a name kept apart are a person kept open. A face and a name joined by a lawful hand are a person closed.

That was the keeping. It was not much. It was a slip of paper in a pocket and a fold in a drawer and a name in a book no one read. It was not the undoing of the scraping, and it was not the bread on the sill, and it was not the board that gave a line back and not the years. It was the smallest kind of keeping, and I had learned from the women in my family that the smallest kind is the kind that lasts, because the law does not bother to strike a thing it cannot see.

I wrapped the rate-book. The name was Tace. The keeping was mine. The fold was the self. And the self was the one thing the lawful cutting could not take, because a self is not a name in a book, and the not-being is the only freedom the law cannot strike.

That night I wrote the name on a slip of paper, small, the way the old clerk had written it, and I did not put it in the rate-book and I did not put it in the drawer with the folded want. I put it in my pocket, next to the T, and the two slips sat together in the pocket, the letter and the whole name, and the whole name was heavier than the letter, and the heaviness was the difference between a doubt and a keeping. I had the name. I would carry it. I would not spend it. A name carried and not spent is a name kept, and I had learned from the women in my family that the kept things outlast the spent ones, and the outlasting was the only vengeance a person could take on a lawful cutting, and the vengeance was not vengeance at all. It was patience. It was the fold. It was the hand that stays above the paper and does not write the rest.

---

## Twenty · The Open Door

The fire had burned to a shell again, and I was awake past the hour I ought, and Edward was beside me, and the notebook was on the floor where it had fallen from the chair, and the day's twenty-first entry was written, and the twenty-second was not, because I had not yet done the twenty-second thing, and the not-doing was a silence I had begun to live inside the way a person lives inside a coat too large for her.

I turned to him. The marriage had given me the right to turn, and the turning was a thing no law could strike, because a wife is not a name in a book. The sheet was cold where I had been lying and warm where he was, and the warmth was the only thing in the room that had been made on purpose for me, and the making-on-purpose was the whole of what he did.

He was awake. He always was, when I turned. He did not pretend sleep, the way a stranger might. He met the turning as he met a column that would not add, with a steady attention to the part that was wrong, and the part that was wrong was the part of me that could not say the word.

He said something. It was about the audit, or the bounds, or the north field, a thing procedural, a thing he said because the saying of it was the only language he had for the moment, and the moment was not procedural, and the not-procedural was the wall between us. He talked about a gate that needed mending, and about a boy from the village who had asked for work, and about the price of nails, and his voice went on in the dark, low and even, the voice of a man who has decided that if he keeps talking about nails he will not have to say the other thing.

I did not listen to the words. I listened to the voice, and the voice was the same one that had read the desk and the knife and the margin, and the reading had been of dead things, and I was not dead, and the not-dead was the only freedom, and the freedom was the turning, and the turning was the open door, and the door was not a confession.

He stopped the procedural thing when he understood that I was not listening. He had that skill, the one I did not: he stopped when the words were not the point, and the stopping was the mercy, because a man who stops when the words fail is a man who reads the empty space and does not fill it with nails.

His hand came to my face. The hand was the same one that held the pen and wrote the entries and set the notebook aside, and the holding was the only language that did not fail, because a hand on a face is not a sentence, and the not-sentence was the point. His palm was warm and a little rough at the base of the fingers, and it covered my cheek and part of my jaw, and the covering was not a claim. It was a question asked in the only grammar he trusted.

I leaned into the hand. The leaning was the want, kept, not named, the way the folded half-sheet was kept, and the keeping was the midpoint, and the midpoint was not the end. His thumb moved once along my cheekbone and stopped, and the stopping was deliberate, the way his pen stopped at the end of a line, and the deliberateness was the thing that undid me, because a man who stops is a man who has decided not to take more than he is given, and the deciding was the whole of his desire and the whole of his restraint at once.

He did not ask. The question that would have been *you, then, what do you want* sat in the dark between us and he did not say it. He had asked it once, in another dark, at the end of the winter before this one, and I had answered him with work. I want to find out what that book is, I had said, and I want to be the one who finds it and not the woman who hands it to a man in a wig. He had taken that for an answer, because it was true and because it was the one I could say, and he had not pressed for the one under it, and I had let him not press. He had never asked the one underneath. He read the empty space and left it open, the way he left the notebook open at the page where the answer was not yet written, and the not-writing was the mercy, and the mercy was the only thing that had ever made me want to answer.

Someone had handed me a second life on the grounds that I had not spent the first, and the not-spending had been that no one asked, at the end, what I wanted. In this life I had a husband who did not ask, and the not-asking was not the same as the not-being-asked, because his not-asking was a mercy and theirs had been a cutting, and the cutting was lawful and the mercy was not, and the not-lawful was the only freedom.

I put my hand over his on my face. Then I moved his hand, not away but down, to the hollow of my throat, and the moving was the only sentence I could make, and the sentence was the open door, and the door was not a confession. He understood the sentence. He had always understood the sentences I made with my hands, and the understanding was the marriage, and the marriage was two people who had learned to speak in the grammar of the body because the grammar of the mouth had failed them both.

What happened after was not a thing I would write down, and it was not a thing he would write down, and the not-writing of it was the only privacy we had that the law could not enter. The fire shell cracked and the light went up the chimney and the room was dark and then less dark, and his hands were on me and my hands were on him, and the wanting was old and familiar and the saying of it was not, and the not-saying was the wall, and the wall stood in the middle of the bed and neither of us climbed it and neither of us turned away. He was careful with me the way he was careful with everything, with a care that was not coldness, and I was not careful at all, and the not-careful was the only thing I had that was entirely my own. When it was over he held me the way a man holds a thing he is afraid will break and knows will not break, and the holding was the only answer either of us could give, and the answer was not a word.

The fire died. His hand was still on my back, flat, warm, the weight of it the weight of a hand that had written twenty-one entries about a woman it had never met and had done it for the woman in the bed. He took his hand away when the fire died, and the taking-away was not a withdrawal. It was the same stopping, the same not-taking-more, and I lay in the cooling room and let the warmth of him fade off my skin, and the fading was slow, and the slowness was the point.

I lay awake after. The half-sheet was in the drawer, folded, kept. The notebook was on the floor, twenty-one entries for a woman no one would name. The open door had been the want, kept, not confessed. And the not-confessing was the wall, and the wall was the one thing neither the law nor the marriage could take down, and the not-taking was the only freedom, and the freedom was mine.

In the morning he was up before me, and the fire was mended, and the kettle was on, and he had set the cup at my place without a word about the carrying of it. I sat down and drank, and the tea was the cheap black he liked, and the warmth of it went down and settled, and the settling was the only thing that had changed and the only thing that had not. He read his letter. I read the rate-book's margin, the four words, and the four words had not changed either, and the not-changing was the whole of the problem, and the whole of the problem was the thing we would have to go on living inside, and the living-inside it was not a failure. It was the marriage.

He looked up once, over the letter, and his eyes were the colour of the river under cloud, and he did not say the thing, and I did not say the thing, and the two not-sayings met in the middle of the table and held, and the holding was the only conversation we had ever been able to finish.

***

He rose and took his coat off the hook, and the coat was the brown one, and the lining had gone at the cuff. He put his arm in. The cuff came away at the seam, and the button went down on the flags and rolled to the fender and stopped.

The boy was in the yard with the horse and a sack of nails, and the horse put its head over the gate and took the frost off the top rail with its lip.

"Leave it," Edward said.

I took the mending box down from the dresser. The lid was warped and came up with the sound of wood letting go of wood, and the thread in it was wound on a card. The thread was grey.

"Hold out your arm."

He held it out. The fire was behind him and the light came round the edge of him, and the coat smelled of the field and of the smoke of the house and of wool that has been wet and dried too often.

I put my hand flat on his chest to hold the cloth steady. The wool was cold. Under my palm it warmed, and the warmth came up through the cloth, and my hand knew the shape beneath it the way it had known the shape in the dark.

He held still. He did not say it. He said, "The thread is grey."

"The coat is brown. It will serve."

"It will serve."

The needle was blunt. I wetted the end of the thread and pinched it and put it at the eye, and it would not go, and I wetted it again. It went. The boy turned his back and studied the wall of the shed with his hands behind him.

I sewed the button on with the cloth pulled tight, and the tightness made a small gather in the sleeve. The gather would show. A thing that shows is honest work.

"You are pulling too hard," he said.

"It will hold."

"Then pull hard."

The thread went through and round and through, and the button came up firm against the cloth. I wound the shank and bit the thread off close. The end of it lay on my tongue. Bitter with the dye.

He turned his arm. He looked at the button the way he looked at a column, for whether it added.

"It will do for the field," he said.

"It will do for the field."

He took his hat off the peg and went out, and the boy held the horse while he got up, and they went down the lane together. The horse's feet were loud on the frozen ground. Then they were not.

I stood in the door with the mending box in my hand. The fire took the chill off the room behind me. The gate stood open. The frost was gone from the top rail where the horse had taken it.

***

After he had gone out I cleared the cups and swept the hearth and stood a while at the window that would not open. The morning was bright and cold and the frost had not lifted from the yard, and the brightness was the kind that does not warm, and I stood in it and tried to answer the question he had not asked.

What do you want.

I said it to myself the way I said the words on the paper, plainly, without expecting an answer. The wanting came back as a list of other people. I wanted the woman at the gate to have a name in a book. I wanted the child to have a line. I wanted Edward's letter to the woman with the cottage to have her name at the top of it, forty years late. I wanted my mother to have been asked, once, before she died, what she wanted, and I wanted to have been the one who asked her. Every want I could find was a want for someone else, and the ones for myself were not wants at all but absences, the shape of a thing that had never been put in me because no one had ever put it there.

I thought about the night before. I had put his hand to my throat and he had understood, and the understanding had been the whole of the conversation, and the whole of it had been true and had not been enough. A body can say a thing a mouth cannot, and the thing the body says is not the same thing. The body says now. The mouth says what. I had given him now, and he had taken now, and the what sat in the drawer in three words and a half, and neither of us had opened it.

I crossed to the drawer and laid my hand on it. The wood was warm from the fire. I did not open it. I stood with my hand on the warm wood and let the question be the question, and the question was not a failure, and the not-opening was not a refusal. It was the same patience Edward had shown me, turned around and used on myself, and the using was harder than the receiving, because a person can accept patience from another and call it love, but a person who is patient with herself is a person who has decided she is worth the waiting, and I had not decided that, and the not-deciding was the oldest thing in me, older than the second life, older than the first.

I thought about my mother's coat, which I had worn all winter, and about the way she had poured tea for a man who did not look up, and about the way she had mended a dead man's shirt for a wife who could not. She had wanted things. Everyone wants things. But she had put her wants where the parish could not see them, and she had done it so well that when she died no one could have said what they were, and I had inherited the putting-away the way I had inherited the coat, without being told, and the inheritance was the only thing she had left me that the law could not strike.

I took my hand off the drawer. The morning was going on outside, and the boy from the village was in the yard with the gate that needed mending, and I could hear the knock of his hammer, and the knock was ordinary and steady and asked nothing. I put on my coat and went out to him, and I held the gate while he set the pin, and the holding was a small service and no one's business, and the pin went in true at the second try, and the boy said thank you, ma'am, and I said the gate had needed it a while, and the saying was ordinary, and the ordinariness was the only thing that had held all winter.

When I came in Edward's notebook was on the table where he had left it, closed, and I did not open it. I put my hand on the cover and felt the worn corner under my palm, and the worn corner was the twenty-one entries and the blank twenty-second, and the blank was the question he had not asked, and the question was the same as the one in the drawer, and the two blanks were the same blank, and the same blank was the shape of the marriage.

That was the account of the night and the morning, and it did not add, and the not-adding was the thing we lived in, and the living-in-it was not the same as giving up. A person can live in a house with a door she has not opened and still call it a house. A person can love a man who has not asked her the one question and still call it love. The calling is not a lie if you know what you are calling it, and I knew, and the knowing was the only honesty I had left, and the honesty was the fold, and the fold was the only thing that was entirely mine.

I thought about the difference between a wall and a shelter. The wall between us was the not-saying, and I had called it a wall all winter, and a wall is a thing that keeps people apart. But a wall is also the thing a house is made of, and a house is a shelter, and the shelter had kept me warmer than any open field of saying would have. The not-saying had held us the way a wall holds a roof. It was not the enemy. It was the material. I had been treating it as the enemy because an enemy can be fought, and the material cannot be fought, only lived in, and the living-in is the harder thing and the only thing that lasts.

Edward came back at noon with the boy's wages in his pocket and the gate mended behind him, and he set the wages on the shelf and did not say a word about the mending, and the not-saying was the same as the mending, done and withdrawn. I gave him his dinner and he ate it and read a letter and did not look up, and I sat across from him and watched him not look up, and the watching was the whole of the marriage, and the marriage was the whole of what I had, and what I had was more than most women were given and less than I had been promised by nothing and no one. I had promised myself a second life. I had not promised myself a second happiness, and the not-promising was the honesty, and the honesty was the fold.

---

## Twenty-One · The Twenty-First

The folded half-sheet was in the drawer, and I touched it through the wood the way you touch a scar you have decided not to show, and the touching was not a want to open it. It was the other thing, the thing a person does to be sure a kept thing is still kept. The wood was cool and the paper under it was dry and folded, and the fold had begun to soften at the edge where my hand had found it so many times, and the softening was the only wear the paper had taken, because I had not opened it, and I had not burned it, and I had not crossed it. I had folded it, and the fold was holding.

I did not open it. The opening would have been to finish the sentence, and the finishing would have been to name the want, and the naming was the one thing I had been given two lives not to do. I had written three words and a half and stopped, and the stopping had become a kind of answer, and the answer was that the asking was the want, and I could not put the asking on a piece of paper because the paper would have made it a demand, and a demand is a thing another person can refuse, and I had been refused enough for two lives.

Edward had the notebook on his knee. He had been writing through the night, in the pauses between the things I did, and the pages had grown to the place where the questions ran out, because I had asked the questions and he had written them, and the questions were a column that added to a silence, and the silence was the only sum that had ever come out even for us. The cover of the notebook was worn at the corner where his thumb rested, and the wear was the record of the writing, the way the soft edge of the folded paper was the record of the keeping.

He wrote the last line. I did not read the words. I read the hand, and the hand was the same one that held the pen and wrote the entries and set the notebook aside, and the holding was the only language that had never failed between us, because it did not need to be translated and it could not be misheard.

"The twenty-first," he said.

"The last."

"The last of this book. The next book is for the one you do not ask."

I sat with that. Twenty-one. I had not counted them as they came. I had counted the reads and the goings and the fold, and the fold had been the fifteenth, and after the fold there had been the finding of the name, and the keeping of it, and the night I did not open the drawer, and each of those had gone into the book without my numbering them. Twenty-one questions about a woman who had been struck from a register, and a twenty-second that he had not written, and the twenty-second was the one I had not asked, and the not-asking was the wall, and the wall was the thing neither the law nor the marriage had been able to take down.

He closed the notebook. The closing was the same not-asking he had given me from the first, and the not-asking was not coldness. It was the way he had learned to hold a thing without crushing it. A man who asks a question he knows will not be answered is a man who has decided his asking is worth more than the other person's silence, and Edward had decided, somewhere in the years before me, that his asking was not worth more than anyone's silence, and he had kept the deciding, and the keeping was the whole of his love, and the whole of his love was a closed notebook and a cup of tea set at my place and a fire kept in against a night he did not name.

The last line of the notebook was the question I had asked him, the one I had not answered, the one he had not asked back. Beneath it, the space was blank. He had left it blank, the way he left the notebook open at the page where the answer was not yet written, and the blank was not an emptiness. It was a held breath. It was the page a man leaves for a thing he believes will come, and the believing was the only faith he had, and the faith was in the blank and not in the words.

I had asked him a question once, before this book, about the dead and the use and the thing that had been taken and not given back. He had not answered it. He had written it down.
