The writing had been the answer, or the beginning of one, and the beginning had been enough for a while, and the while had run out, and the blank under the twenty-first line was the place the rest of the answer would go when I was ready to ask it in a way he could answer. I was not ready. The readiness was the thing the fold was for.

I put my hand on the notebook. It was warm from his knee, and the warmth was the warmth of a thing that has been held a long time by one person, and the holding had made it his, and the making-it-his was the only claim he had ever staked in the house. "You have written twenty-one things about a woman you never met," I said.

"I have written twenty-one things about you," he said. "The woman is the occasion. You are the subject. I have been writing you since the first entry, and the first entry was a scraped line in a parish book, and the scraped line was the first thing you ever asked me to look at that was not a column."

I did not answer that. There was no answer that did not cost more than it gave. He had said the true thing, and the true thing was the kind that sits in a room and waits to be either taken up or left, and I left it, and the leaving was not a refusal. It was the same fold, the same keeping, the same hand above the paper.

The book had changed. Not the rate-book on the shelf. The book in my head. A scraped name had been a mystery, then a confession, then a mercy, then a procedure, then a name kept in a private book no one read, and now it was a question left blank in a notebook that would not be filled in this book. The changing was the only warmth, because a thing that changes is a thing still alive, and the alive was the one thing the lawful answer had not been able to reach, because a life is not a line in a register and cannot be struck by a clerk with a section and a subsection.

I crossed to the window. The night was clear and the yard was white with frost, and the frost made the dark look deeper than it was, and the deepness was restful because it asked nothing. I put my hand on the cold pane and thought about Tace at the gate, and the hand on the rail, and the child's hand on the skirt, and the smoke going up from a house that had decided she was not its business. I had her name in my pocket and her face in my head and her child in a hole no book had thought worth a line, and I had kept all three, and the keeping was the only thing I had done that the law could not undo. It was not enough. It was the only thing there was.

Edward came and stood behind me, not close, and the not-close was the distance he kept so that I would not have to ask him to keep it. "You will take the folded paper with you," he said.

"I will."

"Not to open it."

"Not to open it. To have it."

"Then that is the twenty-second thing," he said, "and I will not write it, because it is not for the book."

He went back to his chair and sat, and the fire was almost out, and the room was going cold at the edges, and neither of us moved to mend it. The cold was honest. It came from the weather and not from a lawful hand, and I had learned to prefer the weather's cold, because the weather does not pretend to be kind.

I sat with the closed notebook and did the sum I had been avoiding. Twenty-one. The first had been the read of the desk and the knife, written the day the rate-book came, and the twenty-first had been written the night I kept the name and did not say it, and between the two there had been a winter, and a name, and a fold, and a night I did not open the drawer, and a morning I did not say the name to the parish. Twenty-one entries for a woman named Tace in a book no one read. If a stranger opened the notebook and read it, the stranger would think the entries were about a parish and a law and a scraped line. The stranger would be wrong. The entries were about the shape of a woman who had been given back and did not know what to do with the giving, and the woman at the gate was the occasion, and the occasion was the only thing that let me look at myself without flinching, because a woman who looks at herself directly sees nothing but the want she cannot name, and a woman who looks at another woman's wound can see the shape of her own.

"Twenty-one," I said. "Why twenty-one? Why not twenty, or thirty?"

"Because twenty-one is where the questions ran out." He had his hands on his knees, and his hands were still, and the stillness was the stillness of a man who has finished a long piece of work and does not yet know whether it is good. "A notebook ends where the questions end. When you begin to ask again, the next book begins. I have kept the third book four years, and it has taken me from the wedding to the gate, and the gate is where the questions ran out, because the last question is the one you will not ask, and a book cannot hold a question that is not asked."

"The last question."

"Yours. Not hers. Hers are all in the book, every one of them, the read and the law and the name and the fold. Yours is the one you have not put to me. The blank under the twenty-first line is where it goes, and I have left it blank because I will not write a question for you. A question written by the man who is supposed to answer it is not a question. It is a trap with a kind face."

I looked at the blank. It was a small space, the height of two lines, and the emptiness of it was not the emptiness of a thing missing. It was the emptiness of a thing held. A held breath is not a breath that has stopped. It is a breath waiting for the word that will let it out, and the word was mine to say, and I had not said it, and the not-saying was the wall, and the wall was the one thing the notebook could not write down.

"I will not ask it tonight," I said.

"I did not think you would."

"Does that make you sorry."

He considered it, the way he considered a column, for whether it added. "No," he said. "A question asked too soon is a question a person answers with the first thing that comes, and the first thing is usually a lie a person tells to be rid of the asking. I would rather wait for the true thing. I have waited forty years for one true thing. I can wait a while longer for another."

He said it without heat, and the without-heat was the thing that made me believe him, because a man who wanted the question answered would have pressed, and he had never pressed, and the not-pressing was not indifference. It was the longest patience I had ever been shown, and I had been shown a great deal of patience by women who fed the struck and did not name them, and his patience was the male version of theirs, quieter and less useful and just as stubborn.

I got up and went to the shelf and took down the rate-book and held it against my chest. The boards were warped and the corners were chewed and the fifteenth line was a pale seam inside it, and the book was the only thing in the house that had come to me without my asking, and it had brought a name and a law and a fold and a blank page, and it had cost me a winter I would not have spent otherwise. I did not regret the winter. The winter was the first season of my second life in which I had done a thing that was entirely mine, and the entirely-mine was the fold, and the fold was the only thing the law had not been able to reach.

"I will carry it," I said. "The folded paper. Not to open. To have."

"I know."

"And when the next book begins, it will begin with the question I have not asked."

"It will begin with the question you have not asked," he said, "and I will not write it until you do, and the not-writing will be the first entry, because a book that begins with a blank begins honestly."

***

The knock at the back came while the fire was low, and it was the carrier's boy with a basket on his hip and his cap pulled to his eyebrows, and the cloth over the basket was stiff as a board.

"From the inn," he said. "The mistress says the washing was hung too late and has taken the frost, and you are to have it, and she is sorry for the creases."

He set the basket on the flags inside the door and stood with his hands in his armpits.

I took the cloth off. The linen was white and hard, and the top sheet had frozen along a fold, and the fold stood up out of it.

"Put your hands by the chimney," I said. "There is a little heat in the brick."

He put them there and held them and said nothing, and his knuckles were cracked and the cracks were black with the cold.

I took the sheet out by the corners. It did not bend. I laid it over the end of the table and it lay with the fold standing, and I put my palm on the fold and pressed, and the frost went into my hand and the fold did not give.

"You are to hang it in the steam," the boy said. "That is what she said. Hang it in the steam and do not press it."

"There is no steam. The fire is low."

"Then it will not come out," he said, and he said it the way a boy says a thing he has been given to say, without knowing what it weighs.

I folded the sheet along the fold that was in it. It went down flat that way and would not go down any other, and the crease came sharp at the edge of the cloth, and the sharpness was the weather's, made in a night, and the weather does not argue with a hand.

"There is the heel of a loaf in the crock," I said.

He looked at the crock and then at the door and then at the crock.

"Take it," I said. "And take the basket. Tell her the sheet is folded, and the crease is hers and not mine."

He put the bread inside his coat and took up the basket and went out, and the door came to, and the cold he had brought in stayed after him and made the room smaller.

Edward had not turned from the notebook. The pen did not stop for the knock, or the boy, or the bread, and the not-turning was the same courtesy he had given the drawer, and it cost him something to give it.

I put my hand on the folded sheet. The cloth was cold and the crease was hard under my palm, and the crease would be in the sheet when the sheet was dry, and pressing would not take it out.

***

The fire was out. The room was cold. I put the rate-book back on the shelf and stood a moment with my hand on it, and the leather was cold, and the cold was the weather's cold and not the law's, and I was glad of it. Then I went to bed and lay in the dark with my hand on the drawer's edge, and the fold was under the wood, and the blank was in the notebook, and the two things were the same thing, and the same thing was the only thing I had that was entirely mine.

I blew out the lamp. In the dark the drawer was a shape I knew by the hand and not by the eye, and the notebook was a weight on his knee, and the rate-book was a weight on the shelf, and the three things together were the only estate I had that the law had not entered. A folded want, a closed book with a blank page, a scraped line. I lay down in the dark and put my hand on the drawer's edge, and the wood was cool, and the fold was under it, and the fold was a promise I had made to a want I could not yet name, and the promise was the only thing in the house that was entirely mine.

---

## Twenty-Two · Who Asked

The name was Tace. The keeping was mine. The fold was the self. And the self led backward, the way a thread leads to the hand that held it, and the hand that had held Tace's name had been a person who asked her, once, what she wanted. That was the thing I could not leave alone. The law strikes and does not ask. The parish feeds and does not ask. The clerk writes and does not ask. But somewhere in the forty years there had been a hand that asked, and the asking had left a mark on the whole case, and the mark was the reason the woman had been fed instead of driven out, and the reason was a person, and a person can be found.

I traced the asking the way Edward traced a column, from the bottom up, checking each figure against the one above it. The chandler's grandfather had written the margin: she was claimed by a man not her husband. The claim had been entered. The entry had been struck. The striking had been by order. An order is a piece of paper, and a piece of paper has a hand behind it, and the hand had come from somewhere above the clerk. I went back to the vestry and asked for the order book, and the young clerk with the thin wrists brought it, and the order was there, in a hand I had not seen before, and the hand was not the clerk's and not the scraper's. It was a woman's hand. The letters were fine and upright and pressed evenly, the hand of a person who had been taught to write well and had never had to write in a hurry, and beside the order was a note in the same hand: the woman is not to be turned out; the parish will keep her in the turning; see that she is fed.

The parish will keep her in the turning. That was the asking. Not a question written down. A decision made by a person who had asked, somewhere, in a room that kept no record, what the woman wanted, and had been told, and had decided the parish would feed her rather than drive her. I sat with the order book open and put my thumb to the note, and the paper was dead and gave me nothing but cold and the smell of dust, and the cold was enough, because the cold was the cold of a hand that had written a thing against the grain of the law.

Who was she. The order was signed with an initial and a surname, and the surname was not a Tindle name, and the initial was an E. I asked the young clerk, and he did not know, and he asked the vestrymen, and they did not know, and the not-knowing was too quick and too smooth, the way a thing is not-known when it has been agreed not to be known. I did not press. I had learned that a parish's not-knowing is a wall, and a wall pressed too soon closes tighter. I wrote the initial and the surname on a slip and put it in my pocket with the T and the Tace, and the three slips sat together, and the pocket was getting heavy with the names of people who had done a thing and not been thanked for it.

That night I took out the third letter. It was the letter from beyond the bridge, the one that had come a hundred and eleven miles and said a thing I had not answered, and I had read it twice already and put it back, and this time I read it with the order book in my head. The hand in the letter was old and fine and upright, and the letters leaned the way the order's letters leaned, and the leaning was the same. I put the letter down and sat very still. The hand that had ordered Tace fed was the hand that had written the third letter. The person who had asked Tace what she wanted was the person beyond the bridge, the one my aunt had known, the one whose name I had been carrying without knowing I carried it.

I was one step from the whole of it. The name Tace. The asker at the bridge. The reader at the end. The three were one, or they were three people in a line, and the line ran from a woman who asked a struck woman what she wanted to a woman who would stand at a bridge and a woman who would read me at the end of the last book. I did not know which. I knew only that the line was there, and that I had come to the end of what the books of Tindle could tell me, and that the rest of the line ran into books I had not read and a life I had not lived.

I could have pointed. I had the initial and the surname and the hand, and a woman with a tongue and a second life could have pointed, and the pointing would have been a kind of justice, and justice was the thing I had been given a second life to want. But the pointing would have named a person the book before this one had not named, and the naming was not mine to do. The book before this one had kept the asker at a bridge and had not said her name, and the book after this one would say it, and the saying was the next book's mercy, and a mercy taken too soon is not a mercy. It is a theft dressed as a gift.

Edward wrote the twenty-second when I told him the asker was the same as the bridge and the end. He did not write the name. He wrote the shape of the not-pointing, and the shape was a line with a gap in it at the far end, and the gap was deliberate, and the deliberateness was the only thing he and I had ever agreed on without speaking.

"The book after this one," he said.

"The book after names her. This one does not."

"This one keeps her. The keeping is the mercy. The naming is the next book's."

He was right, as he usually was, and the rightness was the cold kind, and I took it. I had learned the difference from the two women who had kept a struck woman alive without naming her. A keeping that names too soon is a keeping that spends the thing it means to keep. Mrs. Pettifer had not named Tace. My mother had not named my aunt. The old clerk had written the name small, in a book no one read, which is a way of not naming it out loud. The whole parish had kept its mercy in the subjunctive, in the conditional, in the not-quite-said, and the not-quite-said was the only form a mercy could take in a place where the law had a section and a subsection for everything.

I wrapped the rate-book. The name Tace was kept in a book no one reads. The asker was kept at the bridge. The reader was kept at the end. The three were a line, and the line was a thread, and the thread ran out of Tindle and into the country beyond the bridge, and I would follow it when the time came, and the time was not this book. This book had done what it could. It had found the name and kept it and not spent it, and the not-spending was the hardest thing I had ever done, harder than the reads and harder than the fold, because a woman with a name in her pocket and a second life in her body wants to spend the name the way a rich man wants to spend his money, and the not-spending is the discipline the second life was for.

I went to the bridge that afternoon, before the light went. I had not stood on it since the day I carried the rate-book over, and the standing was a thing I had been avoiding without admitting it, because a bridge points, and pointing was what I had decided not to do. The bridge was old and the parapet was low and the river went under it brown and fast with the thaw, and I stood at the middle and put my hands on the stone and looked at the far bank.

The far bank was a line of willows and a road going up, and the road went to a parish I had not walked, and the parish held the asker, and the asker held the end of the thread. I could have crossed. The bridge was open and the road was dry and nothing but my own decision stood between me and the far bank, and the decision was the thing the whole book had been about, because a woman who has been given a second life is a woman who can cross any bridge she likes and is afraid of the ones that would ask her a question.

I had crossed a bridge before. I had carried my aunt's register over a bridge to a board, and the carrying had been a crossing, and the crossing had cost me a year, and the year had been the first year of my second life, and I had spent it the way a person spends money she is afraid of losing, carefully, in small amounts, and the carefulness had not made the spending any less. The bridge under my hands was the same bridge or a bridge like it, and the far bank was the same far bank or a bank like it, and the difference was that this time I knew what a crossing cost, and the knowing made me slower, and the slowness was not cowardice. It was the same patience I had learned from the women who fed the struck. A thing crossed too soon is a thing you cross twice.

I took the third letter out of my pocket and read it on the bridge. The wind took the paper and I held it flat against the parapet, and the hand in it leaned the way the order's hand leaned, and the letter said what it had said the first time and the second: a thing about a kept woman and a mercy and a debt no one had paid. I had not answered it. I had carried it a hundred and eleven miles in a box and then in a pocket, and the carrying was a kind of answer, the only kind I had, and the kind was not a word. A letter carried and not answered is a letter kept open, and a letter kept open is a question still asking, and the asking was the thing I did best and the thing I was most afraid of, and the fear and the skill had been living in the same hand all winter.

A cart went over the bridge behind me and the boards shook under my feet, and the shaking was the only ordinary thing in the afternoon, and I let it be ordinary. I put the letter back in my pocket. I did not cross. I stood at the middle of the bridge until the light went down the far bank and the willows turned black, and then I turned and walked back to Collow, and the walking-back was a decision, and the decision was not a refusal. It was a keeping. The thread would keep. The bridge would keep. The asker at the far end of the thread had kept a woman fed for forty years without naming her, and the keeping was the thing I was learning, and the learning was slow, and the slow was the fold.

***

The road back from the bridge ran with the river on the left, and the carrier's cart was in it, stopped, the near wheel down in the wet to the axle. The boy had his shoulder in the spokes and his boots in the mud and was getting nothing for it.

"You have the weight on the wrong side," I said. "It goes deeper."

He came out of the spokes and stood back and wiped his forehead with his wrist, and the wrist left a brown stripe above his eye. "The horse will not pull. He has decided against it."

The horse stood in the shafts with its head down, patient, the way a beast is patient when it has learned that a bad road ends somewhere. The cart had settled until the bed of it rested on the mud, and the load was sacks.

"Take him out."

"He will not stand."

"Take him out and hold him by the nose. He will stand for that."

The boy unbuckled the trace and backed the horse out and stood at its head with his hand flat across its nose. The cart eased down and the wheel went in further, and the mud came up over the rim in a slow lip.

I went up the bank and pulled a flat stone out of the tussock and carried it down and set it against the wheel.

"It will go under."

"Hold it with your boot and keep it there."

He put his boot on the stone. I took the shaft in my hands and put my shoulder under it and lifted, and the cart came up, and the mud let go of the wheel with a sound like a mouth opening. The boy kicked the stone in. The wheel came down on it and rocked and stayed.

"Now the horse."

He brought the horse back and buckled the trace and spoke to it low, and the horse leaned into the collar, and the wheel climbed off the stone and took the dry ground, and the cart was out and standing.

The rut stayed. It was full of brown water.

He wound the red cord around his fingers while he got his breath, over the little finger and under the thumb, the same way each time. "The clerk will ask why I am late."

"Tell him the road."

"The road is not an excuse. The clerk counts what goes out."

"And what comes back."

"What comes back he counts again."

I wiped my hands on the grass and the grass took most of the mud. The sacks in the cart were bread, and the smell came through the sacking, and the boy did not look at them.

"You will want a hand at the bend," he said.

"I will walk behind."

He climbed up and took the reins, and the cart went on, and the wheel that had been in the rut turned true, and the true turning of it was the only thing that had come out even. I walked behind it to the lane. The mud dried on my hands and came off in flakes, and the flakes fell, and the road kept them.

***

That night I put the three slips side by side on the table under the lamp. The T. The Tace. The initial and the surname. The lamp leaned over them and made them one small white field of names, and the field was the whole of what I had, and the whole of what I had was three slips of paper and a folded half-sheet in a drawer and a notebook with twenty-two entries and a blank. A person looking at the table would have seen scraps. I saw a map, and the map had a hole in it at the far end, and the hole was the only country I had not walked.

I put the slips back in my pocket and blew out the lamp. The thread ran on in the dark, and I could not see it, and I did not need to see it to know it was there. A thread that has been held is a thread that can be found again. I had held it all winter. I would hold it a while longer. The holding was the keeping, and the keeping was the only thing I had that the law could not reach into and take, because a thing held in a pocket is not a thing entered in a book, and a thing not entered in a book cannot be struck.

I thought about the asker one more time before I slept. She had ordered a woman fed and had not signed the order with her whole name, and she had written a letter a hundred and eleven miles and had not said the thing plainly, and the not-saying had been her own fold, her own keeping, her own way of holding a mercy where the law could not reach it. I did not know whether she had been right. I knew only that she had done the unlawful small thing and had not been caught and had not been thanked, and that the doing had outlasted her, and the outlasting was the only kind of justice a woman could get in a place where the law had a section and a subsection for everything. I would do the same, when my time came. I would do the small unlawful thing and not be thanked, and the not-being-thanked would be the proof that it was a keeping and not a trade, and a keeping that is not a trade is the only kind that cannot be bought back by the law.

I had made one such keeping already. The fold in the drawer was not a trade. No one had paid me for it and no one would. I had made it because a want unkept is a want that dies of exposure, and I had let enough things die of exposure in two lives, and the fold was the first thing I had shielded, and the shielding was the whole of what I had learned about love, and the learning had come from a dead woman's loaf and a living man's notebook, which is where most learning comes from, if a person is willing to look.

---

## Twenty-Three · The Crease Beneath

I had read the dead things of Tindle until the woman at the gate had a name kept in a book no one read. I had not read my own. The marriage register was a live record, but the entry for my wedding was old enough to have a dead part, and the dead part was the one line I had not looked at since the day Edward set his name where mine could not go.

A married woman does not sign. The law says so. The husband signs for her, because she is his to keep, the way a parish keeps a struck person in trust, lawfully, without a name. I had known this when I wed. Knowing is not the same as reading, and I had not read the entry, because to read it would have been to see the cutting done to me by a hand I loved, and a cutting done by a hand you love is the one you cannot call a cutting without calling the love into question.

I took the register down from the shelf above the audit office and set it on the table and opened it at the year. The page was thick and the ink had sunk into it and the sink was uneven, because the clerk had pressed hard on some names and lightly on others, and the pressing was the only record of how much a name had mattered to the man who wrote it. Edward's name was pressed hard. The space where mine should have been was pressed hard too, because the clerk had written his name across it, and the writing-across was the law's way of saying a wife is not a separate line.

I put my thumb to the entry.

Three seconds.

The church was cold and the light came through a window with a crack in the corner, and the crack made a small bright blade on the flagstones. The clerk was a thin man with a bent back, and he had the register open on a ledge, and he wrote the banns first and then the marriage, and when he came to the wife's line he stopped. The stop was not long. A man who stops too long at a register is a man who has begun to think, and a clerk who begins to think is a clerk who loses his place. But he stopped. He put the nib against the paper and did not write, and the nib made a small wet dot, and the dot dried while he decided, and then he took his thumb and ran it along the line where the wife's name would have gone, once, from the inner margin to the outer, and the running of the thumb made a crease in the paper, and the crease was so faint that no eye would ever find it. Then he wrote Edward's name over the wife's space, and the nib crossed the crease, and the crossing did not erase it. The crease stayed under the ink.

There was no warmth in the read. No face turned toward me. No voice. The clerk's thumb was the only living thing in it, and the thumb had hesitated, and the hesitation was the one mercy in a lawful act, and the mercy was so small that it had no name. A thumb along a line. A nib held still for the space of two breaths. That was the whole of the kindness the law had left room for, and it had been given by a man with a bent back who would not remember it, and it had been given to a woman who was not in the room and would never know.

The three seconds ended. I took my thumb off the register.

I was Tace. Not in the facts. In the law. She had been struck from a register because a man not her husband had claimed her, and the claim had been entered and the striking had been lawful. I had been struck from a register because a man who was my husband had signed for me, and the signing had been lawful, and the lawfulness was the same in both cases, and the same lawfulness had done the same thing to two women forty years apart. The law did not distinguish between a claim that took a woman out and a marriage that wrote a woman over. Both were entries. Both were lawful. Both left the woman with no line of her own.

The difference was the hand. Her hand had been a stranger's, and mine had been Edward's, and Edward's hand was the hand that held the pen and wrote the entries and set the notebook aside. The hand that had cut me was the hand that loved me, and the love did not make the cut smaller. It made it larger, because a cut from a stranger is a thing you can hate, and a cut from a lover is a thing you have to hold, and the holding takes both hands, and the hands are then too full to do anything else.

I sat with the register open and did not close it. The crease was there under the ink, and no one had seen it but the clerk who made it and the reader who read it, and the reading was the only thing the law had not taken, because a crease is not a name and cannot be entered, and a thing that cannot be entered cannot be struck. The clerk's thumb had given me the only mercy in the record, and the mercy was a hesitation, and a hesitation is not a justice. It is the shape a conscience makes when it is not allowed to speak.

My mother's marriage would be in a register like this one, in a parish I had not seen since I was a girl. Her name would not be on the line. My father's name would be over it, and the clerk who wrote it would have pressed hard or lightly, and no one would ever know which, because no one had ever put a thumb to that page. I had read dead things all winter and I had not read my mother's marriage, and the not-reading was a choice I had made without naming it, because a daughter who reads her mother's unsigned line reads the whole of what her mother was not allowed to be, and the whole of it was too large to hold in three seconds. My mother had put her wants where the parish could not see them. She had put them there because the line was not hers to write on. A woman who cannot sign learns to keep her wants in her hands, and her hands do the mending and the pouring and the carrying, and the hands are the only register she is allowed, and the register is not read by anyone but the daughter who inherits it.

I thought about my wedding day. It had been a small wedding and a cold one, and I had stood at the rail and Edward had stood beside me, and the clerk had asked the questions and I had answered them with my mouth, and then the clerk had turned the register and dipped the pen and given it to Edward, and Edward had signed, and I had stood with my hands folded and watched his hand make his name. I had not thought, then, that the folding of my hands was the whole of the law. I had thought it was a custom, a thing clerks did, a thing that did not mean anything. The read had corrected me. The read had shown me that the folding of the hands was the line where the law took the pen out of my fingers, and the taking had been so quiet that I had not heard it, and the quietness was the way the law works, and the quietness was the reason it had taken me a winter and a dead woman and a thumb on a crease to hear it at all.

I did not blame Edward. That was the thing I kept turning over, and the turning did not change it. He had signed because the alternative was worse, and the alternative was the gate, and the gate was where Tace had stood, and he had not been willing to leave me there. A man who signs for a woman to keep her inside the law is a man who has chosen the smaller cut over the larger one, and the choosing is a kind of love, and the love does not make the cut clean. It makes it the cut you have to keep, because the hand that made it is the hand that feeds you.

I closed the register. The crease was inside it, under the ink, and it would be there as long as the paper lasted.

***

I went out to the stair to let the cold of the room go off my hands. The stair was narrow. The wall beside it was warm from the bakehouse below, and a woman sat on a step with a paper in her lap and a basket at her feet.

She stood up when the door opened. "You are the woman who reads."

"I read."

"The overseer gave me this and he read it out, and I did not have the whole of it." She held the paper out flat, the way a person holds a thing that has cost her something. "He reads fast. I asked him to go over it and he said it was plain."

The paper was a parish form, ruled, with a hand I knew at the foot of it. It said the relief would be continued while the room was kept in repair. It did not say the room was hers.

"The money goes on," I said, "while the roof is kept."

"The roof is kept. I keep it." She turned the paper over. "Then it is a yes."

"It is a yes with a condition in it."

"A condition." She said the word the way a person says a word in a language not her own. "Then it is not a yes."

"It is a yes the parish can take back."

She sat down on the step and smoothed the paper with the flat of her hand. The smoothing did nothing. The paper had been folded wet and the fold had gone furry. "They want an answer on the back. The overseer said to put my mark."

"Your mark will do."

"My mark will do for them." She looked at the paper and not at me. "I had my name written at the church. The clerk wrote it and I put my mark under it, and the mark was the whole of what I could do."

I took the pencil from my pocket. The point was blunt. I turned it and used the side, the way I turn a knife at the heel of a loaf.

"What is the name?"

She said the name. I wrote it down.

"Put the whole of it," she said. "Not my husband's over it. The whole of mine."

I wrote the whole of it. Her name went down on the back of the parish's paper, above her mark, and the mark was a cross, and the cross was under the name and not over it. The writing was mine. The name was hers. The paper was the parish's, and none of them belonged to the others.

She blew on the ink and folded the paper along the furry fold. "You write a good hand."

"It was taught me."

"By who?"

"By a woman who is dead."

She nodded and put the paper in her sleeve and took up her basket and went down the stair with her hand on the wall where the wall was warm. The bakehouse door opened below. The heat came up the well of the stair, and the smell of bread came with it. I stood on the step until the warmth went out of the wall. Then I went back in.

***

Edward found me at the register. He came up the stairs with the mud of the north field on his boots and stopped in the doorway, and he did not ask what I had read. He reads empties by not looking, and the crease was the emptiest thing in the room, and he gave it the mercy of his not-seeing. But he had seen the register open, and the register open was a thing he could not unsee, and the not-asking was a choice he made with his whole body, and the choice cost him something, because his hand on the door frame was white at the knuckle.

"You read it," he said.

"I read the line where your name is and mine is not."

He came in and sat down across from me, and he did not look at the register. "I signed for you," he said. "I have signed for you a hundred times since, and every time I sign I think of the line, and every time I think of the line I want to put the pen down and not sign, and I sign, because a woman who cannot sign cannot hold a lease or a debt or a suit, and the not-signing would leave you with less than the signing does."

"I know."

"Do you. Because the signing is a cut, and I have known it was a cut since the day I made it, and I made it anyway, because the alternative was to leave you outside the law entirely, and outside the law is where Tace was, and I would not leave you there."

He said it flat, in order, without asking me to feel anything, and the flatness was the only way he could say it and keep his face. I looked at the register and then at him, and the two were the same thing, the lawful cut and the man who made it, and the man who made it had made it to keep me inside a law that had no line for me, and the keeping-inside was a kind of love, and the love was a kind of cut, and the two had been in the same hand all along.

"I am not angry," I said. "That is the strange part. I have been angry at a clerk I never met and at a parish I do not belong to, and I am not angry at you, and the not-angry is not forgiveness. It is understanding. Understanding is worse. Forgiveness ends a thing. Understanding keeps it open."

"Yes," he said. "That is what the notebook is for."

He went to the shelf and took down the notebook and set it on the table beside the register, the closed book and the open book, and the two together were the whole of the marriage: the law's line with his name over mine, and the notebook with the questions I had asked written in his hand. He had written me into a book of his own because the law's book had no line for me, and the writing-in was the only line he could give me, and it was not a line in a register and could not be used to hold a lease or a debt or a suit, and it was the only line that was entirely mine.

I closed the register. The crease was inside it, under the ink, and it would be there as long as the paper lasted, and no one would ever find it but a woman with a thumb and a second life. I put the register back on the shelf and stood with my hand on it, and the leather was cold, and the cold was the cold of the read, and the read had given me no warmth at all, and the no-warmth was the point. The cold read ratio had reached its floor. I had expected, somewhere under the winter, that reading my own marriage would give me something the other reads had not, some small heat, some face turned toward me. It had given me a thumb on a line and a nib held still. That was all the law had left room for, and the mercy had been real, and the realness of it was the coldest thing of all, because a mercy that small is a mercy that knows exactly how small it is allowed to be.

I went to the drawer and rested my palm on the wood. The folded paper was inside, and the want was inside, and the want had been written by a woman who could not sign for herself, and the not-signing was the reason the want had no word, because a person who cannot sign is a person whose wants are kept by other people, and the keeping is a mercy and a cut both, and the both is the only true thing. I did not open the drawer. I stood with my hand on the warm wood and let the cold of the read go out of my fingers, and the going was slow, and the slowness was the only warmth in the room, and the warmth was mine, made by my own hand on my own drawer, and it was the first warmth all day that no one had given me and no one could take.

Edward had not moved. The notebook lay closed beside the open register that was no longer open, and the fire was low, and the room held the two of us and the two books, and the holding was the marriage, and the marriage was a cut and a keeping in the same hand, and the hand was warm, and the warmth was the only thing the law had not been able to write over.

I did not tell him about the crease. It was the one thing in the read I kept for myself, and the keeping of it was not a lie. The clerk's thumb had hesitated over a line that was mine, and the hesitation was the only thing in the record that had been done for me and not to me, and a thing done for you by a stranger who will never know you is the purest kind of gift, because it cannot be repaid. I kept it the way I kept the fold. It was small, and it was mine, and the smallness was the whole of its worth. A crease is not a name, and a name is not a person, and the person is the only thing the law cannot write over.

---

## Twenty-Four · You, Then

The Tindle clay stuck to my heels on the way back, and the wind came off the river, and I came to the gate of the vestry where the assessment hung on the wall, and old Pettifer's cousin was there. He was the shopkeeper with the soft hands, the one who had told me about the wall, and he stood in the gateway the way a man stands in a doorway he means to block, and he did not step back when I came up.

I had not expected him. I had come to look at the assessment one more time, to read the unclaimed line by thumb and see whether the forty years of loaves had left anything in the paper that the eye could not find, and the errand was small and ordinary and I had been looking forward to it the way a person looks forward to a thing she knows how to do. He took that away by standing still. A man who stands still in a gateway makes every step toward him a question, and I had to decide, with the clay on my heels and the wind at my back, whether to ask the question or to go around.

"You have asked for her a long time," he said.

"I have asked. The parish has not answered."

"The parish does not answer. It feeds. The feeding is the answer, and the answer is lawful, and the lawful is the only kind no one need give." He looked at me the way the old look at a person who has done a thing they did not understand they were doing. "You have come all this way. You have read the books and counted the loaves and stood in the cold at that gate. You asked for her. You. What is it you want, then?"

The question landed the way a gate closes. Not slammed. Shut. The shutting was quiet and complete, and the quietness was the part that took the breath out of me, because a slammed gate is a thing done in anger and can be answered with anger, and a gate that is simply shut is a thing done in the ordinary course, and the ordinary course has no handle on the outside.

What do you want.

I opened my mouth.

The want was there. It was behind my teeth, and it was not a word. It was the folded half-sheet in the drawer at home, the three words and the dried bloom, the fold I had pressed with the side of my thumb, the want I had kept and not named and not opened. It was the woman at the gate and the child's hand on her skirt and the ring that was not hers. It was Edward's letter with no name at the top and the forty years of names he had put at the top of other letters to make up for it. It was my mother's coat and my mother's tea and my mother's mended shirt, and all the wants she had put where the parish could not see them, and the putting-away I had inherited with the coat. It was every want I had ever had for someone else, and it was the one want I had never had for myself because no one had ever asked me to have one, and the not-being-asked had been so complete that when the asking finally came, from a shopkeeper in a gateway, the want had no shape to come out in.

A want you have never been asked to name stays a shape without a word. I had spent two lives being asked for other people. I had been asked to read a dead man's book and find his sin. I had been asked to carry my aunt's register to a board. I had been asked, by a parish and a law and a room full of men, to prove I was entitled to the life I had been given back. No one, in either life, had stood in front of me and asked what I wanted and waited. And now a man had asked, and the asking was not a mercy. It was a light turned on a room I had kept dark for so long that the furniture had no names.

I could not answer.

The silence went on. The wind came off the river and moved the corner of the assessment on the wall behind him, and the paper lifted and lay down and lifted again, and the sound of it was the only sound, and the sound was the sound of a thing that had been pinned in place for forty years and had never once been asked whether it wanted to stay.

My hand stayed in the air between us. Not reaching for him. Not reaching for the gate. Simply there, at the height of my chest, the fingers open, the way it had stayed above the open half-sheet in the first winter and above the folded one after, and the staying was not a decision and not the lack of one. It was the body doing the thing the mouth could not, and the thing was the want, held open, with no word on it.

The old man looked at my hand. He had not meant to ask the question that would not answer. He had meant to note that I, who asked for a stranger, had never been asked for myself, and the noting had turned into the asking before he knew it, and the asking had turned into a silence, and the silence was the thing he had not meant to make. He took his hat off. It was an old man's gesture, the hat off in the presence of a thing he did not understand, and the bare head was the only apology he had.

"I did not mean to stop you," he said.

"You did not stop me. You asked me. No one has asked me."

"That is a hard thing to hear a woman say."

"It is a hard thing to be a woman who says it."

He put his hat back on, slowly, and the putting-on was the end of the apology and the beginning of something else, something more useful. "The parish will not ask you," he said. "A parish asks for a name and a rate and a pew. It does not ask a woman what she wants, because a woman's want is not a line in a book, and the parish has no answer for what is not a line in a book. If you are waiting for the parish to ask, you will wait until the loaves stop."

"I am not waiting for the parish."

"Then you are waiting for someone, and the someone is not me, and I am glad of it, because I would not know what to do with the answer." He stepped aside. "Go and look at your wall. I will not stand in your way again."

I went through the gate. The assessment hung where it had hung, yellow and curling, and I stood in front of it and put my thumb to the unclaimed line and did not read it. There was nothing in the paper I had not already taken. I had come for a small ordinary thing and I had been given a large unordinary one, and the large one was sitting in my chest where the want should have been, and the want was still in the air where my hand had been, and the hand had come down and the want had not.

I do not know, I had said, and it had been the only honest answer, and the honesty was the one thing no law had been able to take from me, because a truth is not a line in a register and cannot be struck by a clerk with a section and a subsection. But honesty is a poor comfort when the honest answer is a hole. I had been given a second life so that I would not waste the first, and I had spent the second asking for other people, and when the asking came back around to me it found nothing ready, and the nothing-ready was the waste, and the waste was the one sin the second life would not forgive, and I had committed it in a gateway with the wind at my back.

***

The inn stood where the road bent, and the woman who kept it was at the pump in the yard with her sleeves turned back, working the handle over a bucket that had a leak in it. The water came and went in the bottom of the bucket and did not rise.

"Bring the other," she said, without turning.

I brought the other from the hook by the door. It was sound and it was heavy and the handle was cold. She filled it and washed, and the water went grey.

"You have clay on you," she said.

"Tindle clay."

"It holds." She carried it to the step and set it down and straightened her back. "Sit down. You have the look of a person who has been stood in front of."

I sat on the bench by the door. The bench was worn pale where people sat.

She took up a slate and a rag from the windowsill and wiped it down and set it on her knee. "A man lodged here and went off before the light and left his account behind, and I have to put it in the book, and my hand shakes in the cold." She held out the slate and the chalk. "Put his name at the top for me. I have it in the book. I only want it where I can see it."

I wrote the name at the top of the slate. The chalk was soft.

"Under it, what he owes."

"I will not put an amount."

"Then put the day."

"I will not put a day."

She laughed, a breath of it, and took the slate back. "You are a careful woman."

"I am a woman who has been asked for figures."

"Asked by who?"

"By a parish."

"And did you give them?"

"I gave them what the book said."

She rubbed the name out with the rag. The name went. The slate was grey where it had been. "That is the way of it," she said. "You give them what the book says and they take it and go, and the man who lodges and leaves is not in the book at all, and I am left out of pocket and out of temper."

She put the slate back on the sill. The pump dripped. A hen came into the yard and went out again.

"Will you have a cup? It is made."

"I will."

She went in and came out with a cup. The cup was warm and it had a chip at the rim, and the chip was smooth from years of mouths. I drank it standing in the yard with the clay drying on my boots, and the tea was hot, and it was not kindness, and it was not a question. I drank the whole of it and set the cup on the step.

"Bring the bucket in when you go," she said, and went back through the door.

***

Edward was at the table when I got home. He had the notebook open and he had not written, and the blank page was the twenty-fourth, and the not-writing was his way of waiting for me to say the thing. I sat down and told him what the old man had asked, and I told him that I had not answered, and I told him the truth of why, which was that the want had no word because no one had ever asked it to have one.

He listened to the whole of it without writing. That was the first thing. He let me get to the end, and the getting-to-the-end took a while, and he did not stop me to put a line down, and the not-stopping was a courtesy I had not known I needed until I had it. When I was done he picked up the pen and wrote one line, and the line was the question and not the answer, because there was no answer, and he wrote the question down and set the pen beside it and did not read it back.

"The twenty-fourth," he said.

"The question."

"The question. Not the answer. I will not write an answer I do not have, and I do not have this one, and neither do you, and a notebook that pretends otherwise is a liar's book."

He closed the notebook. The fire was low and the room was warm at the edges, and I sat with my hands in my lap and looked at the closed cover, and the closed cover was the twenty-four entries and the question that had no answer, and the question was mine, and the mine was the thing I had been given two lives not to have.

"May I say a thing," he said, "that you will not like."

"Say it."

"You have spent the winter asking for a woman who cannot answer, and you have done it well, and the doing of it has been the only thing that has made you feel like the second life was worth having. But a woman who asks for the dead because the dead cannot ask her back is a woman who has found a safe place to put her asking. The dead do not want anything from you. The dead cannot ask you what you want. You chose a case with no living person in it, and you chose it because a living person might have asked you the question, and the question is the one thing you cannot survive."

I sat with that. It was the truest and cruelest thing he had ever said to me, and he had said it flat, in order, without asking me to feel anything, and the flatness was the only way he could say it and keep his face. I wanted to be angry. The anger was there, ready, the way it always was when a true thing came too close, and I held it and did not spend it, because the spending would have been a way of not hearing him, and I had spent enough anger on things that deserved it to know the difference.

"You think I chose her because she was safe," I said.

"I think you chose her because she was dead and could not ask you. I think the asking is the thing you are best at and the thing you are most afraid of, and you found a woman who would never turn it back on you, and you have loved her for it, and the love is real, and the fear is real, and the two have been living in the same house all winter."

He was right. I did not say so. I did not have to. He had written the question and not the answer, and the question was the twenty-fourth entry, and the twenty-fourth entry was the first one that was about me and not about her, and the about-me was the thing the book had been building toward without either of us saying it.

That night I took the folded paper out of the drawer and held it. I did not open it. I held it in both hands, and the crease was soft under my thumbs, and the want was inside it, folded, unfinished, and for the first time I let myself want the want itself. Not the woman's name. Not the child's line. Not the parish's answer. The want. The having of a thing of my own to want, and the being asked for it, and the answer, when it came, being mine to give or to keep.

I put the paper back and closed the drawer. The question was still in the air where my hand had been, and the hand had come down, and the question had not, and the question would wait, and the waiting was the fold, and the fold was the only thing I had that was entirely mine, and the entirely-mine was the thing I would have to learn to answer before the next book could begin.

I lay awake and tested the question the way a person tests a sore tooth, carefully, from the side. What do you want. If Edward had asked it, in the dark, in his flat voice, what would I have said. I tried out answers the way a woman tries on a dress she cannot afford. I want a house that keeps the rain out. Too small. I want the woman at the gate to have her name. Not mine. I want to be asked. That one was closer, and the closeness was the frightening part, because to want to be asked is to admit that the not-being-asked has been the wound, and to admit the wound is to give it a shape, and a shape can be looked at, and looking at it is the thing I had spent two lives not doing. I want to be asked and to have an answer ready. That was the truest one, and it was also the one that showed me how far I had to go, because the answer was not ready, and the not-ready was the winter, and the winter had been the asking, and the asking had been for her, and the her had been the safe place, and the safe place was the place I could no longer stay in.

I turned on my side and put my hand on the drawer's edge. The wood was cool. The fold was under it. The want was folded and the question was open and the two were the same thing at different stages, and the stages were the only progress I had made, and the progress was not a solution and not a surrender. It was a woman in a bed in a warm room with her hand on a drawer, and the hand was the hand that had stayed in the air in a gateway, and the air and the drawer were the same distance, and the distance was the thing I would have to cross, and the crossing would not be done by a read or a law or a name in a book. It would be done by a word, and the word was mine, and I did not have it yet, and the not-having was not a failure. It was the shape of a person still becoming, and the becoming was the only thing the lawful world had left me, and the becoming was slow, and the slow was the fold.

---

## Twenty-Five · Rooke Wins

Edward had the window, as he did most evenings, the notebook closed on his knee, the way he kept it when he was somewhere else and would come back when his hands were warm. The fire had been mended. He had done it while I was out, and he had not said so, and the mending sat in the room like a thing done and withdrawn, the way all his services sat, no account offered, no receipt asked.

I set the oilcloth parcel down and stood at the window with him, because the standing was the only company I could offer, and the company was the only thing he had ever asked of me. The yard was white with frost and the gate I had held for the boy stood true in its frame, and the true standing of it was a small ordinary thing, and the small ordinary thing was the only kind of goodness in the day.

"You were asked," he said.

"At the gate. Old Pettifer's cousin. What do you want, he said. I could not answer."

He did not turn from the window. "He was right to ask. You ask for a stranger and not for yourself, and the not-asking is the inheritance. I told you that. The question with no answer only makes a person hurt for nothing."

He had told me that. Not in this book. Before it, in a room much like this one, when I had been tearing myself open over a thing that could not be mended, and he had said the sentence and I had hated him for it, and the hating had been easier than the hearing. A question with no answer is a thing that makes a person ache and gives nothing back. He had said it flat, the way he said most things, and I had gone on asking anyway, because the asking was the whole of what I did, and the doing had been the only use I had, and a use is a hard thing to give up even when it costs more than it gives.

This book, he was right. I had asked for Tace, and the answer had been the law, and the law had a section and a subsection and a polite clerk, and the politeness had been the cruelty, and the cruelty had not been a face I could hate but a procedure I could not argue with. I had asked, and the asking had not given me an answer. It had given me a shape, and the shape was the not-having of an answer, and the shape was what I had carried all winter and called a finding. And then I had been asked in return, by a shopkeeper in a gateway, and I had not been able to answer, and the not-answering had been the proof of his sentence. A question with no answer makes a person ache for nothing. He had asked me what I wanted, and the asking had ached, and it had given me nothing but the ache, and the ache was the thing he had warned me against, and the warning had been right, and the rightness was the wound.

"I am not glad you were right," I said.

He turned then. "I am not glad to be right. The being-right is the wound. A man who is right about a lawful thing has only won a wager with a thing that cannot lose."

He said it without triumph, and the without-triumph was the thing that made it bearable, because a man who had wanted to be right would have smiled, and he did not smile, and the not-smiling was the only courtesy he had left to give. He had been right about a thing that cost me, and he had not enjoyed it, and the not-enjoying was the whole of the difference between him and every other man who had ever been right at my expense.

"You think I should stop asking," I said.

"No. I think you should stop asking the dead." He sat down and set the notebook on his knee and did not open it. "The dead cannot answer. You have spent a winter asking a woman who cannot answer, and the asking has been good work, and it has found a name and kept it, and the finding and the keeping are real. But the work has also been a place to hide. A woman who asks the dead is a woman who never has to hear the answer, because the dead do not give answers. They give shapes. You are very good with shapes. You have been good with shapes all winter, and the shapes have kept you from the one question that has a living person on the other end of it."

"The question you did not ask."

"The question I did not ask. Because you were not ready, and because a question asked before the person is ready is a question that gets a lie for an answer. I have waited. I will go on waiting. But the waiting is not the same as the not-asking forever, and the difference is the thing you will have to decide."

He said it flat, in order, and the flatness was the only way he could say it, and I understood that the flatness was not coldness. It was the way a man says a thing he has thought about for years and has decided not to dress up. He had been waiting. He had told me once that he would not ask a question he could not answer, and the not-asking had been his patience, and the patience had been the longest thing anyone had ever given me, longer than the second life, longer than the winter, and the length of it was the measure of what he thought I was worth.

"You won the book," I said.

"I won nothing. I wrote what you asked. The asking was yours. The answers were the law's. The law has no face. The no-face is the wound. The wound is mine to have told you, because I told you the thing you did not want to hear, and the telling is the only thing I had to give."

He had won the book, and he would not take the winning, and the not-taking was the last and smallest of his services, and the smallest was the one that undid me. I sat down across from him and put my hands in my lap and looked at the closed notebook, and the closed notebook was the twenty-five entries and the blank, and the blank was the question I had not asked, and the question was the one thing in the room that had a living person on the other end of it.

"You are not a stranger," I said. "That is the thing I have been avoiding. A stranger cannot ask me what I want, because a stranger does not know me well enough to want the answer. You know me well enough. You have written twenty-five things about me. You could ask, and the answer would have to be true, because a lie to a man who has written twenty-five things about you is a lie you cannot get away with. That is why I chose the dead. The dead never ask, and the not-asking is safe, and I have been safe all winter, and the safe has been the waste."

He looked at me for a long moment. "That is the truest thing you have said in this book," he said.

"It is the truest thing I have said in either life."

"Then it is worth the winter."

He said it without weight, and the lightness of it was the thing that made it heavy. I put my hand on the notebook, on the worn corner where his thumb rested, and the worn corner was warm, and the warmth was the twenty-five entries and the forty years of letters and the fire he had mended and not mentioned. I did not open the notebook. I did not need to. I had read the entries in the writing of them, and the writing had been the marriage, and the marriage had been the only book in the house that was not the law's.

That night I laid my hand on the drawer and left it shut. The want was inside, folded, unfinished, and the want had a living person on the other end of it now, and the living person was not a stranger, and the not-stranger was the thing that made the want possible and the thing that made it frightening. A want you cannot say to a stranger is a want that does not cost you anything. A want you cannot say to a man who has written twenty-five things about you is a want that costs you everything, and the everything was the only thing I had that the law had not already taken, and I was not ready to spend it, and the not-ready was not a failure.

It was the fold. The fold was the keeping, and the keeping was the only thing I had learned all winter, and the learning was worth the winter, and the winter was worth the second life, and the second life was worth the first, and the first had been taken for a use I had not known I had. I lay down in the dark and put my hand on the drawer's edge, and the wood was cool, and the fold was under it, and the fold was a promise I had made to a want with a living person on the other end of it, and the promise was the only thing in the house that was entirely mine.

The next morning was an ordinary one, and the ordinariness was the thing I had been avoiding all winter, because an ordinary morning has no law in it and no question and no name, and a woman who has been living inside a case does not know what to do with a morning that asks nothing of her. I got up and made the tea and swept the hearth and put the cups away, and the doing of it was the same doing as every other morning, and the sameness was a comfort I had not expected. Edward went out to the north field. I stood at the window that would not open and did not put my shoulder to it, because I already knew, and the knowing saved my shoulder, and the saving was a small wisdom I had earned.

I took the rate-book down and opened it at the back and looked at the fifteenth line. The pale seam was there. The margin was there. The four words were there, and the four words had not changed, and the not-changing was the whole of the problem and the whole of the peace. A thing that does not change is a thing you can put down. I had been carrying the book all winter, and the carrying had been the work, and the work was finished, and the finishing was not a triumph. It was a name kept and a law understood and a fold in a drawer. That was all. It was more than most women got. It was less than I had wanted. The two facts sat side by side and did not cancel each other, and the not-cancelling was the truth, and the truth was the only thing I had ever been able to give Edward that he could not get from a column.

I wrapped the book and put it on the shelf and did not open it again that day. Then I went to the drawer and took out the folded half-sheet and held it without opening it. The paper was soft at the fold, and the want was inside, and the want had a living person on the other end of it, and the living person was out in the north field with the mud on his boots. I could have walked out to him. I could have stood in the field and said the thing. The saying would have cost me the last thing I had that the law had not taken, and the last thing was the not-saying, and the not-saying had been my whole armour, and a woman does not take off her armour in a north field in February.

I put the paper away and shut the drawer. The morning went on. The boy came to the gate and the gate held. The bread came from the bakehouse and the bread was warm. The ordinary day did what ordinary days do, which is to go on whether or not a person has finished her case, and the going-on was the only mercy that asked nothing of me and gave nothing back, and the asking-nothing was the whole of why it was a mercy.

***

I went to the shop in the middle of the morning for tea, because the tin was down to the dust and the dust does not make a cup. The shopkeeper was behind the counter with his sleeves pinned and the day-book open and the pencil on its string, and he was tying a parcel for a woman with a child on her hip.

The shop smelled of tea and nails and the cold that comes off a stone floor. The child had a fist in the woman's collar and was working at it. The shopkeeper cut a corner from the sugar loaf and held it out, and the child took it and stopped working at the collar. "Say thank you," the woman said. The child said nothing. "Say it." The child said it. The woman shifted it to the other hip and spoke into its hair, and what she said was not for me.

She went out and the bell went with her, and then it was my turn, and neither of us said anything about the gateway.

"Tea," I said.

He turned to the shelf and put his hand on a tin at the end of it.

"Not that. The other."

He stopped with his hand on the tin. "The other is dearer."

"It is better."

"It is not always better."

"This is."

He took it down and set it on the counter and lifted the lid, and the smell came up, dry and dark. "You have not done that before."

"No."

"You have stood at this counter and let me put in your hand whatever was in mine."

"I have."

He weighed it and folded the paper and tied it and pushed it across. I put the coin down, and he took it and did not count it in front of me, which was a courtesy he had not offered me before. He opened the drawer under the counter and put the coin in the division for silver and shut the drawer with his knee, and the drawer went in hard and the whole counter moved.

"The question stands," he said.

"It stands."

He waited. A cart went past the window and the light in the shop changed and changed back. The waiting went on. I did not fill it. He put the lid on the tin and set it back on the shelf and shut the case and turned the key in it, and the turning of the key was the end of the errand.

I took the tea. The bell went. The lane was wet. The light lay on the wet, and I walked home with the paper in my hand, and the paper was warm from the counter, and I did not put my face to it until I was at my own door. Then I put my face to it. It smelled of tea and of the shop and of nothing else, and the nothing else was the part I had chosen.

***

Edward came in at noon and ate and read a letter and did not look up, and I sat across from him and watched him not look up, and the watching was the marriage, and the marriage was the cut and the keeping in the same hand, and the hand was warm. I thought about the book after this one. It would begin with the question I had not asked, and the question had a living person on the other end of it, and the living person was eating bread across from me and did not know that the next book had already begun in the space between us, which is where books begin, in the space a person has decided not to cross yet.

That afternoon I went to the bridge and stood on it again. The river was high with the thaw and the willows on the far bank were black and the road went up and away. I did not cross. I stood at the middle and put my hands on the stone and let the cold come up through my palms, and the cold was the weather's cold and not the law's, and I was glad of it. The thread ran on beyond the willows, and the thread would keep, and the bridge would keep, and the woman at the gate had a name in a book no one read, and the name was kept, and the keeping was mine, and the mine was the only thing in the whole of it that no clerk and no law and no careful man could write over.

I went home. The fire was in. Edward had kept it in and not said so. I put my hand on the drawer and left it there a moment, and then I took it away and sat down and drank the tea he had set at my place, and the tea was warm, and the warmth was the only open thing in the room, and I let it be enough. It was not enough. It was what there was. The difference between those two sentences was the whole of the winter, and I had learned to hold the difference without letting it break me, and the holding was the fold, and the fold was the self, and the self was the one thing the lawful world could not reach.

---

## Twenty-Six · The Kept Paper

Lady Day came on a Monday that year, and the year's book had to be closed.

I had closed books before. In my mother's house I had closed nothing, because she kept the accounts in her head and in the small marks she made on the back of the flour bill, and the marks were not a system, they were a memory she could put down. In this house there was a book. Edward had bought it the week we came, a foolscap quarto bound in grey board, and he had ruled the columns himself with a straight edge and a pencil, and he had written the headings in the plain hand he kept for the office. *Received. Paid. Balance.* Three headings, and under them a year of a small life: bread, coals, the woman who came on Mondays, a pair of boots, the carrier, the subscription to the burial club that neither of us would ever draw on, and the nine shillings a quarter that went to the school at the end of the lane because the school taught children whose fathers could not pay and somebody had to.

He had asked me to keep it. He had asked it as a thing he thought I would be good at, and he had been right, and the asking had been the first piece of work anyone had given me that was mine and not borrowed from a dead woman's file. I kept it in the drawer of the small table by the window. The folded half-sheet was in the same drawer. That was not a decision I had made. It was where the drawer was.

I took the paper out first, because the book could wait and the paper could not, and that was its own kind of accounting. It had been folded so long that it did not lie flat. The two halves lifted a little at the edges, the way a hand lifts when it has been held closed. The lifting was the paper remembering. The remembering was most of why I had kept it.

*What I want.* Three words, and the drop the nib had let fall on *want* had dried to the brown of an old scar. I had written them in this room with the pen I was looking at now, and the pen had been full, and the sentence had not come. The want would not come. It had not come the first time, or the second, or the night I folded it, or the morning after, or the night I put my hand flat on the wood of the drawer and left it there until the candle guttered. The not-coming was not a failure of feeling. It was a failure of having been asked. A want you have never been asked to name stays a shape without a word, and a shape without a word is not nothing. It is a thing waiting, and a thing waiting does not spoil, it only gets older.

Every want I could find was a want for someone else. I wanted Tace to have her line in the register. I wanted the child at the gate to have a name to be called by. I wanted Edward's letter to arrive at last with a woman's name at the top of it. Those were good wants and they were not mine, and a life spent wanting things for other people is a life spent, and I had spent two of them that way, and the spending had been the only use I knew.

I folded it back along the same line. The crease was there from before and the folding again was the keeping. I pressed it with the side of my thumb, the way I had pressed it the first time, and the pressing was a small ritual and the ritual was the only liturgy I had. I did not burn it. A fire makes an ending, and I had not earned one. I did not cross it out. Crossing would have been to end it, the way I had ended a thing in the book before this one, and the ending had been right for that thing and wrong for this. A thing crossed is a thing you have decided to leave. A thing folded is a thing you have decided to come back to.

Then I put it back and took out the year's book instead, because a woman cannot sit all morning with a half-sentence when the quarter is ending.

***

The dockets were in a bundle tied with thread. I had kept every one, because Edward had said a book without its papers is a story, and a story is what an auditor is paid to disbelieve. So the bundle had grown all year, and on Lady Day I untied it and laid the papers out in the order of the columns, and the laying-out took the morning.

It is a strange work, closing a year. The money does not care that a quarter has ended. The bread was eaten, the coals were burned, the woman came on Mondays and was paid on Saturdays, and none of it left a mark except the marks I had made, and the marks were the only proof that any of it had happened. A household's whole history goes into a quarto book in a drawer and comes out as a balance, and if the balance is right then the year was real.

I went down the paid column with the dockets beside me and ticked them off. Bread, eleven and fourpence. Coals, fourteen shillings. The woman, two shillings a week for forty weeks and nothing for the six weeks she was at her sister's. Boots, nine and six. The carrier, three shillings the quarter. The burial club, one and six. The school, nine shillings.

And then, between the coals and the boots, in his hand and not mine: *Coals, second order. Two pounds.*

I read it twice. I turned the leaf and read it a third time, the way I read a scraped line, by looking at the place where the paper had been asked to carry something it did not want to carry. The entry sat in the paid column in the same plain hand as the rest, and the rest were all mine, and this one was not.

There was no docket for it. I went through the bundle twice, and the coal merchant's papers were both there, one for fourteen shillings in September and one for nine and six in January, and neither of them was two pounds. A two-pound order of coals in November would have filled the coal house to the roof and we would have been warm until Easter on it, and we had not been warm until Easter. We had been cold in February the way everyone is cold in February.

I sat with my hands flat on the table, one on either side of the book. Outside, the carrier's cart went up the lane with the Lady Day goods. Someone's dog objected to it. The ordinary noise of a Monday went on being ordinary.

Two pounds.

I had sent two pounds to Tindle in October, in with the letter, and I had taken it out of the housekeeping purse in the dark of the morning and had not said so.

***

He came in at six and hung his coat and washed his hands at the scullery tap, and he came to the table because the table was where the book was, and he had known the book would be there, because it was Lady Day.

"The year is closed," I said.

"Does it balance."

"It balances." I turned the book round and pushed it across to him, open at the leaf. "Read the paid column."

He read it. He read it the way he read everything, from the top, and his eye came down the column at the speed of a man who has read ten thousand columns, and it stopped where I had known it would stop, and it did not move for a while.

"The coals," he said.

"The coals," I said. "There is no docket. There are two coal dockets in that bundle and neither of them is two pounds. The coal house was not full in November. The coal house was not full in February, and I know it was not, because I was in it."

He put his hand flat on the page, the way a man does when he means to hold a place. "You are owed an explanation."

"I am owed a true line."

"That too." He sat down. He did not sit the way a man sits who has been caught. He sat the way he sat at his own desk, squared to the work. "In October you sent two pounds to Tindle. I did not know it when you did it. I knew it when I came to enter the quarter and the purse was two pounds short and every docket was accounted for, and there was only one place two pounds could have gone, and it had gone there, because there was nowhere else you would send it and no one else you would send it to."

"You could have asked me."

"I could have."

"You did not."

"No."

"Why?"

He considered the page under his hand. "Because if I had asked, you would have told me, and the telling would have cost you something, and the cost would have been mine to watch and not mine to pay. And because a book that names a sum sent to a parish for a woman the parish has erased is a book that can be read.
