It sits in a drawer in a house in Collow, and the house is mine, and the office knows my hand, and any clerk with a warrant could put that book on a table and ask what the two pounds were for. You cannot answer that question in a court. I cannot answer it either. I can only answer it by saying the money went to Tindle, and then the next question is why, and the why is a woman who does not exist in any book the law will open."

"So you wrote coals."

"I wrote coals."

"You made the book lie."

"The book does not lie," he said. "The book records what left this house. Two pounds left this house in October. The book says so. It says so in the wrong place, and it says so in my hand, and both of those are my doing and not yours."

I took the book back and turned it round and looked at the entry again. It was four words and a figure, and it was the only false line in a year of true ones, and it had been written to keep me.

And that was the thing that would not sit down. Not the lie. The keeping. He had spent an evening with a short purse and a bundle of dockets and had worked out, in the order a man works out a column, that the cheapest way to protect me was to write a false word in a true book and never mention it. He had done it without asking. The not-asking had been the whole of the kindness, and the kindness had been done to me and not with me. There is a difference between those two things. A parish teaches a woman that early and never lets her forget it.

"Edward."

"Mercy."

"You have been protecting me from a question since October."

He did not answer at once. His hand was still on the table, and the hand did not move, and the not-moving was his answer beginning. "I have been protecting you," he said. "I would not put it as you have put it."

"Put it your way."

"I have been keeping the door of a room you do not have to go into. If you go into it, I will go with you. But I will not stand in the doorway and call you out."

"That is a handsome sentence," I said. "It is the kind of sentence a man can say and feel well about. And it leaves the room closed, and it leaves the choosing with you, and you have decided the room is not for me, and you have decided it kindly, and the kindness does not change who decided."

He looked at the book. Then he did a thing I had not seen him do. He took the pen from the ledge and dipped it and drew a line through the entry, one clean stroke, the way a clerk strikes a bad figure, and beside it, in the margin, he wrote: *Two pounds, sent Tindle, October. Not coals. E.R.*

"There," he said. "Now the book says what happened, and it says it in a hand that will hang me and not you, and if a clerk with a warrant ever puts this book on a table, I will tell him I sent the money and you will say nothing at all."

I looked at the margin. The words were small and level and there was no shame in them.

"That is not the repair," I said.

"No."

"The repair is to ask me next time."

"Yes."

"Say it."

"I will ask you next time," he said, and the saying was flat and unhandsome and worth more than the sentence about the door.

***

I put the book away and took the paper out and did not open it.

There was nothing to write. That was the plain fact of it, and I had stopped pretending otherwise. The pen was on the ledge and the ink was in it and the sentence had been waiting on the page since the day I wrote it, and the reason it had not been finished was not that I had no words. It was that the question had never been put to me by anyone who would stay for the answer. My mother had not been asked. Mrs. Pettifer had not been asked. The woman at the gate had not been asked, and neither had the child, and neither, in any way that counted, had I. A person who has never been asked does not learn to answer. She learns to be useful instead, and the usefulness is real, and it is not the same thing, and the difference is the whole of what I had been circling since February.

I put the paper back in the drawer and shut it. I set the flat of my hand on the wood. It was warm from the day's fire, and the warmth came up through my palm and stopped at the wrist, the way a read stops.

That night I dreamed of the gate. The hand on the rail, and the smoke going up from the house beyond, and the child standing where the light from the doorway did not reach. But the dream had turned. I was on the inside of the gate. Tace was on the outside. I put my hand on the rail beside hers, and there was no difference between the two hands, and no way to tell from the rail which side of the law either of them was on. I woke before the gate opened, with my hand on the edge of the drawer.

In the morning the drawer was where I had left it and the fold was inside it and the day was ordinary and cold and the fire had to be built from the grey. I built it. I made the tea and swept the hearth. I did not open the drawer again. The not-opening was not avoidance. It was the same economy I had learned from the rate-book, where the mice had chewed the corners and the chewing had not been the worst of it. The worst of it was the thin place, scraped until the wood showed through. A fold opened every day wears at the crease, and a crease worn through is a tear, and a tear is the one thing a kept paper cannot survive. So I kept it folded and put away, and I waited for a morning when there would be something to write, and the something did not come that week, and the not-coming was not the end of it either.

I had said so to myself in the winter and had believed it as a hope. By Lady Day it had stopped being a hope and had become a fact about the kind of person I was, and facts about yourself are cheaper than hopes and last longer.

---

## Twenty-Seven · The Blank Beneath

The parish answered in nine days, and the answer came as a form.

The carrier's boy brought it at the same hour he had brought the rate-book in February, and he brought it in the same way, wrapped in oilcloth and tied with red cord, and he held it away from his coat as though the cord might stain. He was taller than he had been when the book came, and I could not say when the growing had happened. A boy grows the way a hedge grows, without anyone noticing until the shape is wrong for the gap.

I took it into the kitchen and cut the cord and unfolded the paper on the board.

It was printed. That was the first thing. Somewhere in London a printer had set a form for this, with ruled blanks and small italic instructions, and the parish had bought a stock of them, and the stock was in a cupboard in the vestry, and the cupboard had been there before I was born. The form had a heading: *Representation respecting an entry in the poor rate.* Beneath the heading there were eleven ruled lines, and seven of them were already filled in the clerk's small even hand, and four were empty.

The clerk had written the parish, the year, the ledger, the folio, and the fact of the scraping. He had written *name erased, 1793, order of removal* in the space for the description of the error, and then he had stopped, because the next blank was for the *nature of the amendment required*, and there was no amendment he could name. A name cannot be restored to a book by a man who does not know the name. He had left it empty. Then he had signed at the bottom and dated it, and beneath his signature there were two more ruled lines, and the instruction beside them was printed in the same small italics: *Signature of the party aggrieved, or of the person authorized to act in the party's behalf.*

Two lines. One for the party. One for the person authorized.

I stood at the board with the bread knife in my hand and read the instruction three times, and each time it said the same thing.

***

The clerk's grandson came in the afternoon, on the pretext of the carrier's return load. He was sixteen and had his grandfather's stoop already, and he stood in the doorway with his cap in both hands and did not come in until I said the word.

"You brought the form," I said.

"Grandfather filled what he could." He turned the cap. "He said to tell you the rest is not ours to fill. He said you would understand the reason and I was not to soften it."

"I understand the reason."

"The party aggrieved," he said, and stopped, and began again, because he had rehearsed it on the road and the rehearsal had been better. "The form wants the party, ma'am. Not the person who complains. The party. Grandfather says a representation is made by the party whose rate is wrong, or by someone standing in the party's place by law, and there is no rate in that book for her, so there is no party, so there is nothing to amend. He says the entry is not wrong. He says the entry is correct. The book says she was removed and the book is right, and what was done to the book afterward was done to it by a hand, and a hand is not an entry."

"A hand is not an entry," I said.

"That is what he says."

"And if the hand is named."

"Then it is a matter for the quarter sessions, and the quarter sessions will want the party, and the party is what we have not got." He looked at the floor, which was clean, and then at the window, which was not interesting, and then, because he was sixteen and could not help it, at me. "Grandfather says you will want to sign it yourself. He says to tell you that your signature will not be received."

"Because I am a married woman."

"Because a married woman cannot be a party to a representation," he said, and the saying of it made him redden to the ears, and the reddening was the only part of him that was not his grandfather's. "Your husband can sign for you. That is what the form allows. There is a line for it."

I looked at the two ruled lines at the foot of the paper. One for the party, which was a woman who had been scraped out of a book in 1793 and had no name to write. One for the person authorized to act in the party's behalf, which was, by the printed instruction, the nearest thing the law had made room for.

Two women, and neither of them could write on it, and the form had been printed that way for forty years and no one had ever sent it back.

"Tell your grandfather," I said, "that I have read it, and that I will not sign it, and that he is not to think the not-signing is temper."

"I will tell him."

"Tell him also that I know what it cost him to leave the amendment blank. He could have written *none* and closed it, and he did not write *none*."

The boy put his cap on and took it off again and said, "He said you would see that," and went out to the cart, and I heard him tell the carrier's man that the load was light and they could go.

***

Edward came in at six and I had the form on the table with the bread board moved aside and a weight on one corner because the paper curled.

He read it standing, with his coat still on, and he read the printed italics twice, and then he put it down and took his coat off and hung it and came back and read it a third time sitting down.

"Your name goes here," he said, and put his finger on the first ruled line.

"There is no name to go there."

"No." His finger moved to the second line. "Mine goes here."

"Yes."

He sat back. The chair took his weight and complained in the way that chair did. "If I sign it," he said, "the form is complete. The parish will file it. The file will say that a representation was made respecting the entry, and that the party was represented by her husband, and that the matter was considered. And then the file will be closed, because a file with a signature on it is a file that has been dealt with. The clerk will not be able to raise it again. His grandson will not be able to raise it. The entry stays as it is, and the closing of the file will be the record that it was looked into."

"And if you do not sign it."

"Then the form is incomplete, and it sits in the parish's cupboard with a blank in it, and every clerk who opens that cupboard for the next twenty years will see a form that was sent out and came back unfinished, and will have to decide what to do about it." He turned the paper round with one finger. "An unfinished form is a nuisance. A nuisance is the only thing in a parish office that never gets filed away."

I looked at the two ruled lines, and at the small italic instruction, and at the eleven lines above them with their seven filled spaces and their four empty ones. The form was a machine for turning a wrong into a record that the wrong had been attended to. It had been built well. It had been built by people who did not think of themselves as cruel, and it had a line for a husband, and the line was the mercy in it, and the mercy was the mechanism.

"Then do not sign it," I said.

"I had decided not to before I sat down."

"You might have said so."

"I wanted you to see the reason before you saw the decision," he said. "A decision shown before its reason is a kindness, and a kindness shown before its reason is a way of not telling you the thing."

***

That night he left the notebook on the table when he went to hang his coat, and I came to it the way you come to a gate you have been told not to open, because the gate is the only thing left and the only thing left is the question.

The twenty-seven entries were a column, and the column did not add to an answer. It added to a silence, and the silence had a shape, and the shape was a woman at a gate with a hand on the rail. The first entry was the read of the desk and the knife. The last was the question I had put to him in this room, the one I had not answered, the one he had not asked back. Beneath it the paper was clean.

I set my finger to the blank. The notebook was live and the blank was dead, the way the empty slot in the Tindle register was dead, and a blank is a thing you read with the eye and not the hand, because the eye is the only reader of a space no one has filled. The blank was the height of two lines. It was the cleanest thing in the room. It had been left clean on purpose, and the purpose was the only thing in the whole notebook that he had done for me and not for the record.

"You wrote it down," I said, when he came back. "But you do not ask."

"I write what you ask. The asking is yours to do. I only record."

"Recording is not asking. You have the blank beneath, and you leave it, the way you leave a door ajar for a person you do not expect to come through tonight."

"The blank is yours. I hold it open."

He sat with the notebook between us and did not close it and did not write in it, and the not-writing was the most deliberate thing he had ever done in front of me. I had watched him write twenty-seven entries. I had never watched him not write one, and the not-writing was harder to watch, because a man who writes has decided, and a man who does not write has decided not to decide, and the second is the rarer courage and the cheaper one, and I had not worked out yet which of the two it was in him.

"Why do you not ask," I said.

"Because asking is useless. You will not answer. You have not answered in two lives. The asking would only make you hurt for nothing, and the hurting is a wound, and a wound is not an answer."

There it was, stated plainly, the way he stated a total. His kind of not-asking. The useless kind. The kind that says the answer will not come, so the asking is a cruelty, and the cruelty is a mercy, because to ask and be unanswered is a wound, and the wound is not worth the inflicting. He had learned it from a woman with a cottage and a seal whose name he had never learned, and he had learned it the hard way, and the hardness had made it a law in him, and the law in him was the only law he had ever obeyed without being made to.

There was another kind. The woman at the gate had not been asked, and the not-asking had been the law's, the lawful cutting that strikes and does not ask, and the striking was the cruelty because it was done by a thing with no face. And the asker at the bridge, the one who would read me at the end, had not asked because the asking would hurt the woman, and that not-asking had been a mercy, and the mercy was the kind that costs the one who gives it and spares the one who receives.

And there was a third kind, and I had held it in my hand that afternoon. The form had not asked. The form had a line for a husband and a line for a party and no line for the woman the entry was about, and the not-asking there was not cruelty and it was not mercy, it was architecture. A thing built so that the question cannot be put does not refuse the question. It has never had a place to put it.

Three kinds of not-asking, and I had been living among all three without naming any of them. The law did not ask because it had a section and a subsection and no face. The asker did not ask because the asking would have hurt. Edward did not ask because the answer would not come. None of the three was the same as the others, and the parish had taught me to treat them as one, because a parish that can make you believe all silence is the same silence has made you believe that no silence can be resisted. I had resisted one. I had asked the law a question it did not want to answer, and the law had answered with a procedure, and the procedure had been a silence dressed as an answer, and I had seen through the dress, and the seeing-through was the whole of what I had done this winter.

"Why hold it," I said, "if the answer will not come."

He considered the question the way he considered a column, for whether it added. "Because a blank held open is a question a person can walk back to. A blank closed is a question a person has to break into. I would rather leave you a door than a wall. I have spent my life leaving people doors, and most of them never come back through, and the ones who do are the reason I go on leaving them."

"That is a good answer," I said. "It is so good that I have not asked you for a year whether it is true."

"It is true."

"I know it is true. That is the difficulty." I put my hand flat on the page beside the blank, not on it. "You have told me what you will not do. You have never told me what you would do if I answered. A man who leaves a door open and never stands in it has not made a house. He has made a wall with a hole in it and called the hole a courtesy."

He was quiet for a while. The fire took a coal and settled.

"I would listen," he said at last. "That is all I have. I would listen to the whole of it and write nothing down until you had finished, and then I would ask you whether you wanted it written, and if you said no I would not write it, and the not-writing would be the hardest thing I have done, and I would do it."

"Then ask me."

"Not tonight."

"Why not tonight?"

"Because you have spent the day with a form that has no line for you," he said, "and a question asked tonight would be a question asked to a woman who has just been shown her own place in the architecture. It would be answered out of that, and the answer would be wrong, and a wrong answer given once is hard to take back."

He closed the notebook, but not at the blank. He closed it at the page before, leaving the last page out, the blank beneath the question still visible, still held, still open. The not-closing was the mercy, and the mercy was the kind that costs the one who gives it.

I looked at the open page and thought about the fact that I had spent two lives being asked for other people and had never once been asked for myself, and about the fact that the one man who could ask me would not, and about the fact that he might be wrong.

He might be wrong. That was the thing I had not considered. He had decided the answer would not come, and he had decided it years ago, and the deciding had been made on a woman who was not me, a woman with a cottage and a seal whose name he had never learned. He had taken her silence as the measure of all silence, and he had built his patience on it, and the patience was generous and the measure was wrong, because I was not that woman, and my silence was not her silence, and a man who does not ask because he was once answered with nothing is a man who has let one woman's nothing decide for all the women after.

I did not say that. It was too large to say in a warm room with the fire low. But I put it away the way I put away the folded paper, and the putting-away was the fold, and the fold was the keeping, and the keeping was the thing I did best. I would keep it until the book after this one.

I put the form in the drawer beside the paper. The two of them lay together, one with its blanks ruled and waiting and one with its sentence half written and waiting, and the waiting was the same in both, and the difference between them was that the form had been printed by men who did not know they were building a wall, and the paper had been folded by a woman who knew exactly what she was doing.

I left the notebook as he had left it, the last page out, the blank held open. I did not write in it. I did not close it. I went to the window and stood beside him, and the two of us looked out at the yard and the true gate and the ordinary dark, and neither of us said the thing.

---

## Twenty-Eight · The Final Read

I had read the fifteenth line more times than I could count, and every read had promised a woman and returned a wound.

This one I prepared for the way you prepare for a thing you have already decided will be the last of its kind. I cleared the table. I put the rate-book square to the edge and the lamp where the light would fall across the leaf and not into my eyes. I set the chair at the distance that lets a person's arms reach the page without leaning, because a read done leaning is a read done hurried, and a hurried read gives you the outside of a thing and calls it the whole.

Then I took off my mother's coat.

I had not done that before. Not once in the eight weeks. I hung the coat on the back of the chair and stood in the cold of the room in my sleeves, and the cold came at my arms from the window and from the floor and from the wall behind me, and I let it come. The coat was wool and the wool was twenty years old and it had done what wool does, which is to stand between a woman and the world and take the world's temperature on her behalf. I had been read through that wool all winter. Every read in this book had come to me through the coat, and the coat had kept the worst of it off, and the keeping had been a kindness I did myself and a lie I told myself, and a read taken through a kindness is a read taken at second hand. If this was to be the last read of the book, I would have it first hand or not at all.

The room was cold the way a room is cold in the last week of February, when the fire has been kept low for a month to save the coals and the walls have stopped pretending. I put my hands on the table and the wood was cold. I put my thumb on the page and the paper was colder.

***

Three seconds.

The gate came first, the way it always came, the five bars and the top rail worn smooth where hands had rested, and the smoke going up from the house beyond it in a column that leaned with the wind. Then the cold, which was not the room's cold but the read's own, the particular cold of a February afternoon in a parish where the willows had been cut back and the light came off the wet road.

Tace stood at the gate and she was younger than the basket. That was the thing the reads had never let me forget. The basket belonged to an old woman; the woman at the gate was perhaps thirty, and the scraped line had been made when she was thirty, and the forty years since had been lived by a person the book had already finished with.

She had her right hand on the top rail. Her left hand was closed, and in the closed hand was the ring, and the ring was iron and plain and was not hers to keep, and she held it the way a person holds a thing she has been told to hold and not to wear.

The child stood at her skirt with one fist in the fabric. The child was four or five. The child was not crying. That was the detail that had taken me three reads to see. Having seen it once I could not unsee it: a child of four in a strange parish at a gate in February, and not crying, because the woman holding it was not afraid, and a child takes its fear from the hand it holds.

Then the read did a thing no read had done before.

A horse came down the road.

It came from the north, from between the willows, at a walk, and the sound of it arrived before the shape, the wet road giving up the hooves. Tace's hand came off the rail. Not fast. It came off the way a hand comes off a rail when the person has been holding it for an hour and has stopped believing the holding is doing anything, and then hears hooves.

She did not step forward. She straightened. Her shoulders came up and back, and her chin came up, and the whole of her body went from waiting to being seen, and the change was so quick and so complete that it was like a page being turned.

The horse came level with the gate. The rider was a man in a dark coat with his collar up, and his hat was down against the weather, and he did not turn his head. Not a glance. Not a slackening of the rein. The horse went past the gate at the same walk it had come at, and the sound of it went on down the road behind me, and then it was a sound in the distance, and then it was the road again.

Tace's hand went back to the rail.

That was all. There was no cry and no calling after him and no hand raised. Her hand went back to the top rail and closed on it in the same place it had been, and her shoulders came down, and the coming-down was worse than any crying, because crying is a thing that ends and the coming-down of shoulders is a thing that simply resumes.

The child pulled at her skirt. The child said something. The read did not give me the words. The read gives what the dead thing holds, and the dead thing did not hold the child's words. It held only the pull of the fist in the fabric and the small voice going up into the cold and the sound of it not being answered.

Tace put her hand on the child's head. She did not look down. Her eyes were on the road, and the road was empty, and the emptiness had just been proved, and she kept her eyes on it anyway, and the keeping was the whole of her hope, and the hope was the cruellest thing in the read.

Crueller than the law. The law had done its work in an afternoon with a knife and a margin, and it was over, and a thing that is over can be looked at. The hope was still working. It had been working for an hour at that gate in the cold, and it would go on working after the gate, in the cottage, at the window, every time a horse came down a road in any parish in England.

Then the light changed on the willows, and the three seconds ended, and I lifted my hand from the page.

***

The room was colder than it had been. I had taken off the coat to feel it and I felt it now, and the cold was not the weather's cold. It came off the page the way heat comes off a stone that has been in the sun, except that it was cold. It went through my sleeves and up my arms and settled in the middle of my back. I stood in it and let it do what it did.

I had expected, this last time, to be given the man.

The read had not given him. It had given his horse and his collar and his hat and the fact that he did not turn his head, and it had not given his face, and the not-giving was not a failure of the paper. It was the truth of the thing. The paper held her side of that afternoon, and on her side there was a rider who did not look, and a rider who does not look has no face. A face is a thing you are given by being looked at. He had ridden past a woman at a gate and had not given her his face, and so the paper had none to give me, and forty years had not improved it.

I sat down and put my mother's coat back on. The wool was cold against my arms and then it was warm, and the warming was the coat doing the only thing a coat can do. I put my hands in the pockets. The pockets were deep and the lining of the right one was torn, and the torn lining was a thing I had known about for years and had never mended, and I did not mend it now. A mended lining forgets it was ever torn. The forgetting is what I was trying to avoid.

Edward came in from the yard. He had been at the true gate, the one the boy had mended the week we came. He had his coat on and his hands were red to the wrist. He stopped in the doorway when he saw me in my mother's coat with the rate-book open and the chair pushed back where I had stood.

He did not ask what the page had given me. He shut the door and came to the fire and held his hands out to it, and the holding-out was the only thing he did, and the doing of it was a kind of company.

"The last one," he said.

"The last one."

"Did it give you the man."

"No. It gave me the horse and the coat and the hat. He rode past the gate and did not turn his head." I put my hands flat on the table, one on either side of the book, the way I had sat at Lady Day. "I have been reading that line all winter expecting to be shown a villain. There is no villain in it. There is a man on a horse who did not look, and a woman at a gate who had been told someone would come. The telling was the whole crime. The not-looking was only the proof."

He sat down across from me. The fire was behind him and his face was in shadow, and the shadow made him look older and kinder, and the kindness was the thing I had married and had never once been able to say so.

"Then the book is finished," he said.

"The reading is finished. What is left is the keeping." I turned the rate-book round so the scraped line lay toward him. "And there is a thing I have to ask you, and I have been putting it off because I did not want the answer."

"Ask it."

"If the name went back into that book. If a clerk with a warrant and a form with a signature on it put her name back in the fifteenth line, and the rate was assessed to her, and the book was made right." I put my finger on the thin place. "What happens to the loaves?"

He did not answer at once. He looked at the page the way he looked at a column that will not add, and then he looked past it, at the wall, at the thing behind the column. "The loaves stop," he said.

"Say the rest of it."

"The loaves stop, and something else begins. A person on the rate-book is a person the parish is chargeable for. A person the parish is chargeable for and who has no settlement here is a person the parish may remove. That is what a removal order is for. It is not a punishment. It is an accounting. The parish looks at the book, finds a name it must pay for, and asks where the name belongs, and if the answer is nowhere, the name is moved to the place it belongs, and the moving is done by constables and a cart." He turned his hand over on the table, palm up, empty. "She was removed in 1793 under a lawful order. If she went back into the book, she would be a woman with no settlement and no husband and no parish to receive her, and the same order would be available, and the same section, and the same margin. Only this time it would be a fresh order and she would be seventy years old."

I had been holding three things as one, and he set them apart with two fingers on the table, the way he separated figures that had been run together in a column.

"You are confusing three things," he said, "and the parish has spent forty years making sure that everybody does. There is the parish she stood in, which is Tindle, and Tindle never owned her. There is the parish she was settled in, which was his, by the claim, and which stopped being hers the day the claim was struck, because a settlement that hangs on a struck claim is a settlement no parish will own afterwards. And there is the man, who is not a parish at all and never has to answer for any of it. Three different things. The law permits them to be three."

"And the loaves."

"And the loaves would be unlawful, because a parish may not relieve a person it has not assessed, and every woman who has set a loaf on that sill for forty years has been doing a thing the auditor could strike out." He looked at me. "That is what you have been asking me. Not whether the book can be corrected. Whether the correction is the mercy."

I sat with it. The fire moved and settled.

"So the book is right," I said, "and the wrong is the book."

"The book is correct in every line. That is the thing about it that nobody can be brought to trial for. The scraping is a hand and a hand can be named, and naming it would cost a clerk his place and nothing else. But the book underneath the scraping is correct, and the correctness is what did the work. A parish may not keep a woman who is not on the book, and a woman on the book may be removed, and between those two laws there is no door. There is only the sill and the loaf and a hand that does not write anything down."

"Then I have been chasing a correction that would kill her."

"You have been chasing the name," he said. "The name is not the correction. The name is the thing a person is owed whether or not it is written anywhere." He was quiet a moment. "I have spent thirty years in offices where the book is the truth. I am telling you now, as a man who has closed a thousand books, that there is a place under the book where the truth lives, and it is not written down, and the not-writing is not a failure. It is the only place left."

***

He wrote the twenty-eighth that night. I did not watch the hand. I watched the fire, and the fire was the only thing in the room that did not have to be written down, because a fire writes itself in ash and does not ask to be remembered.

When he had finished he closed the notebook and set it on the table between us, and the closed notebook was twenty-eight entries and a blank, and the blank was still open in the way a closed book can hold an open page. I put my hand on the cover. The cover was warm from his knee, and the warmth was the only thing in the room that had been given and not taken.

"What did you write," I said.

"That the last read gave a horse and a man who did not look, and that the rider's face is not in the paper, and that this is not a gap in the evidence but the evidence."

"You wrote a sentence with a comma in the middle of it and a judgement at the end."

"I wrote what happened and then I wrote what it meant," he said. "It is the only entry in the book where I have done both. I thought the last one might be allowed the liberty."

I did not read it. I could have. The notebook was open to the page before and the entry was there in his small plain hand, and I did not read it, because he had told me in his own words and a thing told is not improved by being checked. That was a new thing in me and I noticed it happening and did not stop it.

That night I read nothing. I sat with the rate-book closed and the folded paper in the drawer and the notebook between us, and I let the winter be the winter. It was nearly over. The nearly-over was a thing a person could feel in the light, which had begun to stay a little longer at the edges of the day. The light is the first thing that tells you a season is turning. It does not announce itself. It stays a minute later and you notice.

I noticed. And the noticing was the whole of the spring, and the spring was coming whether or not the book was finished, and I was not ready, and the not-ready was the only honest thing I had.

---

## Twenty-Nine · The Advance

The name was Tace. The law had struck her. The parish had fed her. The seven loaves had turned for forty years.

That was the what, and the what was complete, and a complete what is a strange thing to hold after a winter of not having it. It is heavier than a partial thing. A partial thing you can carry in one hand. A complete thing takes both.

What she was: a woman claimed by a man who was not her husband, settled in a parish that was not hers, struck out under the law of settlement. What the parish did: struck her and fed her, lawfully, without a name. What the law said: it is entirely lawful. What the loaves said: old Mrs. Pettifer had outlasted the cutting with a turning of bread. What the asking said: a person who asks for others and never for herself is a person at a gate with a ring that is not hers to keep.

The what was complete. The why was not.

Why Tace had been claimed, and the claim entered, and the claim struck, and the striking lawful. Why the parish had chosen feeding over forgetting. Why the asker at the bridge had read me at the end and set me down on this side of the finish and never once asked me what I wanted, when the not-asking had been the whole of both my lives. Those three whys were in no book I could open. They were in the book after this one, and the book after this one was not mine to write in this book, and the not-writing was not a failure. It was a boundary, and I had learned to tell a boundary from a wall.

***

The vestry was cold and smelled of the mice that had been at the rate-book before me. I sat at the table with the order book open and laid out everything I had: the three slips, the copy of the removal order the clerk had let me make, and the page of my own hand where I had written the seven crofts in the order of the turn.

The shape was a line. At one end a woman at a gate with a child and a ring that was not hers. At the other end a hand that had ordered her fed and had not signed its whole name. In between, forty years, seven crofts, a clerk's thumb, a rate-book, and a name written small in a private book that no warrant would ever find. A line with two unfound ends is a line a person can spend a life on and not finish.

The door went. A woman came in out of the yard with a basket on her hip and her shawl pinned at the throat with a thorn, and she stopped when she saw me at the table, and the stopping was not surprise. It was the stopping of a person who has come to a place she knows and found a stranger in it.

"Grandfather is at the tithe barn," she said. "I have brought the quarter's account."

"He is at the tithe barn," I said. "I am not the clerk."

"I know what you are." She set the basket down and did not sit. She was perhaps sixty, and her hands were the hands of a woman who had lifted things all her life, and her apron was clean because she had put on a clean one to come to the vestry, and the clean apron was the whole of the respect she had been able to bring with her. "You are the one that reads the books."

"I read what is finished."

"That is a careful way of putting it." She took a paper out of her sleeve and laid it on the table, and the paper was a bill in a slow careful hand, and the hand had been taught late and had not been taught much. "The account. Four pounds eleven, and the acre at Long Bank, and the girl's wages, and the coal."

The clerk's grandson came in from the back with the ledger and took the paper and began to copy, and the woman stood with her basket and waited, and the waiting was patient in the way of people who have waited in offices before.

I had the name in my pocket. Three slips, and one of them had the name on it, and the name had come out of a private book in this vestry, and the woman standing at the table with a clean apron and a bill in her sleeve was the holder of the turn at Long Bank, and the turn was the loaf, and the loaf was the thing that had kept Tace alive for forty years.

"You are at Long Bank," I said.

"Twenty-two years."

"The turn comes to you at Michaelmas."

Her hands did not move on the basket handle. Her face did not change. But something behind the face closed, the way a door closes at the end of a passage, quietly, and the quietness was the whole of the answer.

"It comes at Michaelmas," she said.

"Do you know why."

The clerk's grandson had stopped writing. He had stopped without lifting his head, and the stopping was a thing he could not undo, and he went on again too quickly, and the scratch of his pen was the loudest thing in the room.

The woman looked at me for a while. Then she did a thing I had not expected. She pulled the other chair out with her foot and sat down, and she put the basket on the floor between us, and she folded her hands on the edge of the table the way a person folds her hands when she has decided to say a thing once and be done.

"No," she said. "And I will not be told."

"You have carried it twenty-two years."

"My mother carried it before me, and hers before her, and there is not a woman in seven crofts can tell you the why. We know the what. The what is a loaf at the sill at Michaelmas and a portion set by at every quarter, and no name on any paper, and no word said at any door. That is the whole of what we know, and it has been enough for four generations, and it is enough for me."

"Would it be enough if you knew the reason were unlawful."

"It would not be unlawful," she said. "Bread is not unlawful."

"The relief of a person not on the book is unlawful. The auditor could strike it out."

"Then the auditor may strike it out," she said, "and the striking out will be a paper, and the paper will go in a drawer in Collow, and the loaf will still go on the sill at Michaelmas, because the auditor does not come to Long Bank and the drawer does not bake."

There was no answer in me for it. It was the truest thing anyone had said to me all winter, and it had been said by a woman with a bill in her sleeve who had never read a statute in her life.

"Mrs. Gow," I said. "If I told you the name."

"Then I would have it," she said, "and having it I would have to do something with it. A name is not a loaf. A name has to be carried, and a woman who carries a name has to say it or hold it, and both are work. I am sixty. I have the acre and the girl and the coal." She picked up her basket and stood, and the standing was the end of the conversation and had been decided before it began. "I have carried the turn without a name for twenty-two years and my mother carried it before me, and if it goes to my daughter without a name then the not-knowing will have been the thing that held, and the holding is worth more than the knowing. Do not tell me. Do not tell my daughter. If the paper in your pocket has a name on it, keep it in your pocket."

She went to the door and stopped with her hand on the latch.

"You said you read what is finished," she said. "That is the right way. The finished does not need feeding."

Then she was gone into the yard, and the door came to behind her, and the clerk's grandson wrote for a while in a silence that had a shape.

***

I folded the slips and put them in my pocket. The young clerk with the thin wrists watched me do it, and he had the look of a man who wanted to ask a question and had been taught not to.

I gave him the rate-book to put back on the shelf, because it was Tindle's book and I had carried it long enough, and the giving back was the end of the carrying, and the end of the carrying was the thing I had been putting off since the book came.

"You are done with us," he said.

"I am done with the book. The rest is not done, but the rest is not in this parish."

He put the rate-book on the shelf and stood with his hand on it, and the hand was his grandfather's hand, thin-wristed and careful. "My grandfather kept a book of his own because the parish book would not hold the truth. You have a book of your own now, I think. The one your husband keeps."

"He keeps it."

"Then the truth is held," he said, "and the holding is the only thing that matters, and the rest is the book after."

He said it plainly, the way his grandfather might have said it, and the plainness undid me a little, because I had spent a winter among people who spoke in sections and subsections and margins, and a plain sentence from a young clerk was the rarest thing Tindle had given me. I thanked him. He nodded and did not see me out, and the not-seeing-out was its own courtesy, because a person who sees you out is a person who is glad to be rid of you, and he was not glad.

***

I walked out into the winter light. It fell on my hands, and the hands were the hands that had held the rate-book, and the thumb that had read the scraped line, and the fingers that had pressed the fold, and the light on them was the light of a season that was turning, and the turning was the first thing all winter that had not had to be asked for.

I stood in the churchyard a while and let the light lie on my hands. The yews were black and the grass was flattened where the frost had been and the stones leaned the way old stones do, and a hand in winter light is a hand that has done a thing and is being looked at by the weather, and the weather does not judge and does not forgive and does not ask, and the not-asking was the only kind I had ever been able to rest in.

Mrs. Gow's cart was going up the lane with the basket in the back. She did not look round. I did not lift my hand. Two women who had each decided the same thing in the same room did not need to wave at one another.

Tace was kept in a book no one reads. The one who asked was kept at the bridge, and the one who will read me was kept at the end. The three were a line, and the line was not finished, and I had stopped being afraid of the not-finished. A question too large for the book you are in is not a threat. It is only a question you have not earned yet, and every book needs somewhere to put those, or it breaks under the weight of its own whys.

***

Edward was at the lychgate with the horse. He had come to fetch me and had not said he would come, and the coming was the same as the fire kept in and the tea set at my place and the notebook left open at the blank.

He took the parcel and I took his arm, and we went out through the gate, and the gate was a true gate on true hinges and it opened when he pushed it, and the opening was a small ordinary mercy, and the ordinary mercy was the only kind I had ever been able to keep.

"Finished," he said.

"The what is finished. The why is not."

"The why is the next book's."

"The why is the next book's," I said, "and I have stopped minding. A person cannot carry two books at once. I have been trying to carry the next one inside this one all winter, and the trying has been the weight, and the weight was never the case. The case was light. It was the next book that was heavy, and I have put it down."

He looked at me sidelong, the way he looked at a column that had come out even and he was not sure he trusted it. "You have put it down."

"I have put it down. It will be there when I pick it up. A book put down is not a book lost. That is the difference between a fold and a cross, and I have learned the difference, and the learning is the only thing I am taking home from Tindle."

We went along the road with the river on our left, and the river was high and brown and going fast, and the willows were black, and the light was longer than it had been. I did not look back at the vestry. I had looked back at too many places in two lives, and the looking-back had never once changed what was behind me.

"The woman at Long Bank," I said. "She would not be told."

"You offered."

"I offered. She said a name is not a loaf. She said if I told her, she would have to do something with it, and she is sixty and has the acre and the girl and the coal." I watched the road. "I have spent the whole winter certain that the not-telling was the cruelty. She has spent twenty-two years certain that the not-telling is the whole of the holding. She is not a bad woman and she is not a coward, and I have no answer for her, and the not having an answer is new."

"It is not new," he said. "It is the first time you have noticed."

"That is a hard thing to say to a woman on a road."

"It is a hard thing to say, and I have not found a kind way to say it, and I have stopped looking for one." He shifted the parcel to his other arm. "You will notice it again. The next book is full of it. There is no case in the world where the truth is owed to everyone in it. A man who says otherwise has never had to hand a paper to a woman in a clean apron."

The clay stuck to my heels and the wind came off the river and the horse breathed in slow pulls. I had advanced the mystery to the name and the law and the not-pointing, and the not-pointing was the mercy, and the mercy was the only thing I had ever been able to give the dead. The living would have to ask for themselves. That was the next book, and the next book would begin with a question, and the question would be mine, and I was not ready, and the not-ready was honest, and the honest was the only thing I had that no clerk and no law and no careful man could write over.

***

We came to Collow at dusk. The gate was true. The fire was in. I put my hand in my pocket and touched the three slips and the folded paper, and the papers were warm from my body, and the warmth was the only heat the words had ever had.

---

## Thirty · The Folded Paper

The folded half-sheet went into my pocket before I left Collow.

Not the drawer this time. The pocket. The keeping had moved from the drawer to the body. The moving was a small thing and it was not a small thing, because a paper in a drawer is a paper you visit and a paper in a pocket is a paper you carry, and I had decided, without saying so, that I would carry it now.

I had the coat on and my hand in the pocket and my thumb on the crease when I found the tear. The lining of that pocket had been torn for years. I had known it the way a person knows a stair that gives, without ever stopping on it. Now the paper went through the tear into the space between lining and cloth. My hand went after it. The hand came out with nothing. For the length of three breaths the folded paper was lost inside my mother's coat.

I stood still in the middle of the room with my arm in the coat up to the wrist, feeling along the lining the way I had felt along a page, and the feeling was the same feeling, the search for the thin place, and the thin place was where the paper had gone. I found it at the seam and worked it back through the tear and held it in my two hands, and it was uncreased no worse than it had been, and the three words were still there, and the bloom on the word *want* had not rubbed.

Edward was at the door with his coat on and his stick in his hand. He had seen the whole of it, the standing and the fishing and the two hands, and he did not look at the paper. He reads empties by not looking, and the folded half-sheet was the fullest empty thing I carried, and he gave it the mercy of his not-seeing.

"I will mend the pocket," I said.

"That coat has wanted a pocket for four years."

"I know how long it has wanted one."

"Then I said a thing you already knew," he said, "which is the commonest thing a man does in a house," and he opened the door and stood aside.

***

The carrier's boy was in the lane with the Collow load and a paper for me folded twice and sealed with a wafer.

It was from the clerk's grandson. It was four lines. The four lines said that the form had been returned to the cupboard unfinished, that his grandfather had written *not proceeded with* across the top of it in pencil and had not signed that either, and that the turn at Long Bank had been taken again for the year at Michaelmas by Mrs. Gow, who had said nothing at the vestry and had set the loaf on the sill on the first Sunday in March as her mother had and her mother before her.

I read it twice and folded it and put it in the other pocket, the one without a tear, and the two pockets of my mother's coat held the two papers, and one was a keeping and one was a confirmation, and neither of them was a thing the law could enter.

"He is well?" I said.

"He says his knee is bad and the weather is worse," the boy said, "and he says to tell you the cupboard is the same cupboard."

***

We walked the bounds of the north field, the way we did on the days he did not say where he went. The hedge was low and the ditch was full and the field beyond was the colour of a thing that has been cold a long time and is thinking about beginning. The wind came off the river and the clay stuck to our heels, and we walked without talking, and the not-talking was the company, and the company was the only thing either of us had ever been good at.

At the far stile he stopped. He took the notebook from his coat and opened it, not at the first page, at the last. The twenty-eighth entry was there, and beneath it the blank, and the blank was still blank, and he had not written in it, and the not-writing had held all winter and would hold a while longer.

"I will wait," he said, "until you ask a question I can answer."

"I have asked twenty-eight."

"Those were for her. The last is for you."

He said it without weight, and the lightness of it was what made it land. The last is for you. I stood at the stile with my hand on the cold wood and the folded paper in my mended pocket, and the paper was warm from my body, and the warmth was the only heat the words had ever had.

"Suppose I never ask," I said.

"Then I will have a blank page, and a blank page is not the worst thing a man can be left with. The worst thing is a page filled with an answer the other person did not mean."

"Suppose I ask, and the answer is nothing. Suppose I open my mouth and there is no word there, the way there was no word at the gate."

"Then you will have asked, and the asking is the thing I have been waiting for, not the answer. I have had answers all my life. Answers are cheap. A question from a person who has never been allowed to ask one is the rarest thing in the world, and I would rather have the question and no answer than the answer with no question."

I looked at him. He stood with the notebook open and the wind moving the pages a little, and his face was the face he had in the field, which was the face he had when he was not performing anything, and the not-performing was the only face I had ever trusted. He had been waiting all winter. He had been waiting longer than the winter. He had been waiting since before I met him. A woman with a cottage and a seal had told him her trouble and not her name, and he had decided from that that the asking was a cruelty. He had built his whole life around not inflicting it. The not-inflicting had been a kind of love and a kind of error, and I had begun to think the error was the larger part.

"You are wrong about the asking," I said. "You have been wrong about it all winter, and you were wrong before the winter, and you will go on being wrong until someone tells you."

He did not flinch. "Tell me."

"The asking is not the cruelty. The not-asking is. A person who is never asked learns to keep her wants where no one can see them, and the keeping is the small death, and the small death is what the law does to a woman it strikes. You thought you were sparing me the wound. You were giving me the law's own silence, and you were giving it kindly, and the kind giving is the one that takes longest to notice. I have been living inside your mercy all winter, and your mercy has been the same shape as the parish's, and the parish's mercy is a loaf on a sill and a woman who never gets asked what she wants."

He stood with the notebook open and did not speak for a long moment. The wind took a page and he put his hand on it and held it flat, and the holding was the only movement he made.

"You are right," he said at last. "I have been sparing you the wrong thing. I have been sparing you the asking and calling it kindness, and the kindness has been a wall with a door in it, and I have stood at the door and not knocked, and the not-knocking has been the whole of what I did wrong."

"Yes."

"Then ask me. Ask me now, and I will answer, and if the answer is nothing, you will have the nothing from me and not from the law."

I put my hand in my pocket and touched the folded paper. The crease was soft under my thumb. The want was inside, folded, unfinished, and the want had a living person on the other end of it, and the living person was standing at a stile with a notebook open and a page held flat against the wind. I had only to open my mouth. I had only to say the three words and the rest of them. The words were there. They had been there all winter, folded, and the folding had kept them, and the keeping had been the point.

I did not say them.

I took my hand out of my pocket and put it on the stile beside his hand, and the two hands were on the same cold wood, and the cold went through both of us at once.

"Not today," I said. "Today I have said the thing that is true, which is that you have been wrong, and the saying of it is the first thing I have asked of you in two lives. It is not the question. It is the asking. Let me have the asking a while before I have the question. I have never had an asking before. I would like to know what it is like to have one and not spend it all at once."

He looked at my hand on the stile and then at my face, and something in him eased, the way a knot eases when the strain comes off it. Then he turned his hand over and put it over mine, palm down, the way a man covers a page he does not want the wind to take, and the covering was not a claim on me. It was the first thing he had ever done with his hands that was not careful.

"All right," he said. "We will have the asking a while."

He closed the notebook, and this time he closed it all the way, and the closing was not a refusal. It was a putting-away of a thing that would be needed again. He put it in his coat and took up his stick, and we went over the stile, and the field beyond was the colour of a thing that is thinking about beginning.

***

We walked to the river and along it. The water was high and brown and going fast, and the willows were black, and the light stayed a little longer than it had the week before, and the staying was the only promise the season made.

At the bend where the river turns toward Tindle, Edward stopped and looked at the water. "She is still there," he said. "In the book. In the loaves. At the gate."

"She is still there."

"And you will go back."

"I will go back. Not this spring. But I will go back, and when I go back I will not go to ask for her. I will go to ask for myself, and the asking will be the first thing I have ever done that is entirely mine, and I will do it badly, and it will be enough."

He nodded. The water went by. The light lay on it in long flat pieces and broke and came together.

"There is a thing I have not told you," he said, "and I have not told you because it is not evidence and cannot be entered, and the book is made of entries." He watched the water. "When I read the rate-book that first evening, before I brought it to the table, I put my thumb to the fifteenth line myself. I did not tell you. It gave me a woman at a gate in the rain, and a hand on the rail, and nothing else, and I thought it was a thin read because the line was thin." He was quiet a moment. "It was not thin. It was the same read you had, and I did not know it, and I put the book down and said nothing, and the saying nothing was the first of the not-askings."

I had never asked him. That was the whole of it, and it took me a moment to see it clear. I had decided, early in the marriage, that a woman who reads the dead does not ask a man what the paper says to him, and I had kept the decision like a rule, and the keeping of it had cost me the one thing I had wanted all winter without knowing I wanted it, which was to learn whether he had seen her too. He had. He had seen her in the rain on the first night of the whole business, and I had spent four months alone with a woman at a gate because neither of us would say a thing we could both have said in a sentence.

"You have kept that since the night the book came."

"I have kept it since the night the book came."

"Why tell me now?"

"Because you have just told me what the not-asking costs," he said, "and a man who has been told the price does not go on buying the thing at that price and call himself prudent."

I put my hand in my pocket and held the folded paper through the cloth, and the paper was warm, and the warmth was mine, and the want was mine, and the not-yet was the only thing in the world that belonged to no one but me.

***

We turned for home, and the clay stuck to our heels, and the wind came off the river, and the gate at Collow was true in its frame.

That evening I mended the pocket.

I did it by the lamp with the paper on the table beside me, and the needle was one of my mother's, and the thread was grey and too thick for the cloth, and the stitches showed. I sewed the tear closed from the inside where it would not be seen. The sewing took half an hour. While I sewed I thought about the thing I had said at the stile and about the thing he had said at the river, and about the fact that I had spent the whole winter reading a dead woman's waiting and had not once sat down to mend my own coat.

The mending was a small betrayal of a thing I had believed in February. I had said then that a mended lining forgets it was ever torn, and the forgetting was what I had been trying to avoid. That was a young thing to have said. A torn lining that is not mended lets the paper fall into the space between cloth and cloth, and a paper in that space is a paper nobody can reach, and the unreachable is not the kept.

I put the paper into the mended pocket and my hand after it and let go, and the paper stayed where it was put, and the staying was the whole of what mending is for.

***

That night I put my hand flat on his chest in the dark, the way I had learned to do in the winter, and he covered it with his own and did not ask what it meant, and the not-asking was not his kind any more and not mine either. It was the ordinary kind. The kind two people arrive at after a while, when the hand on the chest has stopped being a question and has become a way of saying the day is over and we are both in it.

The word was getting heavier every day. Not the want. The word. The want had been folded and carried and had not changed in all that time, and it would keep. But the word for it was growing, in the dark, in the ordinary room, the way a thing grows when it is not looked at. I did not know yet what it was. I knew only that it had begun to have a shape, and the shape had weight, and the weight was the only weight I had ever been glad to carry.

In the morning the coat was on the hook by the door with the paper in the pocket. The fire had to be built. The bread had to be fetched. The day came up over Collow the way it had come up over every day of that winter, without asking anyone whether they were ready.

I was not ready.

I went out anyway.

---

## After the End · A Note for the Reader

Thank you for reading. A few questions, none of which require you to remember the numbers:

1. Where did you stop reading, even for a moment? What was happening on that page?
2. Was there a passage you had to reread to work out what it meant? Was the reread a pleasure or a tax?
3. Did the cold reads still work the eighth time, or had you learned to skip them?
4. How did you find the pace of the last five chapters?
5. Which character would you follow into the next book, and which would you be glad to leave behind?
6. If you described this book to a friend in one sentence, what would the sentence be?

Reply in any form – a list, a rant, notes read off the back of an envelope. All of it helps.
